Polynomial functors from free groups to a stable ∞ \infty -category
Gregory Arone

Abstract
. We study the category of polynomial functors from finitely generated free groups to a stable ∞ \infty -category D . We show that this category is equivalent to the category of excisive functors from pointed spaces to D , and also to truncated right comodules over the commutative operad with values in D . The latter formulation generalizes a result of Geoffrey Powell in characteristic zero. We use the equivalence of categories to calculate Ext \ext in the category of polynomial functors from free groups to abelian groups. We reproduce previous results of Christine Vespa and others, and do many new calculations. Using the work of Aurelien Djament, we give applications to stable cohomology of the group of automorphisms of free groups with coefficients in a polynomial functor.



1.
Introduction

Let
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
be the category of finitely generated free groups, and
Ab
the category of abelian groups. This paper was motivated by desire to calculate Ext groups in the category of functors
[{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ab}}]
. Our approach involves translating the algebraic problem about groups into a more homotopy-theoretical one involving spaces. It gradually transpired that the language of
\infty
-categories provides a convenient framework for the sort of manipulations we need to do. Moreover, it turns out that our general result on the functor category
[{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}}]
(where
Ch
denotes chain complexes) depends only on
Ch
being a stable
\infty
-category. Therefore, the first half of the paper is written in the language of
\infty
-categories, and theorems
A
and
B
are formulated for general stable
\infty
-categories rather than
Ch
or
Ab
.
The second half of the paper is devoted to calculations. Readers who are primarily interested in the calculations can skip to Section
1.1
, read from there to the end of the introduction, and then go straight to Part
II
, while treating Theorem
C
as a black box.

With that said, let
D
be a stable
\infty
-category, such as
Ch
or
Spectra
. In this paper we study functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
D
. To do this, we use the connection between the homotopy theories of groups and of spaces.
Let
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
be the
\infty
-category of pointed spaces. The classifying space functor
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}
induces a restriction on functor categories

\rho_{\operatorname{B}}\colon\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}}).

This functor is not an equivalence of categories, of course. But it restricts to an equivalence between categories of excisive and polynomial functors. Let
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
denote the category of
n
-excisive functors from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
to
D
. Let
\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})
denote the category of degree
n
polynomial functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
D
(defined in Section
5
). We prove the following result. In the paper it is subsumed in Theorem
8.1
.

Theorem A
.

Restriction along the classifying space functor induces, for each
n
, an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\rho_{\operatorname{B}}\colon\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}}).

The inverse to
\rho_{\operatorname{B}}
is given by a composition of two functors, both of which are equivalences:

\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{L}_{\operatorname{B}}}\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{R}}\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}}).

Here
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
is the category of
connected
pointed spaces,
\mathrm{L}_{\operatorname{B}}
is left Kan extension along
\operatorname{B}
, and
\mathrm{R}
is right Kan extension along the inclusion functor
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}\hookrightarrow{\textbf{S}}_{*}
.

Let us note that one can use Theorem
A
to further relate the category of polynomial functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
D
to categories of right (co)modules over the commutative or the Lie operad. Let
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
the category of pointed sets with at most
n
non-basepoint elements. There is an inclusion of categories
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}\hookrightarrow{\textbf{S}}_{*}
, which induces a restriction functor

\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}}).

By Lemma
8.2
, this functor restricts to an equivalence of categories, for each
n

\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}}).

Furthermore, let
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
be the category of unpointed sets with at most
n
elements, and surjective functions between them. By
[
21
,
37
]
there is an equivalence

\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})\simeq\fun({\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})

The category
\fun({\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})
is the same as the category of
n
-truncated right comodules in
D
over the (non-unital) commutative operad. Thus we also denote this category by
\mathtt{Com}\mathrm{-}{\textbf{comod}}_{\leq n}({\textbf{D}})
. Furthermore, there is an equivalence between the category of right comodules over
\mathtt{Com}
and
divided power right modules
over
\mathtt{Lie}
, where
\mathtt{Lie}
is the bar construction on
\mathtt{Com}
, also known as the shifted spectral Lie operad. A version of this equivalence is proved in
[
3
, Theorem 3.82]
, albeit with a somewhat ad hoc notion of divided power module. We denote the category of
n
-truncated divided power modules by
\operatorname{DP}\mathtt{Lie}\mathrm{-}{\textbf{mod}}_{\leq n}({\textbf{D}})
. Putting all these equivalences together, we obtain the following theorem

Theorem B
.

For all
n
, there are equivalences of
\infty
-categories

\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})\simeq\mathtt{Com}\mathrm{-}{\textbf{comod}}_{\leq n}({\textbf{D}})\simeq\operatorname{DP}\mathtt{Lie}\mathrm{-}{\textbf{mod}}_{\leq n}({\textbf{D}}).

The first of these equivalences is proved in Corollary
8.4
. We only mention the connection with divided power
\mathtt{Lie}
-modules in the introduction, for the sake of the broad picture. They are not needed for applications in this paper.

Remark 1.1
.

There is a forgetful functor from the category of divided power right modules over an operad to the category of ordinary right modules. When
{\textbf{D}}={\textbf{Ch}}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is the category of rational chain complexes, this forgetful functor is an equivalence. Therefore, theorem
B
gives equivalences of categories

(1)

\poly_{n}({{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq\mathtt{Com}\!-\!{\textbf{comod}}_{\leq n}({\textbf{Ch}}_{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq\mathtt{Lie}\mathrm{-mod}_{\leq n}({\textbf{Ch}}_{\mathbb{Q}}).

A very similar equivalence of categories (as well as a contravariant version of it) was proved by Geoffrey Powell
[
30
]
. We say very similar, because Powell actually proves an equivalence of categories of functors with values in
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces rather than functors with values in rational chain complexes. It would be good to understand precisely the relationship between (
1
) and the results of Powell in
[
30
]
. But putting this aside, Theorem
B
may be viewed as a generalisation of Powell’s result in the sense that it works not just in characteristic zero, but for functors with values in any stable
\infty
-category, e.g., spectra. Furthermore, Theorems
A
and
B
work for contravariant as well as covariant functors with values in, say,
Ch
or
Spectra
. In fact, one can write a version of Theorems
A
and
B
for bifunctors, i.e., functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\times{{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
to
D
. See Proposition
8.6
.

Idea of proof of the main theorems

We prove Theorem
A
by factoring the functor
\rho_{B}
as a composition of three functors, and showing that each one of them is an equivalence. The factorization takes the following form

(2)

\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\alpha}\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\beta}\exc_{n}({\textbf{sGr}},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\gamma}\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}}).

Explanation: Firstly,
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
is the category of
connected
pointed spaces, and
\alpha
is restriction along the inclusion
i\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}\hookrightarrow{\textbf{S}}_{*}
. We show that restriction along
i
induces an equivalence of categories of excisive functors. A version of this statement was proved by Brantner and Mathew
[
7
, Theorem 3.36]
. We give a different proof, which works without requiring that
D
is stable (but in this paper we only use the result for stable
D
). We also show that the inverse to
\alpha
is given by right Kan extension along
i
(Proposition
7.16
).

Secondly,
sGr
is the category of simplicial groups, and
\beta
is restriction along the classifying space functor
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{sGr}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
. That
\beta
is an equivalence follows essentially from Kan’s theorem that there is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories between
sGr
and
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}

[
24
]
.

Thirdly and finally,
\gamma
is induced by restriction along the inclusion
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\hookrightarrow{\textbf{sGr}}
. We show that
\gamma
is an equivalence (Proposition
6.8
). Its inverse is given by left Kan extension.

1.1.
Applications to Ext groups

Our main application of Theorem
A
is to calculation of Ext groups in the category of functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
Ab
. These Ext groups were studied by a number of people, perhaps most prominently by Christine Vespa
[
36
]
.

Let
Ch
be the
\infty
-category of chain complexes or, equivalently, of
H\mathbb{Z}
-modules. For the application we specialize Theorem
A
to the case
{\textbf{D}}={\textbf{Ch}}
. Functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
Ab
can be viewed as functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
Ch
, and Ext in the category
\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ab}})
can be interpreted as homotopy groups of mapping objects in
\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})
.

Let
\langle S^{1}\rangle\subset{\textbf{S}}_{*}
be the full subcategory of
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
(pointed spaces), spanned by finite wedges of
S^{1}
. Note that the fundamental group functor induces an equivalence of topological categories
\pi_{1}\colon\langle S^{1}\rangle\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
. We prove the following result (an abbreviated version of Theorem
8.7
)

Theorem C
.

Suppose
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
is a polynomial functor of degree
n
. Then there exists an
n
-excisive functor
\widehat{F}\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
, unique up to equivalence, for which there is an equivalence of functors from
\langle S^{1}\rangle
to
Ch

\widehat{F}|_{\langle S^{1}\rangle}\cong F\circ\pi_{1}.

Furthermore if
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
is another polynomial functor, then there is an isomorphism of graded groups

(3)

\ext^{*}_{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ab}})}(F,G)\cong\pi_{-*}\left(\spectralNat\left(\widehat{F},\widehat{G}\right)\right)

where by
\spectralNat(\widehat{F},\widehat{G})
we mean the spectral object of morphisms in the stable
\infty
-category
\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}})
.

Notation 1.2
.

Throughout the paper, given a polynomial functor
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
, we denote by
\widehat{F}
the corresponding functor from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
to
Ch
that is given by Theorem
C
.

We refer to
\widehat{F}
as the extension of
F
. But let us remind the reader that
\widehat{F}
is neither a left nor a right Kan extension. Rather
\widehat{F}
is a right Kan extension of a left Kan extension of
F
.

Remark 1.3
.

Theorem
C
is useful for calculations because the right hand side of (
3
) is often easier to compute than the left hand side. The reader may wonder why this should be the case. We can offer a couple of heuristic reasons:

First, we saw above that the right hand side of (
3
) can be identified with derived natural transformations between functors from
Epi
to
Ch
, while on the left hand side we have functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
Ch
(or
Ab
). It is clear that the category
Epi
has simpler structure than
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
, so it stands to reason that the right hand side of (
3
) is easier to calculate than the left hand side.

The second, and perhaps deeper reason hinges on equivalence
\alpha
on line (
2
), i.e., on extending the domain category from connected spaces to all spaces. Consider for example the functor
P^{n}(X)=\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{n}]
, which represents the homology of
X^{n}
. As a functor from connected spaces to
Ch
,
P^{n}
is not a representable functor, and there is no easy way to calculate
{\spectralNat}_{\,{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}\!\!}(P^{n},-)
. On the other hand, as a functor from all spaces to
Ch
,
P^{n}
is represented by the pointed set
n_{+}=\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, and for any functor
G\in\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}})
the Yoneda lemma gives us the following equivalence, which amounts to a calculation of
\spectralNat_{\,{\textbf{S}}_{*}\!\!}(P^{n},G)
:

\spectralNat_{\,{\textbf{S}}_{*}\!\!}(P^{n},G)\simeq G(n_{+}).

It turns out that for many polynomial functors
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
that do not admit small projective resolutions in
\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ab}})
, their image
\widehat{F}\in\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}})
does admit a convenient presentation as a homotopy colimit of representable functors. This seems to be the “little miracle” that makes Theorem
C
remarkably useful for calculations.

To illustrate the utility of Theorem
C
, we list in Table
1
some commonly used polynomial functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
Ab
, and their extensions in
\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}})
.

A functor
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}

The corresponding functor
\widehat{F}\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}

{\mathrm{ab}}

\widehat{\mathrm{ab}}(X)

=

\Sigma^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X]

\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}

\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)

=

\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}]

\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}

\widehat{\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)

=

\Sigma^{-n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]

\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}

\widehat{\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)

=

(\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}])_{\Sigma_{n}}

\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}

\widehat{\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)

=

\Sigma^{-2n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(SX)^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]

\mathrm{Pa}_{n}

\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}_{n}}(X)

=

P_{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega X]=\nat_{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}_{0}}(S^{i},\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge i}])

Table 1.
A table of functors

Let us give a few explanations of the notation in the table

•

\mathrm{ab}
denotes the abelianization functor,

•

\mathrm{T}^{n}
denotes
n
-fold tensor power,

•

\Lambda^{n}
,
\mathrm{S}^{n}
and
\Gamma^{n}
denote the
n
-th exterior, symmetric and divided power respectively,

•

\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[-]
denotes the functor from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
to
Ch
that represents reduced singular homology. Most of the time we work with
\infty
-categories, and it does not matter which model of this functor we use. For example, one can think of
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X]
as the Eilenberg-Mac Lane spectrum
H\mathbb{Z}\wedge X
, or the reduced singular chains complex of
X
, or the normalized chain complex of the pointed free abelian simplicial group generated by a pointed simplicial set
X
. Occasionally it will be convenient to use the latter construction as our specific model of
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X]
.

•

\mathrm{Pa}_{n}
denotes the
n
-th Passi functor (defined in Section
10
).

•

P_{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega X]
is short for Goodwillie’s
n
-th Taylor approximation of the functor
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega-]
, evaluated at
X
.

•

If
C
is a chain complex and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
then
\Sigma^{n}C
denotes the
n
-fold shift of
C
.

•

If
C
is a (non-negatively graded) chain complex, then
\Omega^{n}C
is defined as follows: starting with the standard simplicial model for
S^{1}
define a simplicial model for
S^{n}
. Use the simplicial structure on
S^{n}
to define
\Omega^{n}C
as a cosimplicial chain complex. Normalize to get a (second quadrant) double complex, and then take total complex.

There is an equivalence
\Omega^{n}C\simeq\Sigma^{-n}C
. We favor the notation
\Omega^{n}C
when we want to indicate that the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
is acting non-trivially on the desuspension coordinates.

•

X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
denotes the strict orbit space of the action of
\Sigma_{n}
on
X^{\wedge n}
.

•

The notation
(\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}])_{\Sigma_{n}}
indicates the strict coinvariants of the action of
\Sigma_{n}
on the chain complex
\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}]
. More details about it are given in Section
9.1
.

As we said in Remark
1.3
, most of the functors in the right column of Table
1
admit explicit small resolutions in terms of representable functors, while the functors in the left column do not seem to admit such resolutions. Because of this, natural transformations are easier to calculate between functors in the right column than in the left column.

The following result, originally due to C. Vespa
[
36
]
, can be derived fairly easily from Theorem
C
and Table
1
, plus the Yoneda lemma. Statement (1) of the corollary is proved in the paper as Corollary
11.4
1
. Statements (2)-(4) are Proposition
12.2
.

Corollary 1.4

(
[
36
]
)
.

There are isomorphisms of graded groups

(1)

\ext^{i}(T^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},T^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{Z}^{\sur(n,m)}&i=n-m\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(2)

\ext^{i}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{Q}^{\Part(n,m)}&i=n-m\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(3)

\ext^{i}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{Q}&m=n\leq 1,\,i=0\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(4)

\ext^{i}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{Q}^{\Stirling(n,m)}&i=n-m\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Here
\sur(n,m)
denotes the set of surjections from a set with
n
elements to a set with
m
elements,
\Stirling(n,m)=\sur(n,m)/_{\Sigma_{m}}
is the set (or number) of ways to partition a
set
with
n
elements into
m
parts, and
\Part(n,m)={{}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\!\backslash\sur(n,m)/_{\Sigma_{m}}
can be identified with the set of partitions of the
number

n
with
m
parts.

We can use Theorem
C
together with Table
1
to do many new calculations. As a general remark, modulo torsion many of calculations in this paper were done before, by Vespa and others. But all the calculations of torsion in this paper are new.

In Section
11
we calculate
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ{\textbf{Ab}},G)
for several functors
G
. The main observation here is that
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ{\textbf{Ab}},G)
is given by the cross-effects of
\widehat{G}
, and the cross-effects are usually easy to compute. The cases when
G=\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab},
or
\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
are calculated easily with our methods. The case when
G=\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
is less easy, because our formula for
\widehat{\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}
is not so explicit. We calculate
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
for
n\leq 9
(see Table
2
), and give a kind of general formula for
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
in terms of
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
. One of the reasons this calculation is interesting to the author is that it is our first example in which Ext groups have lots of torsion (and are trivial rationally).
1
1

1

Added in revision: further progress on
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
has been made in
[
25
]
and also in work in progress by M. Kim, C. Vespa and the author.

In Section
11
we also calculate
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{Pa}_{n})
. This reduces to calculating the homology of the “reduced Ran space” of
S^{1}
. As far as we know, this calculation is new even rationally.

In Section
12
we study
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
for various
G
. The key to calculating these groups is to understand
\nat(X^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}},\widehat{G})
. We accomplish this by writing a kind of “small projective resolution” of
X^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}}
.

To illustrate, let us present a full calculation of
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
. First, let us give a more general result. Suppose
G\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
is a functor. Define
\ce_{2}G(S^{0},S^{0})
to be the second cross-effects of
G
, i.e., the kernel of the homomorphism
G(S^{0}\vee S^{0})\to G(S^{0})\times G(S^{0})
. Note that there is a natural homomorphism
\ce_{2}G(S^{0},S^{0})\to G(S^{0})
. We prove the following fact in Examples
12.11

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
be a polynomial functor. Let
\widehat{G}\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
be the extension of
G
, in the sence of Theorem
C
. The graded group
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
is isomorphic to
\pi_{-*-2}
of the homotopy pullback of the following diagram of chain complexes
2
2

2

We use
\pi_{*}
synonymously with
\operatorname{H}_{*}
when applied to chain complexes

\widehat{G}(S^{0})\to\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+},\widehat{G}(S^{0})))\leftarrow\ce_{2}\widehat{G}(S^{0},S^{0})^{h\Sigma_{2}}.

The proposition is proved by using an explicit resolution of the functor
X^{\wedge 2}_{\Sigma_{2}}
.
As a corollary, we can calculate the groups
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
for all
n
and
*
. The following lemma is Lemma
12.12
in the paper.

Lemma 1.6
.

There is n isomorphism:

\ext^{i}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{Z}^{\Part(n,2)}&i=n-2\\
\mathbb{Z}/2&n<i,n\mbox{ is odd, }i\mbox{ is even.}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Rationally this result agrees with Corollary
1.4
(2), but it shows that when
n
is odd,
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
has
2
-torsion. It is worth mentioning that the torsion comes from the cohomology of
\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}
.

We also describe
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
in Corollary
12.14
. Unsurprisingly, in this case the answer has both
2
and
3
-torsion. Just as the
2
-torsion part is captured by the cohomology of
\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}
, the
3
-torsion part is encoded in
\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{*}(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}/\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{2})
. This is the classifying space of the collection of non-transitive subgroups of
\Sigma_{3}
.

In Theorem
12.10
we give a general formula for
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
as a homotopy limit of an explicit diagram of chain complexes. This gives a spectral sequence for calculating
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
, whose
E^{1}
and
E^{2}
pages are accessible. We speculate that the spectral sequence collapses at
E^{2}
in many cases of interest, for example when
G=\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
.

In Section
13
we investigate
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},G)
. We show that it can be calculated in terms of the rank filtration of
\widehat{G}
. We apply the general formula to study
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{m},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
and obtain a refinement of a result of Vespa
[
36
, Proposition 5]
.

Applications to stable cohomology of
\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)

Let
\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)
be the group of automorphisms of the free group on
n
generators. Suppose
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
is a (polynomial) functor. Then for each
n
,
G(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)
is a module over
\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)
. By taking a suitable limit as
n\to\infty
of
\operatorname{H}^{*}(\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle);G(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle))
one may define the stable cohomology of
\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)
with coefficients in
G
. We denote the stable cohomology by
\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};G)
. By remarkable work of Djament
[
12
]
(which in turn relies on a famous theorem of Galatius
[
16
]
), it is possible to calculate
\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};G)
in terms of
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
. In Theorem
14.5
we give the following reinterpretation of Djament’s result:

Theorem D
.

Let
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
be a polynomial functor of degree
n
. Let
\widehat{G}\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
be the extension of
G
given by Theorem
C
. There is an isomorphism

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};G)\cong\pi_{-*}\pointedmaps\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}_{+},\prod_{d=0}^{n}\ce_{d}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})^{h\Sigma_{d}}\right).

Here
\ce_{d}\widehat{G}
denotes the
d
-th cross-effect of
\widehat{G}
. With help of Theorem
D
we calculate
\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
,
\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
and
\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut},\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
in terms of cohomology of symmetric groups. See Proposition
14.6
. This proposition refines rational results given in
[
36
, Theorem 4]
.

Organization of the paper

In Section
2
we go over some basics regarding
\infty
-categories. In particular, we review how Ext in the category of functors from a small category
I
to
Ab
can be viewed as homotopy groups of spectral mapping objects in the
\infty
-category
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{Ch}})
.

In Section
3
we review the connection between the categories
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
and
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
, via
sGr
. We prove that the category of functors
\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})
is equivalent to the category of sifted colimits preserving functors
\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})
.

In Section
4
we review, in preparation for discussion of polynomial and excisive functors, some notions surrounding cubical diagrams, such as cocartesian and strongly cocartesian diagrams. This is elementary material, but we need to pin down some details of the relation between
\infty
-categorical and
1
-categorical versions of these notions. We check that in some cases cocartesianness in an
\infty
-category can be detected in the homotopy category.

In Section
5
we review the notion of polynomial functors from a pointed category with coproducts to an idempotent complete additive
\infty
-category
3
3

3

Polynomial functors in this setting were considered by Johnson and McCarthy. See for example
[
23
]
. We give a characterization of polynomial functors that is analogous to Goodwillie’s definition of excisive functors.

In Section
6
we review Goodwillie’s notion of excisive functors. We then focus on excisive functors from
sGr
or, equivalently,
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
, and compare them with polynomial functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
or, equivalently,
\langle S^{1}\rangle
. We prove our first substantial result: the classifying space functor
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
induces an equivalence

\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})

from a category of
excisive
functors to a category of
polynomial
functors.

There is similarity and some overlap between our Sections
5
and
6
and
[
5
, Section 2]
. However, we don’t see that we could just quote the paper of Barwick-Glasman-Mathew-Nikolaus, because they only deal with functors whose domain is a stable
\infty
-category, while we are interested in functors whose domain category is not stable.

In Section
7
we compare excisive functors on connected spaces with excisive functors on all spaces. We show that the inclusion
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}\hookrightarrow{\textbf{S}}_{*}
induces an equivalence of categories

\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})

and an inverse is given by right Kan extension. A version of this statment was proved by Brantner-Mathew
[
7
, Theorem 3.36]
. We give a different proof, which works also when
D
is an unstable
\infty
-category.

In Section
8
we prove Theorems
A
,
B
and
C
. We also prove a version for bifunctors.

Section
8
finishes the first part of the paper which is devoted to proving the general results. The second part is devoted to calculations.

In Section
9
we calculate
\widehat{F}
for most functors in the left column of Table
1
. In some cases the easiest way to find
\widehat{F}
is to “guess” the answer and check that it satisfies the condition of Theorem
C
. In this way we calculate
\widehat{F}
for
F=\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab},
and
\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
. We also give a kind of general formula for
\widehat{F}
when
F
factors through abelianization. We use this to describe
\widehat{\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ{\textbf{Ab}}}
. The general formula is of less topological nature and seems less easy to use that the special formulas we have for
\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
etc., but it is perhaps not entirely useless.

In section
10
we describe
\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}_{n}}
, i.e., the extensions of Passi functors. The Passi functors are our main example of functors
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
that do not factor through abelianization.

In Sections
11
,
12
and
13
we calculate
\ext
between a few of the functors in Table
1
, reproducing and extending all previous calculations of this kind that we are aware of.

Finally in Section
14
we combine our results with those of Djament to describe the stable cohomology of
\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)
with coefficients in a polynomial functor.

Part I
Theory

2.
Preliminaries and conventions regarding
\infty
-categories

In this section we review some preliminaries regarding
\infty
-categories. The main point of the section is to recall how classical Ext groups can be interpreted as homotopy (or homology) groups of spectral (or differential graded) mapping objects in a stable
\infty
-category.

Throughout the first half of the paper we use
\infty
-categorical language. Thus terms such as “category”, “limit” and “colimit” are usually meant in
\infty
-categorical sense. Occasionally we may fall back into
1
-categorical usage of these terms, more so in the second part of the paper than the first. We will try our best to be clear as to which meaning is intended! For now, until further notice, terms are used in the
\infty
-categorical sense.

Given objects
X
,
Y
of an
\infty
-category
C
we denote the space of maps from
X
to
Y
by
\map_{{\textbf{C}}}(X,Y)
, or simply
\map(X,Y)
. This is a well-defined homotopy type. If
C
is a pointed
\infty
-category, then
\map_{{\textbf{C}}}(X,Y)
is a well-defined pointed homotopy type. For some categories we use a special notation for the space of maps. If
X
and
Y
are pointed spaces we sometimes use the notation
\pointedmaps(X,Y)
to emphasise that we are considering the space of pointed maps.

\infty
-categories that have limits (resp. colimits) are cotensored (resp. tensored) over the category of spaces
S
. In a similar fashion, pointed
\infty
-categories with limits/colimits are cotensored/tensored over the category of pointed spaces
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
. In practice all
\infty
-categories that we will encounter will be pointed. Given an object
X
in a pointed
\infty
-category
C
and a pointed space
K
we denote the tensoring and cotensoring of
K
and
X
by
K\otimes X
(or
X\otimes K
), and
\pointedmaps(K,X)
respectively.

Given
\infty
-categories
C
and
D
, let
\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
denote the
\infty
-category of functors from
C
to
D
. If
F,G\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
are objects of
\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
we may denote the space
\map_{\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})}(F,G)
by
\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,G)
, or
\nat_{X}(F(X),G(X))
, or just
\nat(F,G)
.

If
C
is a stable
\infty
-category then the mapping space
\map_{{\textbf{C}}}(X,Y)
is naturally an infinite loop space. The
n
-fold delooping of
\map_{{\textbf{C}}}(X,Y)
is equivalent to
\map_{{\textbf{C}}}(X,\Sigma^{n}Y)
. Note that in
[
28
]
Lurie sometimes denotes
\pi_{0}\left(\map_{{\textbf{C}}}(X,\Sigma^{n}Y)\right)
by
\ext^{n}_{{\textbf{C}}}(X,Y)
. As we shall recall shortly (Lemma
2.3
below), when
C
is the category of chain complexes, this recovers the classic Ext groups.

The infinite delooping of
\map_{{\textbf{C}}}(X,Y)
is called the
mapping spectrum
from
X
to
Y
. We will denote the mapping spectrum by
\spectralmaps\hphantom{.}_{\!\!\!{\textbf{C}}}(X,Y)
or just
\spectralmaps(X,Y)
. When
C
is stable,
\spectralmaps(X,Y)
is a well-defined stable homotopy type, and there is a natural equivalence
\map(X,Y)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\spectralmaps(X,Y)
.

In particular if
C
is a small
\infty
-category and
D
is a stable
\infty
-category then
\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
is a stable
\infty
-category. Given two objects
F,G\in\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
we denote the spectral mapping object from
F
to
G
by
\spectralNat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,G)
, or
\spectralNat_{X}(F(X),G(X))
, or just
\spectralNat(F,G)
.

Given a functor
\alpha\colon{\textbf{C}}_{1}\to{\textbf{C}}_{2}
, we denote the corresponding restriction functor
\fun({\textbf{C}}_{2},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{C}}_{1},{\textbf{D}})
by
\rho_{\alpha}
, or just
\rho
. The left and right adjoints to restriction, a.k.a the left and right Kan extensions from
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
to
{\textbf{C}}_{2}
are denoted by
\mathrm{L}_{\alpha}
and
\mathrm{R}_{\alpha}
, or just
\mathrm{L}
and
\mathrm{R}
.

We will consider ordinary categories as
\infty
-categories via the nerve construction. More generally we will implicitly convert simplicially or topologically enriched
1
-categories into
\infty
-categories via the simplicial or topological nerve construction
[
27
, Definition 1.1.5.5]
. Usually we will not distinguish notationally between a category
C
and its (simplicial) nerve, and denote both by
C
.

Aside from simplicial and topological categories, there is a well-known way to associate an
\infty
-category with a Quillen model category, which we will review now.

The underlying
\infty
-category of a model category

Suppose
C
is a Quillen model category. Following Lurie
[
27
]
, we denote by
{\textbf{C}}^{\circ}
the full subcategory of
C
consisting of fibrant-cofibrant objects, or any full subcategory thereof that contains at least one representative of each homotopy equivalence class. If
C
is a combinatorial simplical model category, then
C
is considered as an
\infty
-category via the simplicial nerve of
{\textbf{C}}^{\circ}
. The
\infty
-category associated with
C
is denoted by
\Nerve({\textbf{C}}^{\circ})
in
[
27
, Appendix A.2]
. We will generally not distinguish notationally between a model category
C
and the
\infty
-category
\Nerve({\textbf{C}}^{\circ})
, and denote both simply by
C
, except in this section, where we have to explain precisely which construction we are using.

When
C
is a simplicial model category, the
\infty
-category
\Nerve({\textbf{C}}^{\circ})
is the localisation (in the sense of
[
28
, Definition 1.3.4.1]
) of
C
at the class of weak equivalences
[
28
, Theorem 1.3.4.20 + Remark 1.3.4.16]
. This is the justification for calling
\Nerve({\textbf{C}}^{\circ})
“the underlying
\infty
-category of
C
”. Furthermore, the construction of underlying
\infty
-category can be extended to more general model categories. Given a category
C
with a class of weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
, one can form the Dwyer-Kan localisation (a.k.a the hammock localisation) of
C
at
\mathcal{W}
. This is a simplicial category, which we denote by
L^{H}({\textbf{C}})
. Using the simplicial nerve construction, we may also consider
L^{H}({\textbf{C}})
as an
\infty
-category. When
C
is a combinatorial simplicial model category, there is a natural equivalence of
\infty
-categories

(4)

\Nerve({\textbf{C}}^{\circ})\xrightarrow{\simeq}L^{H}({\textbf{C}}).

This is part of what is proved in
[
22
, Section 1.4.3]
. The category
L^{H}({\textbf{C}})
is well-defined for an arbitrary model category
C
. Thus, one may define
L^{H}({\textbf{C}})
to be the
\infty
-category associated with a general model category
C
. Let us repeat that in the rest of the paper we will denote
L^{H}({\textbf{C}})
simply by
C
, but not in this section.

It is known that a Quillen adjunction between model categories induces an adjunction of the associated
\infty
-categories
[
22
, Proposition 1.5.1]
. It follows that a Quillen equivalence induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categories. It also is known that at least when
C
is combinatorial, the construction
{\textbf{C}}\mapsto L^{H}({\textbf{C}})
takes homotopy (co)limits in
C
to
\infty
-categorical (co)limits in
L^{H}({\textbf{C}})

[
22
, Corollary 1.5.2]
.

Remark 2.1
.

Recall that an
\infty
-category
C
is
stable
if it has finite limits and colimits, and a square diagram in
C
is a pushout if and only if it is a pullback
[
28
, Proposition 1.1.3.4]
. Similarly, a model category is stable if it has finite homotopy limits and colimits, and a square diagram in it is a homotopy pushout if and only if it is a homotopy pullback. It follows that the
\infty
-category associated with a combinatorial stable model category is stable.

Furthermore, Hinich proves that holds when
C
is a model category with a weaker notion of simplicial enrichment than simplicial model category. The definition of a weak simplicial enrichment is given in
[
22
, Definition 1.4.2]
. What matters to us is that the category of unbounded chain complexes
Ch
with the projective module structure satisfies it.

Let
A
be an abelian
1
-category and
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
the category of chain complexes in
A
. Recall that the projective model structure on
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
is the one where weak equivalences are quasi-isomorphisms and fibrations are level-wise surjections. If
A
has a set of compact projective generators, then the projective model structure on
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
exists, and is cofibrantly generated, and therefore clearly combinatorial. For a proof of this see for example
[
9
]
. Given the projective model structure, we may convert
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
into an
\infty
-category by the same. The
\infty
-category
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
is stable, and it has a well-known
t
-structure, whose heart
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}^{\heartsuit}
is the full subcategory consisting of chain complexes whose homology is concentrated in degree zero. It is well known that there is an equivalence of
\infty
categories
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}^{\heartsuit}\simeq{\textbf{A}}
, and thus
A
embeds fully faithfully in
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
as the heart.

The category
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
is enriched over
Ch
(in the
1
-categorical sense), and one can convert the enrichment over
Ch
into enrichment over simplicial sets by first taking
-1
-connected cover and then applying the Dold-Kan functor. See
[
28
, Page 79]
for more details on this. The simplicial enrichment of
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
does not make
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
into a simplicial model category, because
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
is not tensored over simplicial sets, but only “weakly” tensored in a certain sense. But the simplicial enrichment of
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
does satisfy Hinich’s axioms of weak enrichment. See the first Example on page 37 of
[
22
]
. Hinich only states it for chain complexes of modules over a ring, but it is clear that it holds for chain complexes over a general abelian category with enough projectives. As a result, we obtain the following lemma

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
A
be an abelian category with enough projectives, and let
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
be the category of chain complexes over
A
equipped with the projective model structure. Then the equivalence of (
4
) holds for
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
. I.e., there is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\Nerve({\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}^{\circ})\xrightarrow{\simeq}L^{H}({\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}).

It follows that for two chain complexes
P
and
Q
, the space of maps from
P
to
Q
in the
\infty
-category of chain complexes is naturally equivalent to the simplicial mapping space from a projective cofibrant replacement of
P
to
Q
. As a result, we obtain the well-known statement

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
X,Y
be objects of
A
, which we can also consider as objects of
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
via the natural embedding of
A
into
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
. There is a natural isomorphism.

(5)

\ext^{*}_{{\textbf{A}}}(X,Y)\cong\pi_{-*}\left(\spectralmaps\hphantom{.}_{\!\!\!{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}}(X,Y)\right).

Proof.

The isomorphism is obtained from the following chain of isomorphisms (
\operatorname{q}X
denotes a cofibrant replacement of
X
, i.e., a projective resolution of
X
)

\ext^{n}_{{\textbf{A}}}(X,Y)=[\operatorname{q}X,\Sigma^{n}Y]=\pi_{0}\map_{{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}}(X,\Sigma^{n}Y)\cong\pi_{-n}\left(\spectralmaps_{{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}}\!\!(X,Y)\right).

∎

2.1.
Homological and homotopical algebra of functor categories

We continue with the convention that
A
is an abelian
1
-category with enough projectives, and
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
denotes the category of chain complexes over
A
.

Now suppose
I
is a small
1
-category. Let
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{A}})
denote the
1
-category of functors. Then
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{A}})
is again an abelian category with enough projectives. If
A
has a set of compact projective generators, then so does
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{A}})
, and it follows that both categories
{\textbf{Ch}}_{\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{A}})}
and
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})
have a projective model structure, which is combinatorial. There is an obvious isomorphism of
1
-categories

(6)

{\textbf{Ch}}_{\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{A}})}\cong\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}).

Furthermore, this isomorphism induces a bijection between the projective model structures on both sides. Indeed, it is obvious that the weak equivalences and fibrations on both sides correspond to each other, and therefore the cofibrations correspond to each other as well. Subsequently, we will consider
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})
as a stable
\infty
-category. Right now, let us denote the
\infty
-category associated with
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})
by
\Nerve(\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})^{\circ})
.

Suppose
C
is a simplicial model category. There is a canonical functor

(7)

\Nerve(\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{C}})^{\circ})\to\fun({\textbf{I}},\Nerve({\textbf{C}}^{\circ}))

At least if
C
is a combinatorial simplicial model category, this functor is an equivalence by
[
27
, Proposition 4.2.4.4]
. On the other hand, by the results of
[
34
]
or
[
13
]
, every combinatorial model category
C
is Quillen equivalent to a combinatorial simplicial model category. Suppose there is a Quillen equivalence between
C
and a combinatorial simplicial category
{\textbf{C}}^{s}
. Then it induces a Quillen equivalence between
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{C}})
and
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{C}}^{s})
. These Quillen equivalences, together with (
7
) induce a chain of equivalences of
\infty
-categories

(8)

\Nerve(\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{C}})^{\circ})\simeq\Nerve(\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{C}}^{s})^{\circ})\simeq\fun({\textbf{I}},\Nerve({{\textbf{C}}^{s}}^{\circ}))\simeq\fun({\textbf{I}},\Nerve({\textbf{C}}^{\circ}))

In particular equivalences (
8
) hold when
{\textbf{C}}={\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
. Let us write it out explicitly: there is an equivalence of categories

(9)

\Nerve(\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})^{\circ})\simeq\fun({\textbf{I}},\Nerve({\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}^{\circ}))

In other words, the projective model structure on
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})
is a presentation of the
\infty
-category of functors
\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})
.

To summarize, we have the following well-known statement, which is however crucial to the whole paper.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
I
be a small
1
-category, and
A
an abelian
1
-category with a set of projective generators. Let
F,G\colon{\textbf{I}}\to{\textbf{A}}
be functors. We have an isomorphism

(10)

\ext^{*}_{\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{A}})}(F,G)\cong\pi_{-*}\left(\spectralNat(F,G)\right).

Proof.

The result follows by combining Lemma
2.3
and equivalence (
9
)
∎

2.2.
Sifted colimits

Sifted categories are defined in
[
27
, Definition 5.5.8.1]
. A sifted colimit in a category
C
is a colimit of a functor from a sifted category to
C
. We do not need to repeat the definition of a sifted category, but for example filtered colimits are sifted (
[
27
, Proposition 5.3.1.22]
), and simplicial colimits (also known as “geometric realisations”) are sifted
[
27
, Lemma 5.5.8.4]
. Furthermore, these examples generate all sifted colimits in a sense that can be made precise as follows: suppose
{\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}}
are categories that have small colimits. Then a functor
f\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
preserves sifted colimits if and only if it preserves filtered colimits and simplicial colimits
[
27
, Corollary 5.5.8.17]
. We let
\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})\subset\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
denote the full subcategory consisting of functors that preserve sifted colimits. We say more about sifted colimits in Section
3.2.1
, and for more details see
[
27
, Section 5.5.8]
.

3.
From free groups to connected spaces

Throughout this section, let
D
be a fixed
\infty
-category, that has all small colimits. Recall that
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
is the category of finitely generated free groups. Let
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
denote the category of
connected
pointed spaces. In this section we compare the functor categories
\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})
and
\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})
. The main result of this section is Theorem
3.1
below.

Let
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
be the classifying space functor. It is well-defined up to natural equivalence, and we may consider it a functor between
\infty
-categories. It induces a restriction functor

\rho_{\operatorname{B}}\colon\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}}).

Let
\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\subset\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})
be the full subcategory spanned by functors that preserve sifted colimits (Section
2.2
). The following is the main result of this section

Theorem 3.1
.

Composition with the classifying space functor induces an equivalence of categories

\rho_{B}\colon\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}}).

This theorem is essentially well-known. For example, it follows more or less immediately from
[
28
, Corollary 5.2.6.21]
. We will give the proof both for the sake of being self-contained, and because some of the details that come up in the process of proving Theorem
3.1
will be used elsewhere in the paper. The theorem is proved at the end of the section. First we have to do some preparatory work.

3.1.
From free groups to wedges of circles

Let
\langle S^{1}\rangle\subset{\textbf{S}}_{*}
be a full subcategory of pointed spaces spanned by finite wedge sums of
S^{1}
. We may consider
\langle S^{1}\rangle
as being spanned by objects of the form
\operatorname{B}\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle
, where
\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle
is the free group on
n
generators. It follows that the classifying space functor may be considered as a functor from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
\langle S^{1}\rangle
. Indeed, the classifying space functor induces an equivalence of categories:

(11)

\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\langle S^{1}\rangle.

Here when we say that a functor is an equivalence of categories, we mean it in the sense of
[
27
, Definition 1.1.3.6]
: it is essentially surjective on objects, and for any two objects
G_{1},G_{2}
of
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
,
\operatorname{B}
induces an equivalence of spaces (where the source has the discrete topology)

\hom(G_{1},G_{2})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}G_{1},\operatorname{B}G_{2}).

An explicit inverse to equivalence (
11
) is given by the fundamental group functor

\pi_{1}\colon\langle S^{1}\rangle\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}.

It follows that the fundamental group functor and the classifying space functor induce inverse equivalences of functor categories (
[
27
, Proposition 1.2.7.3 (3)]
)

(12)

\rho_{\pi_{1}}:\hbox to176.02pt{\vbox to18.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 88.00812pt\lower-9.15474pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
