3.2.
From wedges of circles to connected spaces

We claim that the category
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
is obtained from
\langle S^{1}\rangle
by
freely adjoining sifted colimits
. To make this precise, we have to digress to recall Lurie’s construction of a free completion of a category under sifted colimits.

3.2.1.
Sifted completion

Let
C
be a category that admits finite coproducts. Let
\mathcal{P}({\textbf{C}})=\fun({\textbf{C}}^{{\mathrm{op}}},{\textbf{S}}_{*})
be the category of presheaves on
C
. The category
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}})
is defined to be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{P}({\textbf{C}})
consisting of presheaves that take finite coproducts to finite products
[
27
, Definition 5.5.8.8]
. Proposition 5.5.8.10 in
[
27
]
implies, among other things, that
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}})
has all small colimits, and the inclusion
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}})\to\mathcal{P}({\textbf{C}})
preserves sifted colimits. It is clear that
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}})
contains the essential image of
C
in
\mathcal{P}({\textbf{C}})
under the Yoneda embedding. Lemma 5.5.8.14 of
[
27
]
says that
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}})
is (equivalent to) the closure of
C
in
\mathcal{P}({\textbf{C}})
spanned by sifted (i.e. filtered and simplicial) colimits. Moreover,
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}})
is the category obtained by
freely
adjoining sifted colimits to
C
. This is made precise in the following lemma. Recall that
\fun_{\Sigma}(-,-)
denotes the category of functors that preserve sifted colimits.

Lemma 3.2

(
[
27
]
, Proposition 5.5.8.15)
.

Let
C
be a small
\infty
-category which admits finite
coproducts and let
D
be an
\infty
-category which admits sifted colimits. The Yoneda embedding
C\hookrightarrow\mathcal{P}({\textbf{C}})
factors through a fully faithful embedding
j\colon{\textbf{C}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}})
. Restriction along
j
induces an
equivalence of categories

\fun_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}}),{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}}).

The inverse of this equivalence is given by left Kan extension.

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
C
be a small
\infty
-category which admits finite
coproducts and
D
an
\infty
-category which admits sifted colimits. Any functor
f\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
factors essentially uniquely as a composition

{\textbf{C}}\xrightarrow{j}\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}})\xrightarrow{F}{\textbf{D}},

where
j
is as defined above, and
F
preserves sifted colimits. Furthermore,
F
is a left Kan extension of
f
.

Furthermore
[
27
, Proposition 5.5.8.22]
gives necessary and sufficient conditions for the functor
F
above to be an equivalence of categories

Lemma 3.4

(
[
27
]
, Proposition 5.5.8.22)
.

Let
C
and
D
be as in Corollary
3.3
, and suppose we have a functor
f\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
. The induced functor
F\colon\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}})\to{\textbf{D}}
is an equivalence if the following conditions hold:

(1)

The functor
f
is fully faithful.

(2)

For every object
X
of
C
the represented functor

\map_{{\textbf{D}}}(f(X),-)\colon{\textbf{D}}\to{\textbf{Spaces}}

preserves sifted colimits.

(3)

The category
D
is generated by the essential image of
f
under sifted colimits.

Back to topological spaces

It follows from Corollary
3.3
that the inclusion
\langle S^{1}\rangle\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
factors essentially uniquely as a composition

(13)

\langle S^{1}\rangle\to\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\langle S^{1}\rangle)\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}

where the first functor is the Yoneda embedding, and the second functor preserves sifted colimits. Next, we claim that the second functor is in fact an equivalence

Lemma 3.5
.

The functor
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\langle S^{1}\rangle)\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
in (
13
) is an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

The lemma is essentially given by
[
28
, Corollary 5.2.6.21]
, which asserts that
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\langle S^{1}\rangle)
and
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
are equivalent categories. It can also be deduced directly from Lemma
3.4
. Indeed, this lemma gives three conditions that one needs to verify. Condition (
1
) is obvious. Condition (
2
) says that if
X
is a finite wedge of circles, then the represented functor
\pointedmaps(X,-)
preserves filtered colimits and geometric realisations of simplicial objects in
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
. It preserves filtered colimits because
X
is compact. That it preserves geometric realisations follows from the well-known fact that the loop space functor
\Omega
preserves geometric realisations of simplicial objects in
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
. This is true because
\Omega
is an inverse to the classifying space functor, which has a simplicial model that clearly preserves geometric realisations.

Finally condition (
3
) says that every object of
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
is equivalent to a sifted colimit of objects of
\langle S^{1}\rangle
. This follows (for example) from the well-known fact that for every connected pointed space
X
,
\Omega X
is weakly equivalent to the geometric realisation of a simplicial group that is free in each degree (this goes back to Kan
[
24
]
). Thus
X
is equivalent to the geometric realisation of a simplicial space that is equivalent to a wedge of circles in each degree.
∎

Since
\langle S^{1}\rangle
is a full subcategory of
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}

\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun(\langle S^{1}\rangle,{\textbf{D}})

has a fully faithful left adjoint (also known as the left Kan extension). We can now describe the image of the left adjoint. It consists of functors that preserve sifted colimits. This is stated formally in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
D
be a category that admits small colimits. The inclusion
\langle S^{1}\rangle\subset{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
induces an equivalence of categories

\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\fun(\langle S^{1}\rangle,{\textbf{D}}).

The inverse is given by left Kan extension, which we denote by

\mathrm{L}\colon\fun(\langle S^{1}\rangle,{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}}).

Proof.

We saw in (
13
) that the inclusion
\langle S^{1}\rangle\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
factors essentially uniquely as a composition

\langle S^{1}\rangle\to\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\langle S^{1}\rangle)\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}.

These induce restriction functors

\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\langle S^{1}\rangle),{\textbf{D}})\to\fun(\langle S^{1}\rangle,{\textbf{D}}).

The first of these restrictions is an equivalence by Lemma
3.5
and the second by Lemma
3.2
.
∎

3.3.
Connection with simplicial groups

It is well-known that the
1
-category of simplicial groups is equipped with a projective model structure, where fibrations and weak equivalences are those of the underlying simplicial sets
[
31
]
,
[
17
, Chapter II, Example 5.2]
. We denote by
sGr
the
\infty
-category associated with this model structure.

There is a fully faithful embedding
\mathrm{c}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\hookrightarrow{\textbf{sGr}}
that sends a group to the associated constant simplicial group. The classifying space functor
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to\langle S^{1}\rangle
extends to a functor, which we denote by the same letter
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{sGr}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
. The latter functor is defined by taking levelwise classifying space of a simplicial group and then taking geometric realisation. On the level of model categories it preserves weak equivalences, and therefore it induces a functor between the associated
\infty
-categories. We obtain a diagram of categories that commutes up to a natural equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle S^{1}\rangle}
sGr
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{B}}

We already saw that because of Corollary
3.3
the inclusion
\langle S^{1}\rangle\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
factors essentially uniquely as a composition
\langle S^{1}\rangle\to\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\langle S^{1}\rangle)\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
. Also by Corollary
3.3
, the functor
\mathrm{c}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{sGr}}
factors essentially uniquely as
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}})\to{\textbf{sGr}}
. Furthermore, the composition functor
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{B}}\langle S^{1}\rangle\hookrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\langle S^{1}\rangle)
factors essentially uniquely through
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}})
. Because of the uniqueness of factorisations, the following diagram of categories, which refines the previous one, commutes up to a natural equivalence

(14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle S^{1}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\langle S^{1}\rangle)}}
sGr
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{B}}

Lemma 3.7
.

Every arrow marked with
\simeq
in Diagram (
14
) is an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

That the middle arrow is an equivalence follows from the fact that the top arrow is an equivalence. That the bottom right arrow is an equivalence is Lemma
3.5
. It is of course well-known that the bottom arrow is an equivalence. This goes back to Kan
[
24
]
. One can also prove directly that the bottom left arrow is an equivalence, very similarly to the proof of Lemma
3.5
, using Lemma
3.4
. It then follows that the bottom arrow is an equivalence.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the main result of this section.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Diagram (
14
) induces the following diagram of functor categories, where the horizontal arrows are induced by the classifying space functor, and all arrows are equivalences (for the lower vertical arrows this follows from Lemma
3.2
):

(15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{sGr}},{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}(\langle S^{1}\rangle),{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}),{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun(\langle S^{1}\rangle,{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\operatorname{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\operatorname{B}}}

Going from top left to bottom right corner gives the desired equivalence.
∎

4.
Cubical diagrams

We saw that the inclusion
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{sGr}}
induces an equivalence of functor categories
\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{sGr}},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})
. Our next goal is to prove that when
D
is stable, this equivalence restricts to an equivalence from the category of
excisive
functors from
sGr
to
D
to the category of
polynomial
functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
D
.

The notions of polynomial and excisive functors use cubical diagrams and cross-effects. In this section we review some generalities regarding cubical diagrams. This is elementary material, but we need to pinpoint some facts about the relationship between being cocartesian in an
\infty
-category and in its homotopy category.

Definition 4.1
.

Given a set
U
, let
\mathrm{P}(U)
denote the category (poset) of
finite
subsets of
U
and inclusions. Most of the time
U
will itself be finite, in which case
\mathrm{P}(U)
is the category of all subsets. We write
\mathrm{P}(n)
for the category of subsets of the set
\underline{n}:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Given an integer
i
, let
\mathrm{P}(U)_{\leq i}
denote the subcategory of
\mathrm{P}(U)
consisting of sets of cardinality at most
i
.

Given a category
D
, an
n
-dimensional cubical diagram in
D
is a functor
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n)\to{\textbf{D}}
.

Next, let us recall the well-known definitions of cartesian, cocartesian and strongly cocartesian cubical diagrams.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n)\to{\textbf{D}}
be a cubical diagram. We say that
\chi
is
cartesian
if it is a pullback cube. I.e., if the following natural map is an equivalence

\chi(\emptyset)\to\underset{\emptyset\neq U\subset\underline{n}}{\lim}\chi(U).

Dually, we say that
\chi
is
cocartesian
if it is a pushout cube. I.e., if the following natural map is an equivalence

\underset{U\subsetneq\underline{n}}{\colim}\chi(U)\to\chi(\underline{n}).

In yet another formulation:
\chi
is cocartesian if and only if
\chi
is equivalent to the left Kan extension of its restriction to
\mathrm{P}(n)_{\leq n-1}
. Finally, we say that
\chi
is
strongly cocartesian
if
\chi
is equivalent to the left Kan extension of its restriction to
\mathrm{P}(n)_{\leq 1}
. Specifically it means that for all
U\subset\underline{n}
the following natural map is an equivalence

\colim\chi|_{\mathrm{P}(U)_{\leq 1}}\to\chi(U).

The following definition of free and split cubical diagrams is perhaps less standard, but these types of cubes come up naturally in the study of polynomial functors. Let
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))
be the category that has the same objects as
\mathrm{P}(n)
, and just the identity morphisms. Let us remark that if
D
is a category with finite coproducts (but not necessarily other colimits), then the left Kan extension functor

\mathrm{L}\colon\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),{\textbf{D}})\to\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),{\textbf{D}})

exists, because pointwise Kan extension only involves finite coproducts in this case.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
D
be a category with finite coproducts. A cubical diagram
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n)\to{\textbf{D}}
is
split
if it is a left Kan extension of a functor
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))\to{\textbf{D}}
.

Example 4.4
.

Let us describe explicitly what a split
n
-dimensional cubical diagram looks like. A functor
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))\to{\textbf{D}}
is a collection of objects
X(U)
, where
U
ranges over subsets of
\underline{n}
. The left Kan extension of this functor to
\mathrm{P}(n)
is defined on objects by the formula

(16)

U\mapsto\coprod_{V\subset U}X(V),

and on the level of morphisms it is given by the obvious summand inclusions.

Remark 4.5
.

It follows from Definitions
4.2
and
4.3
that a cubical diagram is both split and cocartesian (resp. split and strongly cocartesian) if and only if it is a left Kan extension of a functor
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)_{\leq n-1})\to{\textbf{D}}
(resp. a functor
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)_{\leq 1})\to{\textbf{D}}
).

Example 4.6
.

To be really explicit, a split strongly cocartesian
n
-dimensional diagram is determined by
n+1
objects
X_{0},\ldots,X_{n}
and is given by the formula

U\mapsto X_{0}\sqcup\left(\coprod_{i\in U}X_{i}\right).

with maps being inclusions of wedge summands. More generally, every split strongly cocartesian cube in
D
is equivalent to one of this form.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
C
,
D
be categories with finite coproducts. Let
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
be a functor that preserves finite coproducts. Then
F
preserves split cubical diagrams, as well as split cocartesian cubical diagrams and split strongly cocartesian cubical diagrams.

Proof.

We claim that the following diagram of functor categories commutes up to a natural equivalence

(17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),{\textbf{C}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),{\textbf{C}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),{\textbf{D}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}

The reason that the diagram commutes is that, once again, the construction of left Kan extension only involves finite coproducts, and
F
preserves finite coproducts. The split cubical diagrams in
C
and
D
are precisely the objects in the essential image of the horizontal functors in this diagram. It follows that
F
preserves split cubical diagrams. The proof for split (strongly) cocartesian cubical diagrams is similar. One just needs to replace
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))
with
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)_{\leq n-1})
(or
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)_{\leq 1})
) in the left column of the diagram.
∎

Examples 4.8
.

Let
D
be an
\infty
-category with coproducts. Let
\ho(D)
denote the homotopy category of
D
. The canonical functor
{\textbf{D}}\to\ho({\textbf{D}})
from
D
to its homotopy category preserves coproducts, and therefore it preserves split (cocartesian) cubical diagrams.

If
D
is a stable
\infty
-category with a
t
-structure, then the inclusion functor
{\textbf{D}}^{\heartsuit}\hookrightarrow{\textbf{D}}
from the heart of
D
to
D
also preserves coproducts, and therefore preserves split (cocartesian) cubical diagrams.

The functor
\mathrm{c}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{sGr}}
, that sends a group to a constant simplicial group, preserves coproducts, and therefore preserves split strongly cocartesian cubical diagrams.

Notation 4.9
.

Let
\splitcube^{n}({\textbf{C}})\subset\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),{\textbf{C}})
be the full subcategory consisting of split cubical diagrams.

Suppose
I
is a small 1-category, and let
C
be an
\infty
-category. There is a natural functor

(18)

\ho(\fun({\textbf{I}},{\textbf{C}}))\to\fun({\textbf{I}},\ho{\textbf{C}}).

It is well known that in general this functor is not an equivalence of categories. It is neither essentially surjective on objects nor fully faithful. On the other hand, it is conservative. A map between diagrams in
C
an equivalence if and only if it induces an isomorphism between diagrams in
\ho{\textbf{C}}
.

Special cases when (
18
) or a related functor is an equivalence are therefore of note. One obvious case when (
18
) is an equivalence is when
I
is a discrete category. The following easy lemma gives another example when a certain restriction of (
18
) is an equivalence.

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose
C
has finite coproducts. The functor
\ho(\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),{\textbf{C}}))\to\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),\ho{\textbf{C}})
restricts to an equivalence of categories

\ho(\splitcube^{n}({\textbf{C}}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\splitcube^{n}(\ho{\textbf{C}})

from the homotopy category of split cubical diagrams in
C
to split cubical diagrams in the homotopy category of
C
. A similar result holds for cocartesian split diagrams and strongly cocartesian split diagrams.

Proof.

The category
\ho{\textbf{C}}
has finite coproducts, and the functor
{\textbf{C}}\to\ho{\textbf{C}}
preserves finite coproducts.
It follows that the functor
{\textbf{C}}\to\ho{\textbf{C}}
preserves split cubical diagrams (as well as cocartesian and strongly cocartesian split cubical diagrams) by Lemma
4.7
.
It follows that we have a functor
\splitcube^{n}{{\textbf{C}}}\to\splitcube^{n}{\ho{\textbf{C}}}
, which passes through a functor
\ho(\splitcube^{n}({\textbf{C}}))\to\splitcube^{n}(\ho{\textbf{C}})
. Furthermore, there is a commuting (up to isomorphism)
diagram of categories, as in (
17
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ho(\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),{\textbf{C}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ho(\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),{\textbf{C}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),\ho{\textbf{C}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),\ho{\textbf{C}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}

The left vertical functor is an equivalence, because
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))
is a discrete category. It follows that the essential image of the top horizontal functor maps surjectively onto the essential image of the bottom horizontal functor. This means that the functor
\ho(\splitcube^{n}({\textbf{C}}))\to\splitcube^{n}(\ho{\textbf{C}})
is essentially surjective on objects.

Since
\splitcube^{n}({\textbf{C}})
is the essential image of the left Kan extension functor, we obtain the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ho(\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),{\textbf{C}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ho(\splitcube^{n}({\textbf{C}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),\ho{\textbf{C}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\splitcube^{n}(\ho{\textbf{C}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}

We have shown that the right vertical functor is essentially surjective on objects. It remains to show that it induces a bijection on sets of morphisms. Let us suppose we are given two split cubical diagram
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\colon\mathrm{P}(n)\to{\textbf{C}}
. Let us denote the images of these cubes in
\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),\ho{\textbf{C}})
by
\ho\chi_{1}
and
\ho\chi_{2}
. We need to show that the set of morphisms from
\chi_{1}
to
\chi_{2}
in
\ho(\splitcube^{n}({\textbf{C}}))
maps bijectively to the set of morphisms from
\ho\chi_{1}
to
\ho\chi_{2}
in
\splitcube^{n}(\ho{\textbf{C}})
. By assumption
\chi_{1}
is split, which means that
\chi_{1}
is a left Kan extension of some functor
\chi^{0}_{1}\colon\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))\to{\textbf{C}}
. It follows that the set of morphisms from
\chi_{1}
to
\chi_{2}
in
\ho(\splitcube^{n}({\textbf{C}}))
is in bijective correspondence to the set of morphisms in
\ho(\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),{\textbf{C}}))
from
\chi^{0}_{1}
to the restriction of
\chi_{2}
to
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))
. Since the left vertical functor is an equivalence of categories, this is the same as the set of morphisms in
\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),\ho{\textbf{C}})
from
\ho\chi^{0}_{1}
to the restriction of
\ho\chi_{2}
to
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))
. Again using the fact that the horizontal morphisms are Kan extensions and the diagram commutes, this is the same as the set of morphisms in
\splitcube^{n}(\ho{\textbf{C}})
from
\ho\chi_{1}
to
\ho\chi_{2}
.

We have proved that the functor
\ho(\splitcube^{n}({\textbf{C}}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\splitcube^{n}(\ho{\textbf{C}})
is an equivalence. The analogous statement for split (strongly) cocartesian diagrams is proved similarly, replacing
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))
with
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)_{\leq n-1})
(or with
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)_{\leq 1})
)
∎

The following lemma is somewhat similar to the previous one. It is handy for detecting split cubical diagrams in an
\infty
-category.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
C
be a category that admits finite coproducts. A cubical diagram in
C
is split (resp. cocartesian split) if and only if its image in
\ho({\textbf{C}})
is split (resp. cocartesian split).

Proof.

We saw already that the functor
{\textbf{C}}\to\ho({\textbf{C}})
preserves (cocartesian) split cubical diagrams, so the “only if” direction follows. For the “if” direction, consider again the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),{\textbf{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),{\textbf{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ho(\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),{\textbf{C}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ho(\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),{\textbf{C}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),\ho({\textbf{C}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),\ho({\textbf{C}}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}

Let
\chi
be an object of
\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),{\textbf{C}})
, and let
\ho\chi
denote the image of
\chi
in
\fun(\mathrm{P}(n),\ho({\textbf{C}}))
. Suppose that
\ho\chi
is split, i.e. is in the image of the bottom horizontal functor. We want to prove that
\chi
is split. Since the vertical functors on the left of the diagram are essentially surjective on objects, it follows that there exists a split cubical diagram
\bar{\chi}\colon\mathrm{P}(n)\to{\textbf{C}}
such that
\ho\bar{\chi}\cong\ho\chi
, as cubical diagrams in
\ho(C)
. Since
\bar{\chi}
is split, it is a left Kan extension of a diagram
\bar{\chi}_{0}\colon\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))\to{\textbf{C}}
. By the commutativity of the diagram above,
\ho\bar{\chi}
is a left Kan extension of
\ho\bar{\chi}_{0}
. It follows that there is a natural map
\ho\bar{\chi}_{0}\to\ho\chi|_{\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))}
, that left Kan extends to the isomorphism
\ho\bar{\chi}\xrightarrow{\cong}\ho\chi
. Since the bottom left vertical map in the diagram above is an equivalence of categories, and the top left vertical map is essentially surjective on objects and on morphisms, a natural map
\ho\bar{\chi}_{0}\to\ho\chi|_{\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))}
in
\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),\ho({\textbf{C}}))
comes from a natural map
\bar{\chi}_{0}\to\chi|_{\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))}
in
\fun(\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)),{\textbf{C}})
. The left Kan extension of this map gives a map
\bar{\chi}\to\chi
. Using again the commutativity of the diagram above, the map
\bar{\chi}\to\chi
becomes an isomorphism of diagrams in
\ho({\textbf{C}})
, and therefore
\bar{\chi}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\chi
is an equivalence. Since
\bar{\chi}
is a split cubical diagram by assumption, we have proved that
\chi
is a split cubical diagram.

A similar argument applies to split cocartesian diagrams. One just needs to replace
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n))
with
\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n)_{\leq n-1})
everywhere in the argument.
∎

5.
Polynomial functors

The reason that we spent so much space on split cubical diagrams is that they come up when studying the cross-effects of a functor, which in turn are important in the study of polynomial functors. We will next review the notion of a cross-effect. We will follow the original treatment from
[
14
]
, which constructed the cross-effects as images of certain idempotents. Let us briefly review from
[
27
, Section 4.4.5]
the meaning of an idempotent in an
\infty
-category.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
Idem
be the category consisting of a single object, whose monoid of endomorphisms consist of two elements
\{\id,e\}
, where the composition is determined by the relation
e^{2}=e
. Given a category
D
, an idempotent in
D
is a functor
{\textbf{Idem}}\to{\textbf{D}}
.

When
D
is a
1
-category, an idempotent in
D
is an endomorphism
f
of some object in
D
that satisfies
f\circ f=f
. In
1
-categories being an idempotent is a property of an endomorphism. When
D
is an
\infty
-category, an idempotent in
D
is an endomorphism
f
together with compatible homotopies between
f
and its iterates. In
\infty
-categories, being an idempotent is a structure on an endomorphism.

We will avoid subtleties concerning idempotents in
\infty
-categories by carrying out this part of the discussion in the context of
1
-categories. Then in the end we will lift the result that we need to
\infty
-categories using Lemma
4.11
. But let us also remark that it might not be very difficult to carry out the entire forthcoming discussion on the level of
stable

\infty
-categories, since idempotents in the homotopy category of a stable
\infty
-category have lifts to
\infty
-idempotents
[
28
, Lemma 1.2.4.6]
.

Until further notice, let
C
be a pointed
1
-category with finite coproducts, possibly enriched over topological spaces, and let
D
be an idempotent complete additive
1
-category (also known as a Karoubian category). We denote the coproduct in
C
by
\vee
and the coproduct in
D
by
\oplus
. We denote the zero object of
C
by
*
and the zero object of
D
by
0
. Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be objects of
C
.

Definition 5.2
.

For every subset
U\subset\underline{n}
, let

\psi_{U}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\colon X_{1}\vee\ldots\vee X_{n}\to X_{1}\vee\ldots\vee X_{n}

be the map that is the identity on
X_{i}
for each
i\in U
, and collapses
X_{i}
to
*
for each
i\not\in U
.

Sometimes we will just write
\psi_{U}
instead of
\psi_{U}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
, if the
X_{i}
s are clear from the context, or are irrelevant.
It is clear that
\{\psi_{U}\mid U\subset\underline{n}\}
are pairwise commuting idempotents, which satisfy the relation
\psi_{U}\circ\psi_{V}=\psi_{U\cap V}
.

Now let
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
be a functor (if
C
is topologically enriched while
D
is an “ordinary” category, then we assume
F
to be topologically enriched, in the sense of inducing locally constant maps on morphism spaces). For each
U\subset\underline{n}
we have the idempotent

F(\psi_{U})(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\colon F(X_{1}\vee\ldots\vee X_{n})\to F(X_{1}\vee\ldots\vee X_{n}).

By functoriality of
F
, the maps
F(\psi_{U})
are pairwise commuting idempotents, satisfying
F(\psi_{U})\circ F(\psi_{V})=F(\psi_{U\cap V})
. Because
D
is an additive category, we may add and subtract maps of the form
F(\psi_{U})
. Therefore the following definition makes sense

Definition 5.3
.

For every subset
U\subset\underline{n}
, define the map

(19)

D_{U}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\colon F(X_{1}\vee\ldots\vee X_{n})\to F(X_{1}\vee\ldots\vee X_{n})

by the formula

D_{U}F=\sum_{V\subset U}(-1)^{|U|-|V|}F(\psi_{V}).

Lemma 5.4
.

The maps
\{D_{U}F\mid U\subset\underline{n}\}
are pairwise orthogonal idempotents whose sum is the identity.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward. The lemma is proved as part of the proof of
[
14
, Theorem 9.1]
. It also is
[
2
, Lemma 3.21]
, with the following two minor caveats: In
[
14
]
the lemma is proved for functors between abelian categories, and in
[
2
]
it is proved for functors from spectra to spectra. But it is clear that the proof works equally well for functors from a pointed category to an additive category. Second, in
[
14
]
it is assumed that
F
is a reduced functor, i.e., that it preserves the zero object. But there is no need to assume this. This means that we have to include
U=\emptyset
in the decomposition. Note that
D_{\emptyset}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
is the composition

F(X_{1}\vee\ldots\vee X_{n})\to F(*)\to F(X_{1}\vee\ldots\vee X_{n}).

∎

Definition 5.5
.

Let
\ce_{U}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
denote the image of the idempotent map (
19
). When
U=\underline{n}
, we denote it simply by
\ce_{n}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
. We call the functor of
n
variables
\ce_{n}F
the
n
-th cross-effect of
F
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
be a functor, and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be objects of
C
. The idempotents
D_{U}F
induce an isomorphism

F(X_{1}\vee\ldots\vee X_{n})\xrightarrow{\cong}\bigoplus_{U\subset\underline{n}}\ce_{U}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}).

Moreover, the functor
\ce_{U}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
depends only on
X_{i}
for
i\in U
. More precisely, suppose we have morphisms
h_{i}\colon X_{i}\to Y_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
and a set
U
such that
h_{i}
is an isomorphism for all
i\in U
. Then the induced map

\ce_{U}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\to\ce_{U}F(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})
is an isomorphism. In particular, if we have an injective map
\underline{m}\hookrightarrow\underline{n}
, and
U\subset\underline{m}
, then it induces an isomorphism
\ce_{U}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})\xrightarrow{\cong}\ce_{U}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
.

Proof.

The first statement is an immediate consequence of Lemma
5.4
, and also is
[
14
, Theorem 9.1]
. The second statement is very easy to prove from the definition of the idempotents
D_{U}
.
∎

The following easy and well-known lemma is left as an exercise for the reader

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
F
be a functor as above. There is a natural isomorphism

\ce_{n}F(X_{1}\vee X_{2},X_{3},\ldots,X_{n+1})\cong\\
\cong\ce_{n}F(X_{1},X_{3},\ldots,X_{n+1})\oplus\ce_{n}F(X_{2},X_{3},\ldots,X_{n+1})\oplus\ce_{n+1}F(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n+1}).

The following is an immediate corollary

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
F
be a functor as above. Suppose
\ce_{n}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=0
for all
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in{\textbf{C}}
. Then
\ce_{n+1}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1})=0
for all
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}
. More generally,
\ce_{m}F
is the trivial functor for all
m\geq n
.

Now we can define polynomial functors

Definition 5.9
.

Suppose
C
is a pointed category with coproducts, and
D
be either an idempotent complete stable
\infty
-category or an idempotent complete additive
1
-category. We say that a functor
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
is polynomial of degree
n
if
\ce_{n+1}\ho F
is the trivial functor of
n+1
variables. Here by
\ho F
we mean
F
if
D
is a
1
-category, and the composition
{\textbf{C}}\xrightarrow{F}{\textbf{D}}\to\ho{\textbf{D}}
if
D
is a stable
\infty
-category.

It follows from Corollary
5.8
that if
F
is polynomial of degree
n
then
F
is polynomial of degree
m
for all
m\geq n
. We say that
F
is polynomial if it is polynomial of some degree. The next proposition will help establish a connection between polynomial functors and excisive functors in the sense of Goodwillie.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
C
be a pointed
1
-category with finite coproducts. Let
D
be either an idempotent complete stable
\infty
-category or an idempotent complete additive
1
-category. Let
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
be a functor and let
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n+1)\to{\textbf{C}}
be a split strongly cocartesian cubical diagram. Then
F\circ\chi
is always a split cubical diagram. If
F
is polynomial of degree
n
, then
F\circ\chi
is cocartesian. Conversely, if
F\circ\chi
is cocartesian for all split strongly cocartesian
n+1
-dimensional diagrams
\chi
, then
F
is polynomial of degree
n
.

Proof.

Let us first suppose that
D
is an additive
1
-category. We saw in Example
4.6
that there exist objects
X_{0},X_{1},\ldots X_{n+1}
such that
\chi
is equivalent to the diagram given by the formula

\chi(U)=X_{0}\vee\bigvee_{i\in U}X_{i}.

It follows that there are isomorphisms, by Lemma
5.6
, of the following form: suppose
U=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}\subset\underline{n+1}

F\circ\chi(U)\xrightarrow{\cong}\bigoplus_{V\subset U\cup\{0\}}\ce_{V}F(X_{0},X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{s}}).

Furthermore, by the second part of the same lemma, the maps in the cube
F\circ\chi
are as follows: for
V\subset U\subset U^{\prime}
the summands
\ce_{V}F(X_{0},X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{s}})
and
\ce_{V\cup\{0\}}F(X_{0},X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{s}})
of
F\circ\chi(U)
are mapped isomorphically to the summands
\ce_{V}F(\ldots)
and
\ce_{V\cup\{0\}}F(\ldots)
of
F\circ\chi(U^{\prime})
. Here we assumed that
U=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\}
and we avoided naming the elements of
U^{\prime}
.

It follows that
F\circ\chi
is isomorphic to the left Kan extension of the functor
\Theta\colon\operatorname{ob}(\mathrm{P}(n+1))\to{\textbf{D}}
that is defined by the formula

\Theta(U)=\ce_{U}F(X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{s}})\oplus\ce_{U\cup\{0\}}F(X_{0},X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{s}}).

Compare with Example
4.4
. Note that since we assume that
F
takes values in a
1
-category, it is enough that we have verified that
F\circ\chi
is isomorphic to the left Kan extension of
\Theta
on the level of objects and morphisms. We do not need to worry about higher simplices. It follows that
F\circ\chi
is always a split cube, regardless of whether
F
is polynomial.

Suppose
F
is polynomial of degree
n
. Then we have the isomorphism

\Theta(\underline{n+1})=\ce_{n+1}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1})\oplus\ce_{n+2}F(X_{0},\ldots,X_{n+1})\cong 0,

and it follows that
F\circ\chi
is a left Kan extension of the functor

\Theta|_{\mathrm{P}(n+1)_{\leq n}}\colon\mathrm{P}(n+1)_{\leq n}\to{\textbf{D}}.

Thus
F\circ\chi
is split cocartesian (see Remark
4.5
). Conversely, if
F\circ\chi
is always cocartesian, then it implies that
\chi_{n+1}F(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1})=0
for all
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1}
, which implies that
F
is polynomial of degree
n
.

Now suppose that
D
is an idempotent complete stable
\infty
-category. Then
\ho{\textbf{D}}
is an idempotent complete additive
1
-category. If
\chi
is a split strongly cocartesian cubical diagram in
C
then the image of
F\circ\chi
in
\ho{\textbf{D}}
is a split diagram by the first part of the proof, and therefore
F\circ\chi
is a split diagram by Lemma
4.11
. If
F
is polynomial of degree
n
, then by definition
\ho F
is polynomial, so the image of
F\circ\chi
in
\ho{\textbf{D}}
is split strongly cocartesian, again by the first part of the proof, and therefore
F\circ\chi
is split strongly cocartesian, again by Lemma
4.11
. Finally, if
\ho F\circ\chi
is cocartesian for all split strongly cocartesian
\chi
then we know that
\ho F\circ\chi
is split cocartesian for all such
\chi
, so
\ho F
is polynomial, and thus
F
is polynomial.
∎

The next lemma says that if a functor preserves finite coproducts then composition with this functor preserves polynomial functors.

Lemma 5.11
.

Suppose
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
is polynomial of degree
n
, and
L\colon{\textbf{D}}\to{\textbf{D}}_{1}
is a functor that preserves finite coproducts. Then
L\circ F
is polynomial of degree
n
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.10
,
F
is polynomial of degree
n
if and only if for every strongly cocartesian split cubical diagram
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n+1)\to{\textbf{C}}
,
F\circ\chi
is cocartesian split. By Lemma
4.7
, it follows that
L\circ F\circ\chi
is cocartesian split for all strongly cocartesian split
\chi
, and therefore
L\circ F
is polynomial of degree
n
.
∎

Example 5.12
.

Let
D
be a stable
\infty
-category with a
t
-structure. Suppose
C
is, as usual, a pointed category with finite coproducts, and
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}^{\heartsuit}
is a polynomial functor of degree
n
. Then the functor
{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
obtained by composing
F
with the inclusion functor
{\textbf{D}}^{\heartsuit}\to{\textbf{D}}
is polynomial of degree
n
, since the inclusion functor preserves coproducts.

6.
Excisive functors

Excisive functors are defined similarly to polynomial functors, but with a stricter hypothesis. They turned out to be very useful in the study of homotopical functors. In this section we review excisive functors, and establish a precise relationship between polynomial functors on the category of (finitely generated free) groups, and excisive functors on the category of simplicial groups.

Let us recall a condition (
[
28
, Definition 6.1.1.6]
) on a category
D
that helps ensure that Goodwillie calculus works as it should for functors with values in
D

Definition 6.1
.

An
\infty
-category
D
is
differentiable
if it has small colimits and finite limits, and sequential colimits in
D
commute with finite limits.

Remark 6.2
.

Let us remark that if
D
is any one of the following:

•

a stable
\infty
-category with countable coproducts

•

an
\infty
-topos

•

a compactly generated
\infty
-category

then
D
is differentiable
[
28
, Examples 6.1.1.7-9]
.

Let us recall what excisive functors are.

Definition 6.3
.

Suppose
C
is a category with small colimits and
D
differentiable. We say that a functor
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
is
n
-excisive if (a)
F
preserves filtered colimits, and (b) for every strongly cocartesian diagram
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n+1)\to{\textbf{C}}
, the diagram
F\circ\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n+1)\to{\textbf{D}}
is cartesian.
We say that a functor is excisive if it is
n
-excisive for some
n
.

Remarks 6.4
.

The definition of an excisive functor is due to Goodwillie
[
19
]
, except that Goodwillie did not include the requirement that it preserves filtered colimits. But we are only interested in functors that preserve filtered colimits, so we made it part of the definition. Functors that preserve filtered colimits are called
finitary
in
[
19
]
.

If
D
is a stable
\infty
-category with small colimits then it automatically has finite limits, and a cubical diagram in
D
is cocartesian if and only if it is cartesian
[
28
, Proposition 1.2.4.13]
. Thus for functors with values in a stable
\infty
-category we may equivalently state condition (b) as that
F
takes strongly cocartesian
n+1
-dimensional diagrams to cocartesian diagrams.

We will mostly be interested in excisive functors with values in a stable
\infty
-category. It turns out that such functors preserve all sifted colimits.

Lemma 6.5
.

A functor with values in a stable
\infty
-category that is excisive in the sense of Definition
6.3
preserves sifted colimits.

Proof.

Let
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
be an excisive functor. By
[
27
, Corollary 5.5.8.17]
, it is enough to prove that
F
preserves filtered colimits and geometric realisations.
F
preserves filtered colimits by definition. Therefore, it is enough to prove that
F
preserves geometric realisations. In fact, it is enough to know that
F
preserves
finite
geometric realisations, because every simplicial object is equivalent to a filtered colimit of skeletal simplicial objects.

Preservation of finite geometric realisations in known for functors
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
(where
D
is stable) that take strongly cocartesian
n
-dimensional cubes to cocartesian
n
-dimensional cubes. This was proved by Barwick-Glasman-Mathew-Nikolaus as part of
[
5
, Proposition 2.15]
. Barwick-Glasman-Mathew-Nikolaus only state the claim in the case when
C
and
D
are both stable
\infty
-categories, but the relevant part of the proof does not require
C
to be stable. The main ingredient of the proof is Goodwillie’s equivalence between homogeneous functors and symmetric multi-linear functors, and this equivalence holds for functors from an arbitrary
\infty
-category to a stable
\infty
-category. See
[
28
]
Section 6.1.4, in particular Proposition 6.1.4.14.
∎

Notations 6.6
.

Let
\poly_{n}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})\subset\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
denote the category of polynomial functors of degree
n
, and
\exc_{n}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
the category of
n
-excisive functors.

Remark 6.7
.

It follows from Lemma
6.5
that if
C
has sifted colimits and
D
is stable, then
\exc_{n}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
us a (full) subcategory of
\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
. Notice furthermore that
\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
is a full subcategory of
\fun_{\operatorname{ind}}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
of functors that preserve filtered colimits. By definition, Goodwillie’s functor
P_{n}\colon\fun_{\operatorname{ind}}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})\to\exc_{n}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
is left adjoint to inclusion. Here we have restricted the domain of
P_{n}
to consist of filtered-colimit-preserving functors because we have defined
\exc_{n}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
to consists just of filtered-colimit-preserving excisive functors (the assumption that
D
is differentiable guarantees that if
F
preserves filtered colimits then so does
P_{n}F
). It follows that the restriction of
P_{n}
to
\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
, which we will also denote simply by
P_{n}
, is also a left adjoint to the inclusion
\exc_{n}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})\hookrightarrow\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
.

Now we are ready to compare polynomial functors on
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
with excisive functors on
sGr
(or, equivalently,
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
).

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
D
be a stable
\infty
-category with all small colimits. For each
n\geq 0
, the equivalence of categories (seen in Diagram (
15
))

\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{sGr}},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})

restricts to an equivalence

\exc_{n}({\textbf{sGr}},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}}).

Proof.

Let
F\in\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{sGr}},{\textbf{D}})
be a functor from
sGr
to
D
that preserves sifted colimits. To prove the proposition it is enough to show that the restriction of
F
to
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
is polynomial of degree
n
if and only if
F
is
n
-excisive.

First let us prove the “if” direction, which is easier. Suppose
F\colon{\textbf{sGr}}\to{\textbf{D}}
is
n
-excisive. We want to show that the restriction of
F
to
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
is polynomial of degree
n
. By Proposition
5.10
, this is equivalent to showing that
F
takes split strongly cocartesian
n+1
-dimensional diagrams in
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to cocartesian diagrams in
D
. By one of the examples in
4.8
, a split strongly cocartesian diagram in
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
is also strongly cocartesian in
sGr
. Since
F
is
n
-excisive on
sGr
, the desired claim follows.

Now suppose that
F\in\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{sGr}},{\textbf{D}})
is a sifted colimits preserving functor that restricts to a polynomial functor of degree
n
on
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
. We need to prove that
F
is
n
-excisive. First of all, we claim that
F
restricts to a polynomial functor on the category
Fr
of all free groups (as opposed to just the finitely generated ones). To prove this claim we have to show that
F
takes split strongly cocartesian
n+1
-dimensional cubical diagrams in
Fr
to cocartesian diagrams in
D
. Let
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n+1)\to{\textbf{Fr}}
be a split strongly cocartesian diagram. It is easy to show that
\chi
is equivalent to a filtered colimit of split strongly cocartesian diagrams in
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
. Let us note that filtered colimits in the
1
-category
Fr
are also filtered colimits in the
\infty
-category
sGr
. We assume that
F
preserves sifted colimits, so in particular preserves filtered colimits. It follows that
F\circ\chi
is equivalent to a filtered colimit of cocartesian diagrams, and thus it is itself cocartesian.

Finally we have to show that if
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n+1)\to{\textbf{sGr}}
is a strongly cocartesian diagram, then
F\circ\chi
is cocartesian. For this we will use that
sGr
is the underlying
\infty
-category of a Quillen model structure on the
1
-category of simplicial groups. Let us recall a few facts about the Quillen model structure on
sGr
(now considered as a
1
-category). The model structure on
sGr
is cofibrantly generated, where generating cofibrations are
almost-free maps
. The definition of an almost free map can be found in
[
17
, Page 257]
. For our purposes, the relevant property of almost free maps is the following: Suppose
\theta_{\bullet}\colon G_{\bullet}\to H_{\bullet}
is an almost free map. Then in each simplicial degree
n
,
\theta_{n}
is isomorphic to an inclusion of a summand into a free product with a free group. The small-object argument can be used to replace every morphism
G_{\bullet}\to H_{\bullet}
in the
1
-category
sGr
with an equivalent almost free map. Furtheremore, one can replace
G_{\bullet}
with an equivalent almost free group. Thus any morphism in
sGr
is equivalent to a cofibration which in each simplicial degree is an inclusion of the form
F_{0}\hookrightarrow F_{0}*F_{1}
, where
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are free groups.

It follows that a strongly cocartesian diagram in the
\infty
-category
sGr
is equivalent to a strongly cocartesian diagram in the
1
-category of simplicial groups, where the initial maps are cofibrations that satisfy the condition above. Therefore we may assume that a strongly cocartesian diagram
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n+1)\to{\textbf{sGr}}
is a diagram of simplicial groups that in each simplicial degree restricts to a split strongly cocartesian diagram in the
1
-category
Fr
. We have seen that
F
restricts to a polynomial functor on
Fr
, so evaluating
F
in each simplicial degree of
\chi
gives a cocartesian diagram in
D
. Next, we assume that
F
preserves sifted colimits. It follows that
F\circ\chi
can be calculated as the colimit (over
\Delta^{{\mathrm{op}}}
) of the restriction of
F
to each simplicial degree of
\chi
. Thus
F\circ\chi
is a colimit of cocartesian diagrams and is therefore itself a cocartesian diagram.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

Since the inverse to the equivalence of categories

\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{sGr}},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})

is given by left Kan extension, it follows that the inverse of the restricted equivalence

\exc_{n}({\textbf{sGr}},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}}).

is also given by left Kan extension. In other words, if
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{D}}
is a polynomial functor, then its left Kan extension to
sGr
is an excisive functor
\mathrm{L}F\colon{\textbf{sGr}}\to{\textbf{D}}
.

Since the classifying space functor
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{sGr}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
is an equivalence, the following is a corollary

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
D
be a stable
\infty
-category with small colimits. Consider the classifying space functor
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
. For each
n\geq 0
it induces an equivalence of categories

\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}}).

7.
From connected spaces to all spaces

Our next step is to compare the functor category
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})
with
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
. The restriction functor

\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})

is not an equivalence. However, we will see that it restricts to an equivalence from
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
to
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})
. Moreover, the right Kan extension functor
\mathrm{R}\colon\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
restricts to an equivalence between categories of excisive functors, which is an inverse to the restriction functor.

To show this, let us begin by noting that the inclusion functor
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}\hookrightarrow{\textbf{S}}_{*}
preserves colimits, and therefore it preserves strongly cocartesian diagrams. Thus restriction along this inclusion really does induce a functor

\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}}).

To construct an inverse to the restriction functor we will use Goodwillie’s operator
T_{n}
, which he introduced as an ingredient in the definition of the better known operator
P_{n}
— the universal
n
excisive approximation. Roughly speaking,
P_{n}
is an infinite iterate of
T_{n}
.

The definition of
T_{n}
involves a strongly cocartesian cubical diagram of a particular type. Let us first recall the construction of this cubical diagram in the classical setting of topological spaces. Suppose
X
is a topological space and
U
is a finite set. Recall that
X*U
denotes the join of
X
and
U
. The space
X*U
is homeomorphic to the union of
|U|
copies of the cone on
X
, glued along
X
. In particular,
X*\emptyset\cong X
,
X*\{1\}\cong CX
(where
CX
denotes the cone on
X
) and
X*\{1,2\}\cong\Sigma X
(where
\Sigma X
is the suspension of
X
).

Suppose we let
U
range over subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We obtain a cubical diagram

\chi(U)=X*U.

The diagram
\chi
has the following properties

(1)

\chi(\emptyset)\cong X
.

(2)

\chi(\{i\})\simeq*

(3)

\chi
is strongly cocartesian.

These properties determine
\chi
up to equivalence. One can also characterise
\chi
as follows:
\chi
is a final object in the
\infty
-category of strongly cocartesian
n
-dimensional cubical diagrams whose initial object is
X
.

It is clear that the construction of a cubical diagram with properties (1)-(3) can be abstracted and extended to other categories. Kuhn showed how the construction can be carried out in a general model category
[
26
]
, and Lurie generalised it to
\infty
-categories. More precisely, Lurie showed the following

Lemma 7.1

(
[
28
]
, Construction 6.1.1.18)
.

Let
C
be a category with finite colimits and a final object
*
. Let
FI
be the category of finite sets and injective functions between them. There is an essentially unique functor
{\textbf{C}}\times{\textbf{FI}}\to{\textbf{C}}
, which we will denote by
(X,U)\mapsto C_{U}(X)
with the following properties

(1)

C_{\emptyset}(X)\simeq X
. More precisely
C_{\emptyset}(-)
is equivalent to the identity functor on
C
.

(2)

C_{\{i\}}(X)\simeq*

(3)

For all finite sets
U
the following natural map is an equivalence

\underset{S\in\mathrm{P}(U)_{\leq 1}}{\colim}C_{S}(X)\xrightarrow{\simeq}C_{U}(X).

Remarks 7.2
.

The construction
C_{U}(X)
is an abstraction of
X*U
.

In particular, it is clear from the lemma that
C_{\{1,2\}}(X)\simeq*\coprod_{X}*
is a suspension of
X
.

It is also clear from the lemma that if we let
U
range over subsets of
\underline{n}
then the assignment
U\mapsto C_{U}(X)
gives a strongly cocartesian cubical diagram in
C
.

We will need an easy lemma to the effect that
C_{U}
preserves certain colimits.

Lemma 7.3
.

Suppose
C
is a category with finite colimits and a final object
*
. Let
I
be a finite category, or more generally a small category such that
C
has
I
-shaped colimits. Assume that the following natural map is an equivalence

\underset{{\textbf{I}}}{\colim}*\to*.

Then for any fixed finite set
U
the functor
C_{U}(-)\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{C}}
preserves
I
-shaped colimits.

Proof.

Suppose we have a functor
X\colon{\textbf{I}}\to{\textbf{C}}
, which we will denote by
\alpha\mapsto X_{\alpha}
. We need to show that the natural map

\underset{\alpha\in{\textbf{I}}}{\colim}C_{U}(X_{\alpha})\to C_{U}(\underset{\alpha\in{\textbf{I}}}{\colim}X_{\alpha})

is an equivalence. By definition of
C_{U}(X)
, we have a natural equivalence

\underset{S\in\mathrm{P}(U)_{\leq 1}}{\colim}C_{S}(X)\xrightarrow{\simeq}C_{U}(X).

Therefore, we need to show that the following map is an equivalence

\underset{\alpha\in{\textbf{I}}}{\colim}\underset{S\in\mathrm{P}(U)_{\leq 1}}{\colim}C_{S}(X_{\alpha})\to\underset{S\in\mathrm{P}(U)_{\leq 1}}{\colim}C_{S}(\underset{\alpha\in{\textbf{I}}}{\colim}X_{\alpha}).

Exchanging the order of colimits, this is equivalent to showing that the following is an equivalence

\underset{S\in\mathrm{P}(U)_{\leq 1}}{\colim}\underset{\alpha\in{\textbf{I}}}{\colim}C_{S}(X_{\alpha})\to\underset{S\in\mathrm{P}(U)_{\leq 1}}{\colim}C_{S}(\underset{\alpha\in{\textbf{I}}}{\colim}X_{\alpha}).

For this it is enough to show that the following is an equivalence whenever
S
has cardinality at most
1
:

\underset{\alpha\in{\textbf{I}}}{\colim}C_{S}(X_{\alpha})\to C_{S}(\underset{\alpha\in{\textbf{I}}}{\colim}X_{\alpha}).

When
S=\emptyset
,
C_{S}(X)\simeq X
, and the above map is equivalent to the identity map on
\underset{\alpha\in{\textbf{I}}}{\colim}X_{\alpha}
. When
S
is a singleton,
C_{S}(X)\simeq*
, and the above map becomes
\colim_{{\textbf{I}}}*\to*
. This map is an equivalence by hypothesis.
∎

Examples 7.4
.

If
C
is a pointed category then
*
is both an initial and a final object, and then the map
\colim_{{\textbf{I}}}*\to*
is an equivalence for all diagrams
I
. So if
C
is pointed then
C_{U}(-)
preserves all colimits. In general, the map
\colim_{{\textbf{I}}}*\to*
is an equivalence whenever the geometric realisation of
I
is contractible. It follows that
C_{U}(-)
always preserves cocartesian and strongly cocartesian cubical diagrams.

Now we can recall the definition of Goodwillie’s operator
T_{n}
, adapted to the setting of
\infty
categories. The following is taken from
[
28
, Construction 6.1.1.22]

Definition 7.5
.

Let
C
be a category with finite colimits and a final object, and let
D
be a category with finite limits. We define the functor
T_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
as follows. Let
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
be a functor. We define a new functor
T_{n}F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
by the formula

T_{n}F(X)=\lim_{\emptyset\neq U\subset\underline{n+1}}F(C_{U}(X)).

When we need to make the category
C
visible, we write
T_{n}^{{\textbf{C}}}
instead of
T_{n}
.

The following is immediate

Lemma 7.6
.

There is a natural transformation
F\to T_{n}F
, which is an equivalence if
F
is
n
-excisive.

Proof.

The diagram
U\mapsto C_{U}(X)
is strongly cocartesian. If
F
is
n
-excisive it takes strongly cocartesian diagrams to cartesian diagrams.
∎

Remark 7.7
.

Under mild assumptions, the “if” in the last lemma is in fact an “if and only if”. The only if direction follows because if
F\to T_{n}F
is an equivalence, then
F\to P_{n}F
is an equivalence, and
P_{n}F
is an
n
-excisive functor for all
F
by a theorem of Goodwillie
[
28
, Lemma 6.1.1.33]
.

Next let us notice that if
F
is a functor from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
to
D
, then
T_{n}F
can be considered a functor from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
to
D
, because
T_{n}F(X)
depends only on
X*U
where
U
is non-empty, and this space is always connected. Let us formalise this observation in a more general setting.

Hypothesis 7.8
.

Let
C
be a category with small colimits and a final object
*
. Let
{\textbf{C}}_{1}\subset{\textbf{C}}
be a full subcategory with the following properties:

(1)

if
X\in\operatorname{ob}({\textbf{C}}_{1})
and
Y\simeq X
, then
Y\in\operatorname{ob}({\textbf{C}}_{1})
(this assumption is made for convenience and is probably not essential),

(2)

{\textbf{C}}_{1}
is closed under finite colimits in
C
and contains the final objects of
C
,

(3)

For every object
X
of
C
,
\Sigma X\in{\textbf{C}}_{1}
. Here
\Sigma X=\colim(*\leftarrow X\rightarrow*)
is the suspension of
X
.

Example 7.9
.

The subcategory
{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}\subset{\textbf{S}}_{*}
satisfies Hypothesis
7.8
.

We have the following easy but important lemma

Lemma 7.10
.

Suppose
{\textbf{C}}_{1}\subset{\textbf{C}}
satisfy Hypothesis
7.8
, and let
X
be an object of
C
. Then
C_{U}(X)
is an object of
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
for every
non-empty
finite set
U
.

Proof.

When
U
is a singleton,
C_{U}(X)
is a final object, which is in
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
by hypothesis. When
U
has two points,
C_{U}(X)
is a suspension of
X
, which is in
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
, also by hypothesis. For larger sets
U
, notice that there is an equivalence

C_{\underline{n+1}}(X)\simeq\colim\left(C_{\underline{n}}(X)\leftarrow C_{\underline{1}}(X)\rightarrow C_{\underline{2}}(X)\right).

The equivalence is easily verified using property (
3
) in the statement of Lemma
7.1
. Since
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
is closed under finite colimits, it follows by induction on the number of elements in
U
that
C_{U}(X)
is an object
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
for all non-empty finite
U
.
∎

Suppose that
{\textbf{C}}_{1}\subset{\textbf{C}}
are categories that satisfy Hypothesis
7.8
. We saw in Lemma
7.1
that there is a functor
{\textbf{C}}\times{\textbf{FI}}\to{\textbf{C}}
, which sends
(X,U)
to
C_{U}(X)
, that satisfies certain properties. Let
{\textbf{FI}}_{\geq 1}
be the full subcategory of
FI
consisting of non-empty sets. By Lemma
7.10
, for every
X\in{\textbf{C}}_{1}
and
U\in{\textbf{FI}}_{\geq 1}
,
C_{U}(X)
is an object of
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
. Since
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
is a full subcategory of
C
it follows that the functor
(X,U)\mapsto C_{U}(X)
factors essentially uniquely as a functor

(20)

{\textbf{C}}\times{\textbf{FI}}_{\geq 1}\to{\textbf{C}}_{1}.

We will continue denoting this functor by
(X,U)\mapsto C_{U}(X)
.

Suppose
D
is a category with finite limits, and we have a functor
F\colon{\textbf{C}}_{1}\to{\textbf{D}}
. We have defined the functor
T_{n}F\colon{\textbf{C}}_{1}\to{\textbf{D}}
by the formula
T_{n}F(X)=\underset{\emptyset\neq U\subset\underline{n+1}}{\lim}F(C_{U}(X))
. Now we know that for any fixed
X\in{\textbf{C}}
, the functor
U\mapsto C_{U}(X)
takes values in
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
. Thus
T_{n}F(X)
is well-defined for all
X\in{\textbf{C}}
, and indeed we may consider
T_{n}F
as a functor from
C
to
D
. To emphasise that we have extended the domain, we denote this extension of
T_{n}F
by
\widetilde{T}_{n}F
.

Definition 7.11
.

Let
{\textbf{C}}_{1},{\textbf{C}}
,
D
, and
F\colon{\textbf{C}}_{1}\to D
be as above. Define the functor
\widetilde{T}_{n}\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
by the formula

\widetilde{T}_{n}F(X)=\underset{\emptyset\neq U\subset\underline{n+1}}{\lim}F(C_{U}(X)).

With a bit of straightforward diagram chasing, one can prove the following

Lemma 7.12
.

The above formula for
\widetilde{T}_{n}F
gives rise to a well-defined functor

\widetilde{T}_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{C}}_{1},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}}).

Furthermore, let
\rho
be the restriction functor

\rho\colon\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{C}}_{1},{\textbf{D}}).

Then
\rho\circ\widetilde{T}_{n}\simeq T_{n}^{{\textbf{C}}_{1}}
and
\widetilde{T}_{n}\circ\rho\simeq T_{n}^{{\textbf{C}}}
.

We have the following lemma

Lemma 7.13
.

Suppose
F\colon{\textbf{C}}_{1}\to{\textbf{D}}
is
n
-excisive. Then
\widetilde{T}_{n}F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
is
n
-excisive.

Proof.

Suppose that we have a strongly cocartesian diagram
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n+1)\to{\textbf{C}}
. To reduce the amount of parentheses, we will write
\chi_{V}
instead of
\chi(V)
. We need to show that the following map is an equivalence

\widetilde{T}_{n}F(\chi_{\emptyset})\to\underset{\emptyset\neq V\subset\underline{n+1}}{\lim}\widetilde{T}_{n}F(\chi_{V}).

Substituting the definition of
\widetilde{T}_{n}F
, this is equivalent to showing that the following map is an equivalence

\underset{\emptyset\neq U\subset\underline{n+1}}{\lim}F(C_{U}(\chi_{\emptyset}))\to\underset{\emptyset\neq V\subset\underline{n+1}}{\lim}\left(\underset{\emptyset\neq U\subset\underline{n+1}}{\lim}F(C_{U}(\chi_{V}))\right).

Here the diagram
U\mapsto C_{U}(\chi_{V})
lives in
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
, as
U
is always non-empty. Next, we may exchange the order of limits, and rewrite the last map in the following equivalent form

(21)

\underset{\emptyset\neq U\subset\underline{n+1}}{\lim}F(C_{U}(\chi_{\emptyset}))\to\underset{\emptyset\neq U\subset\underline{n+1}}{\lim}\left(\underset{\emptyset\neq V\subset\underline{n+1}}{\lim}F(C_{U}(\chi_{V}))\right).

In this form we can think of this map as the limit of of a diagram of maps of the following form, for each fixed non-empty
U\subset{\underline{n+1}}

(22)

F(C_{U}(\chi_{\emptyset}))\to\underset{\emptyset\neq V\subset\underline{n+1}}{\lim}F(C_{U}(\chi_{V})).

To prove that the map (
21
) is an equivalence, it is enough to prove that the map (
22
) is an equivalence for each
U
. For each non-empty
U
, the diagram
V\mapsto C_{U}(\chi_{V})
is a strongly cocartesian diagram in
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
by Lemma
7.3
and Examples
7.4
, as well as Lemma
7.10
and the fact that
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
is closed under colimits in
C
. Since
F
is assumed to be an
n
-excisive functor on
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
, it follows that (
22
) is an equivalence.
∎

Lemmas
7.12
and
7.13
together imply the following proposition

Proposition 7.14
.

Suppose
{\textbf{C}}_{1},{\textbf{C}}
satisfy Hypothesis
7.8
, and
D
is an
\infty
-category with finite limits. Then the construction
\widetilde{T}_{n}
and the restriction functor
\rho
define inverse equivalences of categories

\widetilde{T}_{n}:\hbox to164.94pt{\vbox to17.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 82.4707pt\lower-8.65973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Notice that since for every
m\geq n
an
n
-excisive functor is also
m
-excisive, one can replace
\widetilde{T}_{n}
in the proposition with
\widetilde{T}_{m}
for all
n\leq m\leq\infty
.

Corollary 7.15
.

Let
D
be an
\infty
-category with finite limits. Then
\widetilde{T}_{n}
and the restriction induces inverse equivalences of categories

\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\simeq\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}}).

Furthermore,
\widetilde{T}_{n}
can be replaced with
\widetilde{T}_{m}
for all
n\leq m\leq\infty
.

There is another interpretation of the functor
\widetilde{T}_{n}
that seems worth mentioning, even though we will not make direct use of it. Namely,
\widetilde{T}_{n}
is right Kan extension, or more precisely, it agrees with right Kan extension on excisive functors.
Suppose
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
is a subcategory of
C
. Let
\mathrm{R}\colon\fun({\textbf{C}}_{1},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
denote the right Kan extension.

Proposition 7.16
.

Suppose
{\textbf{C}}_{1}\subset{\textbf{C}}
satisfy Hypothesis
7.8
, and
D
is a differentiable category. The following diagram commutes up to a natural equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\exc_{n}({\textbf{C}}_{1},{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun({\textbf{C}}_{1},{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\exc_{n}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{T}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}

Recall that
\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,G)
denotes the space of maps from
F
to
G
in the relevant category of functors. In what follows, the source category of
F
and
G
may be either
C
or
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
, but the target category
D
will be fixed, so we do not make it part of the notation. Also, given a functor
F\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
we denote the restriction of
F
to
{\textbf{C}}_{1}
by
\rho F
.

Proof.

Let
F\in\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
and
G\in\exc_{n}({\textbf{C}}_{1},{\textbf{D}})
. To prove the proposition it is enough to show that there is a natural equivalence

\nat_{{\textbf{C}}_{1}}(\rho F,G)\simeq\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,\widetilde{T}_{n}G).

We have a chain of maps

\nat_{{\textbf{C}}_{1}}(\rho F,G)\xrightarrow{\alpha}\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho F,\widetilde{T}_{n}G)\xrightarrow{\beta}\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,\widetilde{T}_{n}G)\xrightarrow{\gamma}\\
\to\nat_{{\textbf{C}}_{1}}(\rho F,\rho\widetilde{T}_{n}G)\xrightarrow{\delta}\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho F,\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho\widetilde{T}_{n}G).

Let us point out that the map
\beta
is restriction along the map
F\to T_{n}F
, followed by the natural equivalence
T_{n}F\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho F
(Lemma
7.12
). The definition of the maps
\alpha
,
\gamma
and
\delta
should be self evident.

Our goal is to prove that the composition
\beta\alpha
is an equivalence. We will prove that
\gamma\beta\alpha
and
\delta\gamma
are equivalences. This implies that each one of the maps
\beta\alpha
,
\gamma
, and
\delta
is an equivalence.

Let us start with the map

\gamma\beta\alpha\colon\nat_{{\textbf{C}}_{1}}(\rho F,G)\to\nat_{{\textbf{C}}_{1}}(\rho F,\rho\widetilde{T}_{n}G).

It is straightforward to check that the map is induced by the natural transformation
G\to\rho\widetilde{T}_{n}G
, which is the same as the map
G\to T_{n}G
. Since
G
is
n
-excisive, this map is an equivalence, and therefore
\gamma\beta\alpha
is an equivalence.

To prove that
\delta\gamma
is an equivalence, we extend this chain of maps as follows

\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,\widetilde{T}_{n}G)\xrightarrow{\gamma}\nat_{{\textbf{C}}_{1}}(\rho F,\rho\widetilde{T}_{n}G)\xrightarrow{\delta}\\
\to\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho F,\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho\widetilde{T}_{n}G)\xrightarrow{\zeta}\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho\widetilde{T}_{n}G)

Here the map
\zeta
is induced by the map
F\to T_{n}F\simeq\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho F
. Once again, it is straightforward to check that the composition

\zeta\delta\gamma\colon\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,\widetilde{T}_{n}G)\to\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho\widetilde{T}_{n}G)

is induced by the map
\widetilde{T}_{n}G\to T_{n}\widetilde{T}_{n}G\simeq\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho\widetilde{T}_{n}G
. This map is an equivalence, because
\widetilde{T}_{n}G
is an
n
-excisive functor, by Lemma
7.13
. We have shown that
\zeta\delta\gamma
is an equivalence, and it remains to show that
\zeta
is an equivalence, which would imply that
\delta\gamma
is an equivalence. By definition,
\zeta
is induced by the map
F\to T_{n}F\simeq\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho F
. Notice again that the functor
\widetilde{T}_{n}\rho\widetilde{T}_{n}G\simeq\widetilde{T}_{n}G
is
n
-excisive. It remains to prove the following claim: Let
Q\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
be
n
-excisive. Then the induced map
\nat(T_{n}F,Q)\to\nat(F,Q)
is an equivalence. This follows by considering the maps
F\to T_{n}F\to P_{n}F
, where
P_{n}
is an infinite iteration of
T_{n}
. The functor
P_{n}
can be thought of as a functor
P_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})\to\exc_{n}({\textbf{C}},{\textbf{D}})
that is left adjoint to the inclusion
[
28
, Theorem 6.1.1.10]
. This means that the map
\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(P_{n}F,Q)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,Q)
is an equivalence. The map
\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(P_{n}F,Q)\to\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(T_{n}F,Q)
is an equivalence as well, because the map
T_{n}F\to P_{n}F
is equivalent to the map
T_{n}F\to P_{n}T_{n}F
. It follows that the map
\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(T_{n}F,Q)\to\nat_{{\textbf{C}}}(F,Q)
is indeed an equivalence.
∎

Corollary 7.17
.

Let
D
be a differentiable category. Restriction induces an equivalence of categories.

\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}}).

The inverse equivalence is given by right Kan extension.

8.
The main theorems

Putting all the previous results together, we get the following theorem

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
D
be a stable
\infty
-category with small colimits. For each
n\geq 0
there is a diagram of categories and functors, commuting up to a natural equivalence, where all functors are equivalences of categories, and parallel arrows are inverses of each other.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\poly_{n}(\langle S^{1}\rangle,{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\exc_{n}({\textbf{sGr}},{\textbf{D}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\operatorname{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\operatorname{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\pi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

Here
\rho_{\operatorname{B}}
denotes restriction along the functor
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{sGr}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
, or its restriction
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to\langle S^{1}\rangle
, which we denote with the same letter
\operatorname{B}
.
\rho_{\pi_{1}}
denotes restriction along the functor
\pi_{1}\colon\langle S^{1}\rangle\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
. Other arrows marked
\rho
denote restriction along the obvious inclusion functor. The arrows marked
\mathrm{L}
denote left Kan extension, or more precisely the restriction of left Kan extension to the category of polynomial functors. Similarly
\mathrm{R}
denotes right Kan extension.

Proof.

That the pair of horizontal arrows at the bottom of the diagram are inverse equivalences follows Proposition
6.8
and the fact that left Kan extension is the inverse functor of restriction in this case. The arrows marked
\rho_{\operatorname{B}}
are equivalences because the functors
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\langle S^{1}\rangle
and
\operatorname{B}\colon{\textbf{sGr}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}
are both equivalences of categories (the former was discussed in Section
3
, the latter is Lemma
3.7
). The pair of maps at the top right of the diagram are inverse equivalences by Corollary
7.17
.

It is clear that the diagram consisting of restriction functors commutes. Since the two vertical restriction functors marked
\rho_{\operatorname{B}}
are equivalences, and the functor marked
\rho
at the bottom of the diagram is an equivalence, it follows that the functor marked
\rho
at the top left of the diagram is an equivalence.
∎

Reinterpretation in terms of operad (co)modules

Recall that
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
denotes the category of pointed sets with at most
n
non-basepoint elements. Let
D
be a stable
\infty
-category. Let
\rho_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})
be the restriction functor.
and
\mathrm{L}_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
the left Kan extension.
The following lemma is well-known.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
D
be a stable
\infty
-category with small colimits. The restriction functor

\rho_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})

restricts to an equivalence, which we still denote by
\rho_{n}

\rho_{n}\colon\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}}).

The inverse equivalence is given by left Kan extension
\mathrm{L}_{n}
.

A proof is given in
[
3
]
Section 3 for functors from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
to
Spectra
in the setting of model categories. A more general
\infty
-categorical statement appears as
[
28
, Corollary 6.1.5.7]
(see also Corollary 6.1.5.2 for the special case of functors from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
to
Spectra
) with the additional hypothesis that
D
is presentable. Lemma
8.2
is less general than
[
28
, Corollary 6.1.5.7]
in that we only consider domain category
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
, but it is more general in that we do not assume that
D
is presentable, but only cocomplete stable. This is desirable, because it implies, for example, that Lemma
8.2
applies to contravariant functors from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
to
Spectra
, even though the category
{{\textbf{Spectra}}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
is not presentable.

Sketch of proof of Lemma
8.2
.

To begin with, for an object
d\in{\textbf{D}}
the functor
X\mapsto d\otimes X^{m}
is
m
-excisive. This is essentially
[
18
, Example 3.5]
. Any functor in the image of the left Kan extension

\mathrm{L}_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})

can be written as a colimit of functors of the form
d\otimes X^{m}
with
m\leq n
. This follows from the coend formula for left Kan extension, about which one can read for example in
[
20
]
. It follows that the image of
\mathrm{L}_{n}
lies in
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
. It follows that
\mathrm{L}_{n}
factors as a composition

\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{L}_{n}^{0}}\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\hookrightarrow\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}}).

Since
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
is a full subcategory of
\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
, we have an adjunction
\mathrm{L}_{n}^{0}\dashv\rho_{n}
between
\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})
and
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
. We claim that for every
F\in\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D)}}
the map
F\to\rho_{n}\mathrm{L}F
is an equivalence, and for every
G\in\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
the natural map
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}G\to G
is an equivalence. The first claim holds because
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
is a full subcategory of
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
.
We need to prove the second claim. Thus we need to show that for every CW complex
X
, and every
n
-excisive functor
G\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{D}}
the natural map
\epsilon_{X}\colon\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}G(X)\to G(X)
is an equivalence. Note that
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}G(X)
is an
n
-excisive functor. We argue that
\epsilon_{X}
is an equivalence for all CW complexes
X
, using the following steps.

(1)

the map
\epsilon_{X}
is an equivalence when
X\in{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
, because
\rho_{n}\mathrm{L}_{n}
is equivalent to the identity.

(2)

It follows that
\epsilon_{X}
is an equivalence for all
X\in{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}
. To see this, suppose
m>n
and assume by induction that
\epsilon_{X}
is an equivalence whenever
X
is a pointed set with fewer than
m
nonbasepoint elements. Since
m>n
, one can construct a strongly homotopy cocartesian diagram
\chi\colon\mathrm{P}(n+1)\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}
such that
\chi(\underline{n+1})
is a set with
m
elements and for every proper subset
U\subsetneq\underline{n+1}
,
\chi(U)
has fewer than
m
elements. It follows by induction hypothesis that whenever
U\subsetneq\underline{n+1}

\epsilon_{\chi(U)}
is an equivalence, and we want to show that
\epsilon_{\chi(\underline{n+1)}}
is an equivalence. Since
G
and
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}G
are both
n
-excisive,
\epsilon_{\chi}\colon\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}G\chi\to G\chi
is a map of cocartesian cubical diagrams (this is where we use that
D
is stable). This map is an equivalence when evaluated at a proper subset
U\subsetneq\underline{n+1}
, and thus it is an equivalence at
\underline{n+1}
. This means that
\epsilon_{\underline{m}}\colon\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}G(m_{+})\to G(m_{+})
is an equivalence.

(3)

We have shown that
\epsilon_{X}
is an equivalence when
X
is a zero-dimensional complex. Suppose by induction that
1\leq d
and
\epsilon_{X}
is an equivalence when
X
is a finite complex of dimension less than
d
. Now suppose
X
is a a finite CW complex of dimension
d
, with
1\leq k
cells of dimension
d
, and
\epsilon_{X}
is an equivalence whenever
X
is a finite
d
-dimensional complex with fewer than
k
cells in dimension
d
. By punching
n+1
holes in a top-dimensional cell of
X
we can present
X
as the pushout of a strongly cocartesian
n+1
-dimensional diagram where all the other terms have fewer top-dimensional cells than
X
. Using a similar argument to the one in the previous step, we conclude that
\epsilon_{X}
is an equivalence.

(4)

We have shown that
\epsilon_{X}
is an equivalence for all finite CW complexes
X
. We assumed that
G
commutes with filtered colimits, as part of our definition of
n
-excisive functors. The functor
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}G
also commutes with filtered colimits, because objects of
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
are compact objects in
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
. It follows that
\epsilon_{X}\colon\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}G(X)\to G(X)
is an equivalence for all CW complexes
X
.

We have shown that
\mathrm{L}_{n}
and
\rho_{n}
induce inverse equivalences between
\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})
and
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
.
∎

Furthermore, let
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
be the category of (unpointed) finite sets of cardinality at most
n
and epimorphisms between them. There is a rather well-known “Morita” equivalence between categories

\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})\simeq\fun({\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}}).

The equivalence is induced by a “
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
-
Epi
-bimodule”, i.e., a bifunctor

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\textbf{S}}_{*}\times{\textbf{Epi}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}&\to&{\textbf{S}}_{*}\\
(X,i)&\mapsto&X^{\wedge i}\end{array}

Note that for each
n
we can restrict this to a
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
-
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
-bimodule. This bimodule induces a pair of adjoint functors

-\otimes_{i}X^{\wedge i}:\hbox to212.54pt{\vbox to18.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 106.27077pt\lower-9.12332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
