The following statement was discovered by Pirashvili for
1
-categories when
D
is an abelian category
[
29
]
. It was proved by Helmstutler (with technical hypotheses) for stable model categories
[
21
]
, and then by Walde for
\infty
-categories in the generality stated here
[
37
, Section 4.2]

Proposition 8.3
.

Suppose
D
is an idempotent complete additive
\infty
-category. The adjunction above is an equivalence of categories for any
n\leq\infty
.

Functors from
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
to
D
are, by definition
n
-truncated right comodules over the the commutative operad
\mathtt{Com}
with values in
D

[
3
]
. So we also denote the category
\fun({\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})
by
\mathtt{Com}-{\textbf{comod}}_{\leq n}({\textbf{D}})
. Combining Theorem
8.1
, Lemma
8.2
and Proposition
8.3
, we obtain the following corollary

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
D
be an
\infty
-category with small colimits. There are equivalences of categories

\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{D}})\xleftarrow[\rho_{\operatorname{B}}]{\simeq}\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathtt{Com}\!-\!{\textbf{comod}}_{\leq n}({\textbf{D}}).

Bifunctors

Theorem
8.1
and Corollary
8.4
apply when
{\textbf{D}}={\textbf{Spectra}}
or
Ch
, and also when
{\textbf{D}}={{\textbf{Spectra}}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
or
{{\textbf{Ch}}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
. To put it another way, our main results apply both to covariant and contravariant polynomial functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
Ch
or
Spectra
. In the second half of the paper we focus on applications to covariant functors. But we hope that our approach will prove useful for contravariant functors as well. Furthermore, recently there has been interest in (Ext groups between) bifunctors, i.e., functors
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\times{{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
. We will prove a version of Theorem
8.1
for bifunctors. For this, we need to review the notions of polynomial/excisive functors of several variables (we will content ourselves with two)

Definition 8.5
.

Let
{\textbf{C}}_{1},{\textbf{C}}_{2},{\textbf{D}}
be categories. A functor
F\colon{\textbf{C}}_{1}\times{\textbf{C}}_{2}\to{\textbf{D}}
is called
(n_{1},n_{2})
-polynomial if for every object
X
of

{\textbf{C}}_{1}
the functor
F(X,-)\colon{\textbf{C}}_{2}\to{\textbf{D}}
is polynomial of degree
n_{2}
, and for every
Y
in
{\textbf{C}}_{2}
the functor
F(-,Y)\colon{\textbf{C}}_{1}\to{\textbf{D}}
is polynomial of degree
n_{1}
.
In an analogous way we define
(n_{1},n_{2})
-excisive functors. Let
\poly_{(n_{1},n_{2})}({\textbf{C}}_{1}\times{\textbf{C}}_{2},{\textbf{D}})
denote the category of
(n_{1},n_{2})
-polynomial functors, and similarly let
\exc_{(n_{1},n_{2})}({\textbf{C}}_{1}\times{\textbf{C}}_{2},{\textbf{D}})
denote the category of
(n_{1},n_{2})
-excisive functors.

There are obvious isomorphisms of categories

\poly_{(n_{1},n_{2})}({\textbf{C}}_{1}\times{\textbf{C}}_{2},{\textbf{D}})\cong\poly_{n_{1}}({\textbf{C}}_{1},\poly_{n_{2}}({\textbf{C}}_{2},{\textbf{D}}))

and

\exc_{(n_{1},n_{2})}({\textbf{C}}_{1}\times{\textbf{C}}_{2},{\textbf{D}})\cong\exc_{n_{1}}({\textbf{C}}_{1},\exc_{n_{2}}({\textbf{C}}_{2},{\textbf{D}})).

Now we can prove a bifunctor version of Theorem
8.1

Proposition 8.6
.

Suppose
D
is an
\infty
-category that has small limits and colimits. The classifying space functor
\operatorname{B}\times{\operatorname{B}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\times{{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}\times{{\textbf{S}}_{*}}^{\!\!\!{\mathrm{op}}}
induces an equivalence, for every pair
(n_{1},n_{2})
of natural numbers

\exc_{(n_{1},n_{2})}({\textbf{S}}_{*}\times{{\textbf{S}}_{*}}^{\!\!\!{\mathrm{op}}},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow[\rho_{\operatorname{B}\times{\operatorname{B}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}}]{\simeq}\poly_{(n_{1},n_{2})}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\times{{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}}^{{\mathrm{op}}},{\textbf{D}}).

Proof.

It is clear that the restriction functor
\rho_{\operatorname{B}\times{\operatorname{B}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}}
is equivalent to the following composition, where each factor is an equivalence either by adjunction, or by an application of Theorem
8.1

\exc_{(n_{1},n_{2})}({\textbf{S}}_{*}\times{{\textbf{S}}_{*}}^{\!\!\!{\mathrm{op}}},{\textbf{D}})\to\exc_{n_{1}}({\textbf{S}}_{*},\exc_{n_{2}}({{\textbf{S}}_{*}}^{\!\!\!{\mathrm{op}}},{\textbf{D}}))\to\\
\to\exc_{n_{1}}({\textbf{S}}_{*},\poly_{n_{2}}({{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}}^{{\mathrm{op}}},{\textbf{D}}))\to\poly_{n_{1}}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},\poly_{n_{2}}({{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}}^{{\mathrm{op}}},{\textbf{D}}))\to\\
\to\poly_{(n_{1},n_{2})}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\times{{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}}^{{\mathrm{op}}},{\textbf{D}}).

∎

When the target category is abelian

Next, we want to apply Theorem
8.1
to polynomial functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to an abelian category. As before, we let
A
denote an abelian
1
-category with enough projectives, and a set of compact projective generators. Let
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
be the
\infty
-category of chain complexes over
A
.
We have the following result about polynomial functors from
sFr
to
A
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
A
and
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
be as above. Suppose
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{A}}
is a polynomial functor of degree
n
. Then there exists an
n
-excisive functor
\widehat{F}\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
, unique up to equivalence, for which there is an isomorphism of functors from
\langle S^{1}\rangle
to
A

\operatorname{H}_{0}\widehat{F}|_{\langle S^{1}\rangle}\cong F\circ\pi_{1},

and
\operatorname{H}_{i}\widehat{F}|_{\langle S^{1}\rangle}\cong 0
for
i\neq 0
.

Furthermore if
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
is another polynomial functor, then there is an isomorphism of graded groups

\ext^{*}_{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{A}})}(F,G)\cong\pi_{-*}\left(\spectralNat\left(\widehat{F},\widehat{G}\right)\right)

Proof.

As we mentioned already, by
[
28
, Proposition 1.3.2.19]
there is an equivalence of categories
{\textbf{A}}\simeq{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}^{\heartsuit}
, where
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}^{\heartsuit}
is the heart of
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
. More concretely,
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}^{\heartsuit}
is the full subcategory of
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
consisting of chain complexes whose homology is concentrated in degree zero. Thus we have a canonical (up to equivalence) fully faithful “inclusion” functor
{\textbf{A}}\hookrightarrow{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
. It is clear that the inclusion functor preserves coproducts (as mentioned already in Example
5.12
).

Let
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{A}}
be a polynomial functor of degree
n
. Let
F^{\prime}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
be the post-composition of
F
with the inclusion functor. Note that
F^{\prime}
is characterised by the property that there is an isomorphism
\operatorname{H}_{0}F^{\prime}\cong F
, and
\operatorname{H}_{n}F^{\prime}\cong 0
for
n\neq 0
. Since the inclusion preserves coproducts,
F^{\prime}
is polynomial of degree
n
by Lemma
5.11
(see also Example
5.12
). By Theorem
8.1
there are equivalences of categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\poly_{n}(\langle S^{1}\rangle,{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\pi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

It follows that there is an essentially unique
n
-excicive functor
\widehat{F}\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
such that there is an equivalence
\widehat{F}|_{\langle S^{1}\rangle}\simeq F^{\prime}\circ\pi_{1}
. Since by construction
F^{\prime}
takes values in
{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}^{\heartsuit}
, it follows that
\widehat{F}
is determined by the requirement
\operatorname{H}_{*}\widehat{F}|_{\langle S^{1}\rangle}\cong\operatorname{H}_{*}F^{\prime}\circ\pi_{1}
, which is equivalent to saying that
\operatorname{H}_{0}\widehat{F}|_{\langle S^{1}\rangle}\cong F\circ\pi_{1}
and
\operatorname{H}_{i}\widehat{F}|_{\langle S^{1}\rangle}\cong 0
for
i\neq 0
. This proves the first half of the theorem.

Let
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{A}}
be another polynomial functor of degree
n
. Let
G^{\prime}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
be the post-composition of
G
with the inclusion functor. By the standard connection between homological algebra and stable homotopy theory (see Lemma
2.4
and the discussion preceding it), there is a natural isomorphism

\ext^{*}_{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{A}})}(F,G)\simeq\pi_{-*}\left(\spectralNat\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime}\right)\right).

Here by
\spectralNat\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime}\right)
we mean the spectral mapping object from
F^{\prime}
to
G^{\prime}
in the full category of functors
\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{\textbf{A}})
. But in fact
F^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
are objects of the full subcategory
\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{\textbf{A}})
. It follows that there is an isomorphism of graded groups

\pi_{*}\left(\spectralNat\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime}\right)\right)\cong\pi_{*}\left(\spectralmaps\hphantom{.}_{\!\!\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{\textbf{A}})}\!\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime}\right)\right).

By Theorem
8.1
there is an equivalence of categories

\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})\simeq\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{\textbf{A}})

induced by the functor
\operatorname{B}{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}
, and by definition
\widehat{F}
and
\widehat{G}
are the images of
F^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
under the inverse of this equivalence. It follows that there is a natural isomorphism

\pi_{*}\left(\spectralmaps\hphantom{.}_{\!\!\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}}_{\textbf{A}})}\!\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime}\right)\right)\cong\pi_{*}\left(\spectralmaps\hphantom{.}_{\!\!\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}}_{\textbf{A}})}\!\left(\widehat{F},\widehat{G}\right)\right).

Finally, since
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})
is a full subcategory of
\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}})
, we have an isomorphism

\pi_{*}\left(\spectralmaps\hphantom{.}_{\!\!\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}}_{\textbf{A}})}\!\left(\widehat{F},\widehat{G}\right)\right)\cong\pi_{*}\left(\spectralNat\left(\widehat{F},\widehat{G}\right)\right).

Putting all these isomorphisms together, we obtain the isomorphism

\ext^{*}_{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{A}})}(F,G)\simeq\pi_{-*}\left(\spectralNat\left(\widehat{F},\widehat{G}\right)\right).

This is what we wanted to prove.
∎

Part II
Calculations

9.
Functors that factor through abelianization

Unlike in much of the first part, in this section “categories” and “functors” are meant in the
1
-categorical sense. Let
A
be an abelian category, and let
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{A}}
be a polynomial functor. We continue to denote by
\widehat{F}\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}_{{\textbf{A}}}
the extension of
F
, as characterized by Theorem
8.7
. In this section we calculate
\widehat{F}
for a number of commonly occurring functors
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
. In particular, we will prove the correctness of Table
1
. This will enable us to use Theorem
8.7
to perform calculations. We start with a fundamental example, on which many subsequent calculations will build. Recall that
\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
is the
n
-fold tensor power of abelianization.

Lemma 9.1
.

There is a natural equivalence

\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)=\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}].

Proof.

By Theorem
8.1
there is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})

which is induced by restriction along the classifying space functor. By definition,
\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}
is the preimage of
\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
under this equivalence. This means that we have to show that the following two functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
Ch
are equivalent: one is
G\mapsto(G^{\mathrm{ab}})^{\otimes n}
and the other is
G\mapsto\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\operatorname{B}G^{\wedge n}].
We claim that both functors take value in the heart of
Ch
, i.e., in chain complexes whose homology is concentrated in degree zero. For the functor
\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
this holds by definition. Regarding the second functor, we know that
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\operatorname{B}G^{\wedge n}]
is a simplicial abelian group whose homotopy groups are naturally isomorphic to the reduced homology group of
\operatorname{B}G^{\wedge n}
. Note that if
G\in{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
, then
\operatorname{B}G
is equivalent to a finite wedge of circles. It follows that the reduced homology of
\operatorname{B}G^{\wedge n}
is concentrated in degree
n
. It follows that
\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\operatorname{B}G^{\wedge n}]
is a chain complex whose homology is concentrated in degree zero.

To prove that two functors
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
that take values in
{\textbf{Ch}}^{\heartsuit}
are equivalent, it is enough to show that they become isomorphic after applying
\pi_{0}/\operatorname{H}_{0}
, as
1
-functors
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
. To this end, we have isomorphisms, natural in
G

\pi_{0}(\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\operatorname{B}G^{\wedge n}])\cong\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\operatorname{B}G^{\wedge n}])\cong\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{n}(\operatorname{B}G^{\wedge n})\cong[\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{1}(\operatorname{B}G)]^{\otimes n}\cong(G^{\mathrm{ab}})^{\otimes n}.

∎

Remark 9.2
.

The functor
\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
has a natural action of
\Sigma_{n}
, permuting the factors. This action will be important when we consider applications to stable cohomology of automorphisms of free groups. It follows that
\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ{\textbf{Ab}}}
has a corresponding action of
\Sigma_{n}
. It is clear that the action of
\Sigma_{n}
on
\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}]
corresponding to the action on
\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
is the action by conjugation, where
\Sigma_{n}
acts both on
\Omega^{n}
and on
X^{\wedge n}
by permuting coordinates. This is the correct action because with it the isomorphism of abelian groups

\pi_{0}(\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\operatorname{B}G^{\wedge n}])\cong(G^{\mathrm{ab}})^{\otimes n}

respects the action of
\Sigma_{n}
.

The next two examples concern the functors
\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
and
\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
. Let us review these functors. For an abelian group
A
,
\Lambda^{n}(A)
is defined to be the quotient of
A^{\otimes n}
by the subgroup generated by all pure tensors
a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}
where at least two of the factors are the same. At least when
A
is a free abelian group, there is an equivalent definition of
\Lambda^{n}(A)
as the group of skew-symmetric tensors. Let
A^{\otimes n}_{\pm}
denote the
n
-fold tensor power of
A
, equipped with the
sign action
of
\Sigma_{n}
. Let
(A^{\otimes n}_{\pm})^{\Sigma_{n}}
denote the invariants of the sign action. There is a well-known homomorphism

(23)

\Lambda^{n}(A)\to{A^{\otimes n}_{\pm}}^{\Sigma_{n}}

which is defined on pure tensors by the formula

a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}}\operatorname{sign}(\sigma)a_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{\sigma(n)}.

The following lemma is elementary and well-known.

Lemma 9.3
.

The homomorphism (
23
) is well-defined and natural in
A
. When
A
is a free abelian group it is an isomorphism.

Subsequently
A
will be
\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{1}(X)
, where
X
is a finite wedge of circles, so
A
will be a finitely generated free abelian group. We are therefore justified in identifying the group
{A^{\otimes n}_{\pm}}^{\Sigma_{n}}
with
\Lambda^{n}(A)
. Similarly,
\Gamma^{n}(A)
is identified with
{A^{\otimes n}}^{\Sigma_{n}}
- the invariants of the unsigned action of
\Sigma_{n}
on
A^{\otimes n}
.

Proposition 9.4
.

(1)

If
F(G)=\Lambda^{n}(G^{\mathrm{ab}})
then
\widehat{F}(X)=\Sigma^{-n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]

(2)

If
F(G)=\Gamma^{n}(G^{\mathrm{ab}})
then
\widehat{F}(X)=\Sigma^{-2n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(SX)^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]

Proof.

(
1
) Using the uniqueness part of Theorem
8.7
, it is not hard to see that our task amounts to showing that when
X
is restricted to the category
\langle S^{1}\rangle
of finite wedges of circles, there is an isomorphism of graded groups, natural in
X

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}})\cong\Lambda^{n}(\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X)).

We will make use of the homology transfer for strict orbits. Let
K
be a pointed regular CW-complex with a cellular action of a finite group
G
. It is well-known that there is a natural transfer homomorphism

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X/G)\to\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X)^{G}.

One of the defining properties of the transfer homomorphism is that the composition

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X)\to\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X/G)\to\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X)^{G}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X)

is equal to
\sum_{g\in G}g_{*}

[
8
, Section III.2]
.

When
X
is a wedge of circles,
X^{\wedge n}
has the structure of a regular CW-complex with an action of
\Sigma_{n}
. Let us fix such an
X
. Then
\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X)
is a finitely generated free abelian group concentrated in degree 1, and
\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X^{\wedge n})
is concentrated in degree
n
. In degree
n

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X^{\wedge n})
is canonically isomorphic, as a group with an action of
\Sigma_{n}
, to
\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{1}(X)^{\otimes n}_{\pm}
. It follows that the transfer induces a homomorphism

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X^{\wedge n})^{\Sigma_{n}}.

The target of this homomorphism is, by Lemma
9.3
, naturally isomorphic to
\Lambda^{n}(\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X))
. So our goal is to show that the homomorphism above is an isomorphism, whenever
X
is a wedge of circles.

For the purpose of this proof let us denote
F_{n}(X)=\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}})
and
G_{n}(X)=\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(X^{\wedge n})^{\Sigma_{n}}
. We have a natural transformation
F_{n}\to G_{n}
, and we want to show that it is an isomorphism for all
n>0
when evaluated on a finite wedge of circles.

Next we claim that
F_{n}
and
G_{n}
satisfy the same recursive formula. Namely, we claim that there is a natural isomorphism

(24)

F_{n}(X\vee Y)\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}F_{i}(X)\otimes F_{n-i}(Y)

and
G_{n}
satisfies an exactly analogous formula. Furthermore, the natural transformation
F_{n}\to G_{n}
respects this decomposition. All this follows easily from the “binomial formula” for
(X\vee Y)^{\wedge n}
, and the naturality of the transfer. By induction on the number of circles, it follows that it is enough to show that the homomorphism
F_{n}(S^{1})\to G_{n}(S^{1})
is an isomorphism for all
n
. When
n=1
we get the transfer map for
\Sigma_{1}
, i.e., the trivial group, which certainly is an isomorphism. For
n>1
, it is well-known that the orbit space
S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
is contractible, so
F_{n}(S^{1})=0
, and it is easy to see that
G_{n}(S^{1})=0
as well. So in this case the homomorphism
F_{n}(S^{1})\to G_{n}(S^{1})
can only be an isomorphism.

(
2
) Using the uniqueness part of Theorem
8.7
once again, we see that our task is to prove that the transfer induces an isomorphism, whenever
X
is a finite wedge of circles.

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}((SX)^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}((SX)^{\wedge n})^{\Sigma_{n}}.

This is equivalent to showing that the tranfer induces an isomorphism, whenever
Y
is a finite wedge sum of copies of
S^{2}

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(Y^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(Y^{\wedge n})^{\Sigma_{n}}.

Once again, it is easy to see that both the source and the target of this homomorphism satisfy the same recursive relation as in (
24
). It follows that it is enough to prove that this is an isomorphism when
Y=S^{2}
. In this case we obtain a homomorphism of the following form

(25)

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(S^{2n}_{\Sigma_{n}})\to\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(S^{2n})^{\Sigma_{n}}.

We want to show that the transfer homomorphism (
25
) is an isomorphism. It is well-known that the orbit space
S^{2n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
is in fact homeomorphic to
S^{2n}
. It follows that the source and the target of (
25
) are in fact isomorphic graded abelian groups, and are both isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
, concentrated in degree
2n
. Furthermore, the quotient map
S^{2n}\to S^{2n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
induces multiplication by
n!
on
\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{2n}
. To see this, note that it is a map between oriented manifolds of dimension
2n
, and it restricts to an oriented
n!
-fold covering over an open subset of
S^{2n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
, so it has degree
n!
.

On the other hand, the following composition of homomorphisms is also multiplication by
n!

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(S^{2n})\to\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(S^{2n}_{\Sigma_{n}})\to\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(S^{2n})^{\Sigma_{n}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(S^{2n}).

Indeed, by the defining property of the transfer map, the composition is equal to
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}}\sigma_{*}
. But
\Sigma_{n}
acts trivially on
\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(S^{2n})
, so the composition is just multiplication by
n!
. Since the first homomorphism, and the composition are both multiplication by
n!
, it follows that the transfer homomorphism
\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(S^{2n}_{\Sigma_{n}})\to\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(S^{2n})^{\Sigma_{n}}
is an isomorphism.
∎

9.1.
A general formula

So far we calculated
\widehat{F}
by guessing it. But it is good to also have a general procedure for calculating it. We will now develop a kind of formula for
\widehat{F}
in case when
F
factors through abelianization.

Let
F
be a polynomial functor from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
Ab
. Recall that
\mathrm{ab}
denotes the abelianization functor from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to the category of finitely generated free abelian groups. We say that a functor
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
factors through abelianization if there is a functor
F^{\mathrm{ab}}
from the category of finitely generated free abelian groups to abelian groups, such that there is an isomorphism of functors
F\cong F^{\mathrm{ab}}\circ\mathrm{ab}
.

Remark 9.5
.

It is easy to see that if a factorization
F\cong F^{\mathrm{ab}}\circ\mathrm{ab}
exists then
F^{\mathrm{ab}}
is unique up to isomorphism. This is so because the abelianization functor (from free groups to free abelian groups) is full and essentially surjective on objects. Furthermore, if
F
is polynomial of degree
n
then so is
F^{\mathrm{ab}}
, since the abelianization functor preserves coproducts.

Suppose
X
is a pointed (simplicial) set. Normally we interpret
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[-]
as an
\infty
-categorical functor, but now we need to commit ourselves to a strict (i.e., 1-categorical) model for this functor. So let us define
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[-]
to be a
1
-categorical functor from pointed (simplicial) sets to (simplicial) abelian groups by the formula
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X]:=\mathbb{Z}[X]/\mathbb{Z}[*]
. If
K
is another pointed finite simplicial set, in this section we let
\pointedmaps(K,\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X])
denote the cosimplicial simplicial abelian group which in bi-degree
([i],[j])
is given by
\hom(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[K_{i}],\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X_{j}])
. Again, note that in this section we deviate slightly from our customary notation. Normally
\pointedmaps(K,\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X])
would denote the cotensoring of the chain complex
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X]
by
K
in the
\infty
-category
Ch
, but here we use the
1
-categorical model for it as a cosimplicial-simplicial group. Since we assume that
K
is finite, it follows that
\pointedmaps(K,\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X])
is free abelian in each bidegree. If
S^{1}
is a simplicial circle, we use the notation
\Omega\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X]:=\pointedmaps(S^{1},\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X])
.

Definition 9.6
.

Suppose
F^{\mathrm{ab}}
is a functor from finitely generated free abelian groups to abelian groups. Then we denote by
F^{\mathrm{ab}}\left(\pointedmaps(K,\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X])\right)
the chain complex obtained in the following three steps

(1)

apply
F^{\mathrm{ab}}
levelwise to the cosimplicial simplicial abelian group
\pointedmaps(K,\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X])
, to get another cosimplicial simplicial abelian group

(2)

apply the Dold-Kan normalized chains functor in both directions to the cosimplicial simplicial abelian group obtained in step (
1
), to get a second quadrant bicomplex,

(3)

take the total complex of the bicomplex obtained in step (
2
).

Now we are ready to give our formula for
\widehat{F}

Theorem 9.7
.

Let
F\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
be a polynomial functor. Suppose that
F
factors through abelianization, so there is an isomorphism of functors
F\cong F^{\mathrm{ab}}\circ\mathrm{ab}
. Then on the category of pointed simplicial sets, there is an equivalence, natural in
X

\widehat{F}(X)\simeq F^{\mathrm{ab}}\left(\Omega\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X]\right).

Before proving the theorem, we need to review the simplicial model for the circle that we will use. Recall that
\Delta
is the category with objects
[0],[1],\ldots,[n],\ldots
. Here
[n]=\{0,\ldots,n\}
, and the morphisms in
\Delta
are order-preserving functions between these sets. The standard simplicial model for the circle is the following contravariant functor
\Delta^{{\mathrm{op}}}\to{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}
:

[n]\mapsto\Delta([n],[1])/\{c_{0},c_{1}\}

where
c_{0},c_{1}
denote the two constant maps from
[n]
to
[1]
. We view it as a pointed simplicial set. Thus the following simplicial abelian group is a model for the reduced simplicial chains on
S^{1}

[n]\mapsto\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Delta([n],[1])/\{c_{0},c_{1}\}]

where
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[-]
denotes the pointed free abelian group functor.

On the other hand, there is a simplicial abelian group

[n]\mapsto\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}/\mathbb{Z}

where
\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}:=\mathbb{Z}^{[n]}
, and the group is quotiened by the diagonal copy of
\mathbb{Z}
, that is by the group of constant function from
[n]
to
\mathbb{Z}
. We denote this simplicial abelian group by
\mathbb{Z}^{\bullet+1}/\mathbb{Z}
.

The inclusion of sets
[1]\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
induces a function
\Delta([n],[1])\to\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
, which extends to a group homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}[\Delta([n],[1])]\to\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
. It is clear that it takes constant functions to constant functions, to it induces a homomorphism

\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Delta([n],[1])/\{c_{0},c_{1}\}]\to\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}/\mathbb{Z}.

This homomorphism is obviously natural in
[n]
, and it is easy to check that it is an isomorphism (both the source and the target are isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
). We have proved the following lemma

Lemma 9.8
.

The simplicial abelian group
\mathbb{Z}^{\bullet+1}/\mathbb{Z}
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{1}_{\bullet}]
, where
S^{1}_{\bullet}
is the standard simplicial circle.

Proof of Theorem
9.7
.

By Theorem
C
, we know the value of
\widehat{F}
on wedges of circles. The key step is to find the value of
\widehat{F}
on pointed finite sets. From there, one can evaluate
\widehat{F}
on general spaces by left Kan extension from
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}
to
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
, which, if we take spaces to mean simplicial sets, is equivalent to evaluating
\widehat{F}
level-wise and then taking geometric realization.

Let
X
be a pointed finite set. By Corollary
7.15
, we can calculate

\widehat{F}(X)
as
\widetilde{T}_{\infty}\widehat{F}(X).

Here
\widetilde{T}_{\infty}
is essentially Goodwillie’s operator
T_{\infty}
defined in
[
19
]
, and we will now omit
\sim
from the notation. The reason
T_{\infty}\widehat{F}(X)
is defined when
X
is a pointed finite set is that
T_{\infty}\widehat{F}(X)
is a homotopy limit of objects of the form
F(\pi_{1}(X*U))
, where
U
is a non-empty finite set. Here
X*U
denotes the pointed joint of
X
and
U
, i.e. the mapping cone of the map
X\wedge U_{+}\to X
. Note that whenever
X
is a pointed set and
U
is a non-empty set,
X*U
is homotopy equivalent to a wedge of circles, and thus there is a natural equivalence
\widehat{F}(X*U)\simeq F(\pi_{1}(X*U)).

We will use a cosimplicial model for
T_{\infty}\widehat{F}(X)
. For all
X
,
T_{\infty}\widehat{F}(X)
is equivalent to the totalization of the following cosimplicial object

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{F}(X*[0])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{F}(X*[1])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{F}(X*[2])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

This cosimplicial model for
T_{\infty}
appeared in a preprint version of
[
19
]
, where it was credited to Waldhausen. The cosimplicial model did not make it into the published version of
[
19
]
. There is an account of it in
[
15
]
.

Since we assume
X
is a pointed finite set,
X*[n]
is equivalent to a wedge of circles for every
n
. By the defining property of
\widehat{F}
given in Theorem
C
, there is a natural equivalence
\widehat{F}(X*[n])\simeq F(\pi_{1}(X*[n]))
. We assume that
F
factors as
F^{\mathrm{ab}}\circ\mathrm{ab}
, so there is furthere a natural equivalence
\widehat{F}(X*[n])\simeq F^{\mathrm{ab}}(\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{1}(X*[n]))
. Recall that
X*[n]
is the homotopy cofiber of the map
X\wedge[n]_{+}\to X
. It follows that there are isomorphisms, natural in
X
and
[n]

\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{1}(X*[n]))\cong\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X]\otimes\ker\left(\bigoplus_{n+1}\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}\right)\cong\hom(\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}/\mathbb{Z},\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X]).

It follows
\widehat{F}(X)
is the totalisation of the cosimplicial object obtained by applying
F^{\mathrm{ab}}
levelwise to
\hom(\mathbb{Z}^{\bullet+1}/\mathbb{Z},\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X])
. By Lemma
9.8
this cosimplicial abelian group is isomorphic to
\hom(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{1}_{\bullet}],\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X])\cong\Omega\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X].

We have proved the theorem in case when
X
is a pointed finite set. Since
\widehat{F}
is an excisive functor, it preserves geometric realizations (Lemma
6.5
). Therefore, if
X
is a pointed finite simplicial set, one can evaluate
\widehat{F}
on
X
by applying it level-wise and then taking geometric realization. The geometric realization of a simplicial cosimplicial abelian group is equivalent to the total complex of the associated bi-complex, and therefore we can conclude that
\widehat{F}(X)\simeq F^{\mathrm{ab}}(\Omega\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X])
when
X
is a finite simplicial set.
∎

By way of application we can now give a kind of formula for
\widehat{F}
when
F
is the symmetric powers functor.

Corollary 9.9
.

Let
\mathrm{S}^{n}
be the
n
-th symmetric power functor, and consider the functor
\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}(G)={G^{\mathrm{ab}}}^{\otimes n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
. Then
\widehat{\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)=\left(\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}]\right)_{\Sigma_{n}}.

Proof.

The corollary follows from Theorem
9.7
, and the isomorphism

\hom(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[A],\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X])^{\otimes n}\xrightarrow{\cong}\hom(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[A^{\wedge n}],\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}].

Here
A
and
X
are pointed finite sets, and the isomorphism is natural both in
A
and in
X
.
∎

Divided power functor, revisited

For another example, let us calculate
\widehat{F}
for
F(G)=\Gamma^{n}(G^{\mathrm{ab}})
for the second time. We know from Proposition
9.4
(
2
) that
\widehat{F}(X)=\Sigma^{-2n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(SX)^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]
. On the other hand, it follows from Theorem
9.7
that
\widehat{F}(X)=\left(\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}]\right)^{\Sigma_{n}}
. For sanity check, let us prove directly that there is an equivalence of functors

(26)

\Sigma^{-2n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(SX)^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]\simeq\left(\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}]\right)^{\Sigma_{n}}.

Both functors are excisive of degree
n
, so both are left Kan extended from
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
by Lemma
8.2
, and thus it is enough to show that the restrictions of these functors to
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
are equivalent. Next, by Proposition
8.3
, it is enough to show that the cross-effects of these functors are equivalent as functors from
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
to
Ch
. So let us calculate the cross-effects of both functors. Let
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
denote the functors on the left hand side and the right hand side of (
26
). We have

\ce_{i}F_{1}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})=\Sigma^{-2n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}\left[\bigvee_{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{i}=n}S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{i}}}\right]

The wedge sum is indexed by ordered
n
-tuples
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})
of positive integers that add up to
n
. Note that if
i<n
then at least one of the numbers
n_{1},\ldots,n_{i}
is greater than one, and then
S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{i}}}\simeq*
. Thus
\ce_{i}F_{1}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
is homotopically trivial for
i<n
. On the other hand,
\ce_{n}F_{1}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\simeq\Sigma^{-2n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{n}]
. As a chain complex with an action of
\Sigma_{n}
,
\ce_{n}F_{1}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
is equivalent to the chain complex concentrated in degree
-n
, in which it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
on which
\Sigma_{n}
acts by sign.
On the other hand, an easy calculation shows that

\ce_{i}F_{2}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})=\prod_{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{i}=n}\map_{*}(S^{n}/_{\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{i}}},\mathbb{Z})

where the product is, as before, over
i
-tuples of positive integers that add up to
n
. Once again, it is easy to see that
\ce_{i}F_{2}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
is trivial for
i<n
, and
\ce_{n}F_{2}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})=\Omega^{n}\mathbb{Z}
, which, again, is equivalent to the complex that has the sign representation in degree
-n
and is zero otherwise.

We found that
\ce_{i}F_{1}
and
\ce_{i}F_{2}
are both trivial for
i<n
, and are equivalent as complexes with an action of
\Sigma_{n}
for
i=n
. It follows that they are equivalent as functors from
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
to
Ch
.

10.
Passi functors

Let
G
be a group. Recall that we have defined
\operatorname{I}(G):=\ker(\mathbb{Z}[G]\to\mathbb{Z})
, and
\mathrm{Pa}_{n}(G):=\operatorname{I}(G)/\operatorname{I}(G)^{n+1}
.
\mathrm{Pa}_{n}
is called the
n
-th Passi functor. As usual, we will only evaluate it on finitely generated free groups. It is well-known that
\mathrm{Pa}_{1}
is the abelianization functor, but for
n>1
,
\mathrm{Pa}_{n}
does not factor through abelianization, and this is our main example of functors that do not factor through abelianization. In this section we will describe
\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}}_{n}
(Proposition
10.1
). In Sections
11.2
and
13
we will use the result of this section to calculate Ext into the Passi functor and out of the Passi functor respectively.

One of the reasons that the Passi functors are interesting is that
\mathrm{Pa}_{n}
provides a universal
n
-polynomial approximation to the free functor
G\mapsto\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[G]
. Since
\widetilde{Z}[G]\simeq\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega BG]
, one may expect the functor
\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}}_{n}
to be the universal
n
-excisive approximation to the functor
X\mapsto\widetilde{Z}[\Omega X]
. The purpose of this section is to make this intuition precise.

Recall that
P_{n}F
denotes Goodwillie’s
n
-excisive approximation of
F
. Consider the functor
X\mapsto\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega X]
. It will be useful to consider also the unreduced version of the functor
\mathbb{Z}[\Omega X]=\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega X_{+}]
. There are equivalences

\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega X]\simeq\operatorname{cofiber}(\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}[\Omega X]\simeq\operatorname{fiber}(\mathbb{Z}[\Omega X]\to\mathbb{Z}).

Note that
\operatorname{fiber}(\mathbb{Z}[\Omega X]\to Z)=I(\Omega X)
. So we really have an equivalence

I(\Omega X)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega X].

The functor
\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}}_{n}
will be described in terms of
P_{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega-]
, so we will review what is known about the Taylor tower of the functor
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega-]
. One way to think about this functor is as the functor
X\mapsto H\mathbb{Z}\otimes\Omega X
from pointed spaces to
H\mathbb{Z}
-modules. Let
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}_{0}
denote the full subcategory of
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
consisting of non-empty sets. In
[
1
]
it was shown that the
n
-th Taylor approximation of the functor
X\mapsto E\otimes\pointedmaps(K,X)
, where
E
is a spectrum and
K
is a finite CW-complex has the following form:

P_{n}(E\otimes\pointedmaps(K,-))(X)=\underset{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}_{0}}{\spectralNat}(K^{\wedge i},E\otimes X^{\wedge i}).

Note that the assignments
i\mapsto K^{\wedge i},E\otimes X^{\wedge i}
give contravariant functors from
Epi
to
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
and
Spectra
respectively. It is perhaps worth noting that the functor
i\mapsto K^{\wedge i}
is cofibrant in the projective model structure on
\fun({\textbf{Epi}}^{{\mathrm{op}}},{\textbf{S}}_{*})
. Therefore with minor assumptions on cofibrancy of
\Sigma^{\infty}
and fibrancy of
E\otimes X^{\wedge i}
one can take
\spectralNat
in the formula above to be a
1
-categorical mapping object.

The natural map
E\otimes\pointedmaps(K,X)\to\underset{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}_{0}}{\spectralNat}(K^{\wedge i},E\otimes X^{\wedge i})
is defined to be adjoint to the natural map

K^{\wedge i}\otimes E\otimes\pointedmaps(K,X)\to K^{\wedge i}\otimes E\otimes\pointedmaps(K^{\wedge i},X^{\wedge i})\to E\otimes X^{\wedge i}.

Specialising to
K=S^{1}
and
E=H\mathbb{Z}
, we can write the following model for the Taylor tower of
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega-]

P_{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega-](X)=\underset{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}_{0}}{\spectralNat}(S^{i},\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge i}]).

Proposition 10.1
.

There is an equivalence, natural in
X

\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}}_{n}(X)\simeq P_{n}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega-])(X)\simeq\underset{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}_{0}}{\nat}(S^{i},\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge i}]).

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
6.8
, together with Corollary
6.10
that there is a homotopy commutative diagram of
\infty
-categories

(27)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{Ch}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1},{\textbf{Ch}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\operatorname{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{q}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\operatorname{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Here the horizontal functors are induced by the classifying space functor. The left vertical functor is Goodwillie’s
P_{n}
. By definition,
P_{n}
is the (
\infty
-categorical) left adjoint to the inclusion functor (see Remark
6.7
). It follows that there is an essentially unique functor
\tilde{q}_{n}
that makes the diagram commute. Furthermore,
\tilde{q}_{n}
is the
\infty
-categorical left adjoint of the inclusion
\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})\hookrightarrow\fun_{\Sigma}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})
.

Consider the functor
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega-]\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}^{1}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
. Recall that
\widetilde{Z}[Y]=\mathbb{Z}[Y]/Z[*]
, and thus
\widetilde{Z}[Y]
is also equivalent to
\ker(\mathbb{Z}[Y]\to\mathbb{Z})
. Suppose
G
is a finitely generated free group. We have equivalences, natural in
G

\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega\operatorname{B}G]\simeq\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[G]\cong\operatorname{I}(G).

It follows that the equivalence of categories at the top of diagram (
27
) takes
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega-]
to
\operatorname{I}(-)
. Thus to prove the proposition we need to show that there is a natural equivalence
\mathrm{Pa}_{n}\simeq\tilde{q}_{n}(\operatorname{I})
, where
\operatorname{I}
is the augmentation ideal functor.

It is in fact well-known that
\mathrm{Pa}_{n}\cong q_{n}(\operatorname{I})
, where

q_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ab}})\to\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ab}})

is the
one-categorical
left adjoint of the inclusion functor. See for example
[
11
, Proposition 3.7]
. So what we want is equivalent to
\tilde{q}_{n}(\operatorname{I})\simeq q_{n}(\operatorname{I})
. This, too, follows from results in
[
11
]
. Indeed, to show that
\mathrm{Pa}_{n}\simeq\tilde{q}_{n}(\operatorname{I})
, it is enough to show that for every
n
-polynomial functor
T\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
, the quotient map
\operatorname{I}\to\mathrm{Pa}_{n}
induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categorical spectral objects of natural transformations

(28)

\spectralNat(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},T)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\\
\spectralNat(\operatorname{I},T).

It is enough to prove this equivalence when
T
is a functor that takes values in chain complexes homologically concentrated in degree zero. In other words, if
T
is in the essential image of the “inclusion” functor
\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ab}})\to\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})
. Indeed, if (
28
) is an equivalence for
T
concentrated in degree zero, then it is true for all
T
that take values in bounded chain complexes, and then it follows that it is true for all
T
, because every chain complex is naturally equivalent to the inverse homotopy limit of bounded complexes.

Note that when
T
is a functor taking values in chain complexes concentrated in degree zero, then both the domain and the range of (
28
) are spectra whose homotopy groups vanish in positive degrees. So we only need to show that the map induces isomorphism on homotopy groups in degrees
\leq 0
. Thus, to prove that (
28
) is an equivalence whenever
T
is concentrated in degree zero, is equivalent to showing that the quotient homomorphism
\operatorname{I}\to\mathrm{Pa}_{n}
induces isomorphisms for all
i\geq 0

\ext_{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}^{i}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},T)\xrightarrow{\cong}\ext_{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}^{i}(\operatorname{I},T).

For
i=0
this is an isomorphism because
\mathrm{Pa}_{n}=q_{n}(\operatorname{I}).
For
i>0
note that
\operatorname{I}(G)
is a direct summand of
\mathbb{Z}[G]\cong\mathbb{Z}[\hom(\mathbb{Z},G)]
, naturally in
G
. Thus
\operatorname{I}
is a projective object in
\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})
, and therefore
\ext_{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}^{i}(\operatorname{I},T)=0
for
i>0
. So it remains to show that
\ext_{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}^{i}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},T)=0
for
i>0
. This follows from the following two resuts of
[
11
]
. First, there is
[
11
, Corollary 4.3]
, which says that
\mathrm{Pa}_{n}
has homological dimension zero in
\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ab}})
, and therefore
\ext_{\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}^{i}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},T)=0
for
i>0
. Second, Theorem 1 of [loc. cit] says that there are isomorphisms for all
i

\ext_{\poly_{n}({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}^{i}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},T)\cong\ext_{\fun({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}^{i}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},T).

This implies the result we want.
∎

11.
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab},-)

In the next few sections we will use our general results to calculate
\ext
groups between polynomial functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
Ab
. We begin with investigating
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
, where
G\in\poly({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ab}})
. We remind the reader that
\mathrm{T}^{m}
is the
n
-fold tensor power functor, and
\mathrm{ab}
is abelianization. The calculation will be in terms of the cross-effects of
\widehat{G}
. In this section we use the term cross-effect as defined by Goodwillie
[
19
]
. Let us recall the definition

Definition 11.1

(Cross-effects, in the sense of Goodwillie)
.

Let
H\colon{\textbf{C}}\to{\textbf{D}}
be a functor, where
C
is pointed and
D
is a stable
\infty
-category. The
n
-th cross-effect of
H
is a functor
\ce_{n}H\colon{\textbf{C}}^{n}\to{\textbf{D}}
. The value
\ce_{n}H(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
is the total homotopy fiber of the
n
-dimensional cubical diagram that sends a subset
U\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
to

H\left(\bigvee_{i\notin U}X_{i}\right),

and whose maps are induced by collapsing the appropriate
X_{i}
s to the zero object.

It is well-known, and not difficult to prove that this definition of cross-effect is equivalent to the one in Definition
5.5
. See, for example
[
2
, Lemma 3.29]
, which establishes this equivalence for functors from Spectra to Spectra.

Here is a general result regarding
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
.

Proposition 11.2
.

Let
G\in\poly({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ab}})
, and let
\widehat{G}\in\exc({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}})
be the extension of
G
, as characterised in Theorem
C
. Then there is an equivalence, respecting the action of
\Sigma_{m}

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)\cong\pi_{-*}\left(S^{m}\otimes\ce_{m}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\right)\cong\pi_{-*-m}\ce_{m}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})^{\pm}.

Here the
\pm
indicates that the action of
\Sigma_{m}
on the right hand side is twisted by the sign representation.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.1
,

\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}}\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
is the functor

\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)=\Omega^{m}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge m}].

By Theorem
C
, there is an isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)\cong\pi_{-*}\spectralNat_{X}(\Omega^{m}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge m}],\widehat{G}(X))

and the right hand side is isomorphic to
\pi_{-*}\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge m}],S^{m}\otimes\widehat{G}(X))
.

Let us recall the functor
X^{\wedge m}
represents the
m
-th cross effect. The following lemma is elementary and well-known.

Lemma 11.3
.

For any functor
H\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{D}}
where
D
is an
\infty
-category there is an equivalence

(29)

\nat_{X}(X^{\wedge m},H(X))\simeq\ce_{m}H(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0}).

Proof.

Notice that if we replace
X^{\wedge m}
with
X^{m}
,
X^{m}\cong\pointedmaps(m_{+},X)
is a representable functor, represented by
m_{+}
, and we obtain an equivalence by the Yoneda lemma

\nat(X^{m},H)\simeq H(\underbrace{S^{0}\vee\ldots\vee S^{0}}_{m}).

Now write
X^{\wedge m}
as a total cofiber of a cube of representable functors, indexed by subsets of
\{1,\ldots,m\}
, and apply
\nat(-,H)
. For more details of this argument, see
[
3
, Lemma 3.13]
, where the claim is proved in the context of model categories for functors from spaces to spectra.
∎

Using the free-forgetful adjunction between
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
and
Ch
, it follows that if
H
is a functor from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
to
Ch
there is an equivalence

(30)

\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge m}],H(X))\simeq\ce_{m}H(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0}).

Taking cross-effects commutes with suspension. It follows that there is a natural isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)\cong\pi_{-*}(S^{m}\otimes\ce_{m}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})).

Note that the action of
\Sigma_{m}
on
S^{m}
induces the sign action on its non-trivial reduced homology group. It follows that the right hand side is isomorphic to

\pi_{-*-m}(\ce_{m}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0}))
, where the action of
\Sigma_{m}
is twisted by the sign.
∎

As a consequence, we can calculate
\ext
from
\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}
to most of the functors in Table
1
. We begin with the easy ones. Recall that if
A
is a graded abelian group, or a chain complex,
\Sigma^{n}A
is the shift of
A
by
n
. In the absence of grading, a group is considered concentrated in degree zero. Recall that
\sur(n,m)
denotes the set of surjections from
\{1,\ldots,n\}
to
\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Let
\Comp(n,m)={{}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\!\backslash\sur(n,m)
denote the set of compositions of
n
with
m
components.

Corollary 11.4
.

There are isomorphisms, that respect the action of the appropriate groups of automorphisms.

(1)

\ext^{i}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{Z}[\sur(n,m)]&i=n-m\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.,

where the natural action of the group
\Sigma_{m}\times\Sigma_{n}
on
\mathbb{Z}[\sur(n,m)]
on the right hand side is twisted by the tensor product of the sign representations of
\Sigma_{m}
and of
\Sigma_{n}
.

(2)

\ext^{i}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,m)]&i=n-m\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.,

where the action of
\Sigma_{m}
on
\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,m)]
is twisted by the sign.

(3)

\ext^{i}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{Z}&i=m=n=0\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right..

Here
\Sigma_{m}
acts trivially on the right hand side.

Remark 11.5
.

Part (
1
) is
[
36
, Theorem 1]
. See also [loc. cit., Proposition 2.5] for a discussion of signs.

Proof.

(
1
) By Proposition
11.2
we need to calculate

S^{m}\otimes\ce_{m}\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0}).

By Lemma
9.1
,
\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}
is the functor
X\mapsto\Omega^{n}\widetilde{Z}[X^{\wedge n}]
, so we need to calculate the cross-effect of this functor. Shift commutes with cross-effects, so we suppress
\Omega^{n}
at first. Consider the case
X=U_{+}=\underset{U}{\bigvee}S^{0}
where
U
is a finite set. We have
\widetilde{Z}[U_{+}^{\wedge n}]\cong Z[U^{n}]
. It follows that
\ce_{m}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[-^{\wedge}](S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
is equivalent to the total fiber of the contravariant cubical diagram
U\mapsto\mathbb{Z}[U^{n}]
, were
U
ranges over subsets of
\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Furthermore, the maps in the cubical diagram are defined as follows: if
U_{1}\subset U_{2}
then the corresponding map
\mathbb{Z}[U_{2}^{n}]\to\mathbb{Z}[U_{1}^{n}]
sends a map
n\to U_{2}
to itself if its image is in
U_{1}
, and to
0
if its image is not in
U_{1}
. It is easy to see that taking the total fiber of this diagram amounts to “killing” the non-sujective functions from
\{1,\ldots,n\}
to
\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Thus we obtain that

\ce_{m}\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[-^{\wedge n}](S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\cong\Omega^{n}\mathbb{Z}[\sur(n,m)].

It follows that

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\pi_{-*}\left(S^{m}\otimes\Omega^{n}\mathbb{Z}[\sur(n,m)]\right)=\Sigma^{n-m}\mathbb{Z}[\sur(n,m)].

Note that the natural action of
\Sigma_{m}
on
S^{m}
introduces on homology a twist by the sign representation of
\Sigma_{m}
and the action of
\Sigma_{n}
on
\Omega^{n}
introduces a twist by the sign representation of
\Sigma_{n}
. This proves part (
1
).

(
2
) Appealing again to Proposition
11.2
, and to Proposition
9.4
(
1
), we need to calculate the cross-effects of the functor
X\mapsto\Sigma^{-n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]
. Once again, note that if
U
is a finite set then
\widetilde{Z}[(U_{+}^{\wedge n})_{\Sigma_{n}}]\cong Z[(U^{n})_{\Sigma_{n}}]
. It follows that the
m
-th cross-effect of the functor
X\mapsto\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X^{\wedge n})_{\Sigma_{n}}]
evaluated at
(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
is equivalent to the total fiber of the cubical diagram
U\mapsto\mathbb{Z}[(U^{n})_{\Sigma_{n}}]
, were
U
ranges over subsets of
\{1,\ldots,m\}
. It is easy to see, once again, that taking the total fiber of this diagram amounts to “killing” the non-sujective functions from
\{1,\ldots,n\}
to
\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Thus we obtain that

\ce_{m}\Sigma^{-n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(-^{\wedge n})_{\Sigma_{n}}](S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\cong\Sigma^{-n}\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,m)].

It follows that

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\pi_{-*}S^{m}\otimes\Sigma^{-n}\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,m)]=\pi_{n-m-*}\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,m)],

which means that
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\Sigma^{n-m}\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,m)]
, as claimed. We proved part (
2
).

(
3
) It follows from Proposition
11.2
and Proposition
9.4
(
2
) that we have an isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\pi_{-*}\left(S^{m}\otimes\Sigma^{-2n}\ce_{m}\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(S^{1}\wedge-)^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]\right)(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\right).

As usual, let us first (a) suppress the (de)suspension and (b) consider the value of this functor at a finite pointed set
U_{+}
. We have

\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(S^{1}\wedge U_{+})^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]\cong\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(S^{n}\wedge U^{n}_{+})_{\Sigma_{n}}].

As before, it follows that the
m
-th cross-effect of the functor
X\mapsto\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(S^{1}\wedge X)^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]
, evaluated at
S^{0},\ldots,S^{0}
, is equivalent to
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{n}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}\sur(n,m)_{+}]
.

It is elementary to show that
S^{n}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}\sur(n,m)_{+}
is homeomorphic to a wedge sum of terms of the form
S^{n}_{H}
, where
H
ranges over a representing set of stabilizers of the action of
\Sigma_{n}
on
\sur(n,m)
.
When
n<m
this is trivial, of course. On the other hand, when
n>m
, the stabilizer of every element of
\sur(n,m)
is a non-trivial Young subgroup of
\Sigma_{n}
. We already mentioned that the quotient space
S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
is contractible whenever
n>1
. It follows that the quotient of
S^{n}
by the action of a non-trivial Young subgroup of
\Sigma_{n}
is contractible as well. We conclude that when
m\neq n
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{n}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}\sur(n,m)_{+}]
is trivial, and therefore
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ{\textbf{Ab}},\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})=0
. On the other hand, when
m=n
we have the equivalence

\ce_{m}\widetilde{Z}[(S^{1}\wedge-)^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}}](S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\simeq\widetilde{Z}[S^{m}\wedge_{\Sigma_{m}}\sur(m,m)_{+}]\simeq\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[S^{m}]

where
\Sigma_{m}
is acting on the right hand side by permuting the coordinates of
S^{m}
.

It follows that

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Gamma^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\pi_{-*}S^{2m}\otimes\Sigma^{-2m}\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathbb{Z}.

Note that
\Sigma_{m}
acts by permutation on
S^{2m}
and acts trivially on
S^{-2m}
. The action of
\Sigma_{m}
on
S^{2m}
is trivial on homology, and therefore the
\Sigma_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{Z}
on the right hand side is trivial.
∎

11.1.
\ext^{*}(T^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})

Recall that
\mathrm{S}^{n}
is the
n
-th symmetric power functor
\mathrm{S}^{n}(A)=A^{\otimes n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
. We will now consider
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
. By Corollary
9.9
,

(31)

\widehat{\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)=\left(\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}]\right)_{\Sigma_{n}}.

This formula is less topological than the one we have for
\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
,
\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
and
\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
, and for this reason calculating
\ext
into
\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
is harder. We will do some calculations for small
m
and
n
. One reason for doing it is to illustrate how one can work with the formula in (
31
). Another reason why this calculation is interesting is that it is the first example where we get torsion in the
\ext
groups.

Let us begin by considering the case
m=1
. Combining the case
m=1
of Proposition
11.2
with Corollary
9.9
, we conclude that

\ext^{*}\left(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}\right)\cong\pi_{-*}\left(S^{1}\otimes(\Omega^{n}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{n}}\right).

It is well-known that there is a
\Sigma_{n}
-equivariant homeomorphism
S^{n}\cong S^{1}\wedge\bar{S}^{n-1}
. Here
\Sigma_{n}
acts on
S^{n}
by permuting the coordinates, while it acts trivially on
S^{1}
, and acts by reduced standard representation on
\bar{S}^{n-1}
. It follows that there is an equivalence
S^{1}\otimes(\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}})_{\Sigma_{n}}\simeq(\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{n}}
. We obtained the following lemma.

Lemma 11.6
.

There is an isomorphism

\ext^{*}\left(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}\right)\cong\pi_{-*}\left((\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{n}}\right).

This has the following easy consequences

Lemma 11.7
.

(1)

\ext^{i}\left(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}\right)\otimes\mathbb{Q}=0
for
n>1
and all
i
.

(2)

\ext^{i}\left(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}\right)=0
for
i\geq n
.

Proof.

For the first part, we need to show that
\pi_{*}\left((\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{n}}\right)\otimes\mathbb{Q}=0
whenever
n>1
. This is the same as the homology of the chain complex
(\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Q})_{\Sigma_{n}}
. Since we are in characteristic zero, there is an equivalence

(\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Q})_{\Sigma_{n}}\simeq(\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Q})_{h\Sigma_{n}}

where by the right hand side we mean
\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\Sigma_{n}}\mathbb{Z}[E\Sigma_{n}]
. There is a spectral sequence

\operatorname{H}_{p}(\Sigma_{n};\pi_{q}(\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Q}))\Rightarrow\pi_{p+q}\left((\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Q})_{h\Sigma_{n}}\right).

\pi_{*}(\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Q})
consists of the rationalized sign representation of
\Sigma_{n}
in degree
1-n
, and nothing else. Assuming
n>1
, the rational homology of
\Sigma_{n}
with coefficients in the sign representation is zero in all degrees, including degree zero. It follows that
\operatorname{H}_{p}(\Sigma_{n};\pi_{q}(\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Q}))=0
for all
p
and
q
, and therefore
\pi_{*}\left((\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{n}}\right)\otimes\mathbb{Q}=0
.

For the second part, note the
\Sigma_{n}
-space
\bar{S}^{n-1}
is the geometric realization of an
n-1
-dimensional
simplicial set (see below). It follows that
(\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{n}}
is equivalent to a chain complex concentrated in degrees
1-n,\ldots,0
. It follows that
\pi_{i}(\bar{\Omega}^{n-1}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{n}}=0
for
i\leq-n
and therefore
\ext^{i}\left(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}\right)=0
for
i\geq n
.
∎

The space
\bar{S}^{n-1}
is
\Sigma_{n}
-equivariantly homeomorphic to the quotient space
\Delta^{n-1}/\partial\Delta^{n-1}
, where
\Delta^{n-1}
is the
n-1
dimensional simplex, on which
\Sigma_{n}
acts by permuting the vertices. The space
\Delta^{n-1}
is homeomorphic to the geometric realization of the poset of non-empty subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. The boundary
\partial\Delta^{n-1}
is the realisation of the poset of non-empty
proper
subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. It follows that
\bar{S}^{n-1}
has a pointed simplicial model, where the set of
k
-dimensional simplices is the set of chains of subsets
U_{0}\subset U_{1}\subset\cdots\subset U_{k}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
, together with a basepoint which corresponds to all chains that don’t end at the maximal element. Notice that the stabilizer group of a chain of this form is
\Sigma_{|U_{0}|}\times\Sigma_{|U_{1}|-|U_{0}|}\times\cdots\Sigma_{n-|U_{k-1}|}
. It follows that
\bar{S}^{n-1}
has an equivariant pointed simplicial model of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\frac{\Sigma_{n}}{\Sigma_{n}}_{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\bigvee_{1\leq n_{0}\leq n_{1}=n}\frac{\Sigma_{n}}{\Sigma_{n_{0}}\times\Sigma_{n-n_{0}}}_{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots{\displaystyle\bigvee_{1\leq n_{0}\leq\cdots\leq n_{k}=n}\frac{\Sigma_{n}}{\Sigma_{n_{0}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n-n_{k-1}}}_{+}}\cdots}

Applying
\map(-,\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{n}}
to this simplicial set we obtain a cosimplicial abelian group. Taking normalized chains, one obtains a chain complex of the following form

(32)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{1\leq n_{0}<n_{1}=n}\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{1\leq n_{0}<n_{1}<\cdots<n_{k}=n}\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{k-1}}

The number of tuples of integers that satisfy
1\leq n_{0}<\cdots<n_{k}=n
is
n-1\choose k
. Thus the
k
-th coboundary homomorphism in this complex is a homomorphism of the form

\delta^{k}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{n-1\choose k}\to\mathbb{Z}^{n-1\choose k+1}.

It follows that
\delta^{k}
can be represented with an
{n-1\choose k}\times{n-1\choose k+1}
matrix of integers. Let us denote this matrix by
[\delta^{k}]
.
The rows of
[\delta^{k}]
are indexed by tuples of the form
0=n_{-1}<n_{0}<\cdots<n_{k}=n
and the columns by tuples of the form
0=m_{-1}<m_{0}<\cdots<m_{k+1}=n
.
We will now describe the entries of
[\delta^{k}]
, which one obtains by unraveling the definitions. Suppose that the tuple
(m_{0},\ldots,m_{k+1})
is a refinement of
(n_{0},\ldots n_{k})
. By this we mean that there exists a
0\leq j\leq k
such that there is an identity of tuples

(m_{0},\ldots,m_{j-1},m_{j},m_{j+1},\ldots m_{k+1})=(n_{0},\ldots,n_{j-1},n_{j}^{\prime},n_{j},\ldots,n_{k})

where
n_{j-1}<n_{j}^{\prime}=m_{j}<n_{j}=m_{j+1}
. Then the entry of
[\delta^{k}]
corresponding to row
(n_{0},\ldots n_{k})
and column
(m_{0},\ldots,m_{k+1})
is
(-1)^{j}{n_{j}-n_{j-1}\choose n_{j}^{\prime}-n_{j-1}}
. In all other cases, the entry is zero.
We have proved the following proposition

Proposition 11.8
.

The groups
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
are isomorphic to the cohomology groups of the cochain complex (
32
), whose coboundary homomorphisms are described above.

Examples 11.9
.

Consider the case
n=2
. Then the cochain complex (
32
) has the following form. Each copy of
\mathbb{Z}
is labeled with the corresponding increasing sequence
(n_{0},n_{1}\ldots,n_{k})
, where
n_{k}=n=2
.

\underset{(2)}{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{2}\underset{(1,2)}{\mathbb{Z}}.

It follows that
\ext^{1}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab})=\mathbb{Z}/2
, and
\ext^{i}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab})=0
for
i\neq 1
.

Next, consider the case
n=3
. In this case (
32
) assumes the following form

\underset{(3)}{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{[3,3]}\underset{(1,3)}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\underset{(2,3)}{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\begin{bmatrix}-2\\
2\end{bmatrix}}\underset{(1,2,3)}{\mathbb{Z}}.

It follows that
\ext^{1}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab})=\mathbb{Z}/3
,
\ext^{2}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab})=\mathbb{Z}/2
, and
\ext^{i}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab})=0
for
i\neq 1,2
.

Finally let us consider the case
n=4
. This is the last case we will do by hand. In this case (
32
) takes the following form

\underset{(4)}{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\tiny[4,6,4]}\underset{(1,4)}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\underset{(2,4)}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\underset{(3,4)}{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\begin{bmatrix}-3&-3&0\\
2&0&-2\\
0&3&3\end{bmatrix}}\underset{(1,2,4)}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\underset{(1,3,4)}{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\underset{(2,3,4)}{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\tiny\begin{bmatrix}2\\
-2\\
2\end{bmatrix}}\underset{(1,2,3,4)}{\mathbb{Z}}

It follows that
\ext^{1}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{4}\circ\mathrm{ab})=\mathbb{Z}/2
,
\ext^{2}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{4}\circ\mathrm{ab})=\mathbb{Z}/3
,
\ext^{3}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{4}\circ\mathrm{ab})=\mathbb{Z}/2
, and
\ext^{i}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{4}\circ\mathrm{ab})=0
for all
i\neq 1,2,3
.

for
