Next let us discuss how one can calculate
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
. It turns out that it essentially can be reduced to the case
m=1
. We have the following generalization of Lemma
11.6

Lemma 11.10
.

There is an isomorphism

\ext^{*}\left(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}\right)\cong\bigoplus_{\underset{n_{1},\ldots,n_{m}>0}{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{m}=n}}\pi_{-*}\left((\bar{\Omega}^{n_{1}-1}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{n_{1}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes(\bar{\Omega}^{n_{m}-1}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{n_{m}}}\right).

Note that the right hand side can be calculated with the Kunneth formula. Thus the case of general
m
can be calculated given knowledge of the case
m=1
.

Example 11.11
.

Let us calculate
\ext^{*}\left(\mathrm{T}^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{4}\circ\mathrm{ab}\right)
. By Lemma
11.10
, this graded group is isomorphic to the following

\pi_{-*}\left(\mathbb{Z}\otimes(\bar{\Omega}^{2}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{3}}\right)\oplus\pi_{-*}\left((\bar{\Omega}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{2}}\otimes(\bar{\Omega}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{2}}\right)\oplus\pi_{-*}\left((\bar{\Omega}^{2}\mathbb{Z})_{\Sigma_{3}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}\right).

Using Table
2
and the Kunneth formula, we obtain the following result

\ext^{i}(\mathrm{T}^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{S}^{4}\circ\mathrm{ab})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/3\oplus\mathbb{Z}/3&i=1\\
\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2&i=2\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

11.2.
Ext from abelianization to Passi functors

In this section we calculate
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{Pa}_{n})
(see Corollary
11.14
below).
We hope that someone will extend this calculation to
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{T}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{Pa}_{n})
.

By case
m=1
of Proposition
11.2
, there is an isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{Pa}_{n})\cong\pi_{-*}S^{1}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}}_{n}(S^{0}).

By Proposition
10.1

There is an equivalence,

\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}}_{n}(S^{0})\simeq\underset{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}}{\nat}(S^{i},\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[{S^{0}}^{\wedge i}]).

We have a canonical isomorphism
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[{S^{0}}^{\wedge i}]\cong\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, this is a constant functor in the variable
i
. Let us introduce the notation

R_{n}=\colim_{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}}S^{i}.

It follows that there is an equivalence

\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}}_{n}(S^{0})\simeq\pointedmaps(R_{n},\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}).

We will see shortly that
R_{n}
is a suspension of another space, which we denote
\bar{R}_{n}
. We have the following consequence

Lemma 11.12
.

There are isomorphisms

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{Pa}_{n})\cong\pi_{-*}\pointedmaps(\bar{R}_{n},\mathbb{Z})\cong\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{*}(\bar{R}_{n}).

Let us analyze the (contravariant!) functor
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}
that sends
i
to
S^{i}
, and its colimit
R_{n}
. Let us begin by showing that indeed this functor is a suspension of another functor. Following
[
4
]
, let us define the simplex
\widehat{\Delta}^{i-1}
by the following formula

\widehat{\Delta}^{i-1}=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{i}\mid 0\leq x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},\ \underset{1\leq j\leq i}{\max}{x_{j}}=1\}.

It is not hard to show that
\widehat{\Delta}^{i-1}
is homeomorphic to the
i-1
-dimensional simplex, and furthermore its boundary consists of
i
-tuples
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i})
where at least one of the coordinates is zero. The reason for using this model of simplices is that it is functorial in
i
. More precisely, there is a contravariant functor

\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\textbf{Epi}}&\to&{\textbf{S}}\\
i&\mapsto&\widehat{\Delta}^{i-1}\end{array}

where functoriality on morphisms is given by repeating coordinates in the obvious way (see
[
4
]
for more details). Let
I=[0,1]
. Consider the map

(33)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\widehat{\Delta}^{i-1}\times I&\to&I^{i}\\
((x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}),t)&\mapsto&(x_{1}t,\ldots,x_{i}t).\end{array}

It is not hard to check that this map is a natural transformation of contravariant functors from
Epi
to
S
, and that for each
i
it gives a homeomorphism

(\widehat{\Delta}^{i-1}\times I,\partial(\widehat{\Delta}^{i-1}\times I))\xrightarrow{\cong}(I^{i},\partial I^{i}).

Let us define
\widehat{S}^{i-1}=\widehat{\Delta}^{i-1}/\partial\widehat{\Delta}^{i-1}
. By quotiening the homeomorphism of (
33
) by the boundary, we obtain a homeomorphism of contravariant functors
{\textbf{Epi}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}

\widehat{S}^{i-1}\wedge S^{1}\cong S^{i}.

Thus,
S^{i}
desuspends functorially in
i
. Let us define
\bar{R}_{n}=\underset{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}}{\colim}\widehat{S}^{i-1}
. We have a homeomorphism
R_{n}\cong S^{1}\wedge\bar{R}_{n}
, as promised.

Now we are ready to describe the homotopy type of
\bar{R}_{n}

Lemma 11.13
.

The space
\bar{R}_{n}
has the structure of a pointed CW-complex, with two zero-dimensional cells (one of which is the basepoint), and a single cell of dimension
i
for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
. When
n
is odd,
\bar{R}_{n}\simeq S^{n-1}
. When
n
is even,
\bar{R}_{n}\simeq*
.

Proof.

Let us note that
{\textbf{Epi}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
is a generalized Reedy category in the sense of
[
6
]
, with all maps raising degree, and only isomorphisms lowering degree. If
D
is a cofibrantly generated Quillen model category, then the 1-category of functors
[{\textbf{Epi}}^{{\mathrm{op}}},{\textbf{D}}]
has a Reedy model structure, which, since the matching objects are trivial, agrees with the projective model structure.

Consider the functor
\widehat{S}^{*-1}\colon{\textbf{Epi}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}
,
i\mapsto\widehat{S}^{i-1}
. For each
n
, the latching object of this functor at
n
is

\underset{n\twoheadrightarrow i\in n\downarrow{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n-1}}{\colim}\widehat{S}^{i-1}.

It is easy to see that this colimit is homeomorphic to the fat diagonal in
\widehat{S}^{n-1}
. More precisely the colimit is homeomorphic to the quotient of the following subspace of
\widehat{\Delta}^{n-1}
by its intersection with the boundary of
\widehat{\Delta}^{n-1}

\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\widehat{\Delta}^{n-1}\mid x_{a}=x_{b}\mbox{ for some }a<b\}.

Let us denote the latching object at
n
by
\Sigma^{-1}\Delta^{n}S^{1}
, since it is a desuspension of the fat diagonal in
S^{n}
. The space
\Sigma^{-1}\Delta^{n}S^{1}
is a
\Sigma_{n}
-equivariant subspace of
\widehat{S}^{n-1}
. It consists exactly of the points where
\Sigma_{n}
does not act freely. It follows that the inclusion
\Sigma^{-1}\Delta^{n}S^{1}\hookrightarrow\widehat{S}^{n-1}
is a
\Sigma_{n}
-cofibration for all
n
. This means that the functor
i\mapsto\widehat{S}^{i-1}
is Reedy cofibrant (and therefore also projective cofibrant).

Now we restrict the domain of the functor
\widehat{S}^{*-1}
to
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
. Let
\mathrm{L}_{n-1}^{n}\widehat{S}^{*-1}
denote the functor obtained by restricting
\widehat{S}^{*-1}
to
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n-1}
and then left Kan extending it back to
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
. There is a natural transformation
\mathrm{L}_{n-1}^{n}\widehat{S}^{*-1}\to\widehat{S}^{*-1}
. It is a homeomorphism for
*<n
. For
*=n
it is the inclusion
\Sigma^{-1}\Delta^{n}S^{1}\hookrightarrow\widehat{S}^{n-1}
.

The spaces
\Sigma^{-1}\Delta^{n}S^{1}
and
\widehat{S}^{n-1}
are spaces with an action of
\Sigma_{n}
. We may consider them as contravariant functors from
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
to
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
, by letting the functor to be trivial at all
i<n
. There is a standard square diagram of contravariant functors from
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
to
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
, which is both a pushout and a homotopy pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{S}^{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{S}^{*-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{-1}\Delta^{n}S^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{L}_{n-1}^{n}\widehat{S}^{*-1}}}

Passing to colimits over
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
we get a pushout diagram of the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{S}^{n-1}/_{\Sigma_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{R}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma^{-1}\Delta^{n}S^{1}/_{\Sigma_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{R}_{n-1}}}

Let us analyze the spaces in the left half of this square. We mentioned already that
S^{n}/_{\Sigma_{n}}
is a contractible space for
n>1
. The space
\widehat{S}^{n-1}/_{\Sigma_{n}}
is a desuspension of this contractible space, and indeed it is contractible as well. In fact, we can describe it very explicitly. Recall that
\widehat{S}^{n-1}
is the quotient of the space of
n
-tuples
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of non-negative real numbers with maximum
1
by the subspace consisting of
n
-tuples where at least one coordinates is zero. Every such
n
-tuple of real numbers is equivalent, up to permutation, to a unique
n
-tuple that satisfies
0\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{n-1}\leq x_{n}=1
. It follows that
\widehat{S}^{n-1}/_{\Sigma_{n}}
is homeomorphic to the quotient of the space of
n
-tuples of the form
0\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{n-1}\leq x_{n}=1
by the subspace of such
n
-tuples where
x_{1}=0
. This is the quotient of an
n-1
-dimensional simplex by the face determined by the equation
x_{1}=0
. In particular
\widehat{S}^{n-1}/_{\Sigma_{n}}
is homeomorphic to an
n-1
-dimensional simplex.

Next let us analyze the space
{\Sigma^{-1}\Delta^{n}S^{1}/_{\Sigma_{n}}}
. By a similar analysis to the above, it can be identified with a quotient of the space of
n
-tuples that satisfy
0\leq x_{1}\leq\ldots\leq x_{n-1}\leq x_{n}=1
, where at least two of the
x_{i}
s are the same. The space is quotiented by the subspace that satisfies
x_{1}=0
. This is exactly the boundary of the
n-1
-simplex, quotiened by the face
x_{1}=0
.

Let
\Delta^{n-1}
be the simplex defined by the inequalities
0\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{n-1}\leq 1
. Let
\Delta^{n-1}_{0}
be the face defined by
x_{1}=0
. We have shown that there is a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{n-1}/\Delta^{n-1}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{R}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\Delta^{n-1}/\Delta^{n-1}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{R}_{n-1}}}

It follows that
\bar{R}_{n}
is obtained from
\bar{R}_{n-1}
by attaching a single cell of dimension
n-1
. It is clear that
\bar{R}_{0}=S^{0}
. It follows by induction that
\bar{R}_{n}
has a single cell in each dimension between
1
and
n-1
. Furhtermore, if
i<n
then
\bar{R}_{i}
is the
i
-th skeleton of
\bar{R}_{n}
.

It remains to prove the statement about the homotopy type of
\bar{R}_{n}
. We already mentioned that
\bar{R}_{1}=S^{0}
. It is easy to check directly that
\bar{R}_{2}
is homeomorphic to an interval, and therefore is contractible. Assume by induction that the statement we want to prove holds for
\bar{R}_{i}
for
i<n
. We need to show that if
\bar{R}_{n-1}
is contractible (and
n
is odd) then
\bar{R}_{n}
is a sphere and if
\bar{R}_{n-1}
is a sphere (and
n
is even) then
\bar{R}_{n}
is contractible.

Since
\bar{R}_{n}
is obtained from
\bar{R}_{n-1}
by attaching a single cell of dimension
n-1
, it is clear that if
\bar{R}_{n-1}
is contractible then
\bar{R}_{n}\simeq S^{n-1}
. Suppose that
n
is even, and
\bar{R}_{n-1}\simeq S^{n-2}
. To understand the homotopy type of
\bar{R}_{n}
we need to understand the degree of the attaching map
\partial\Delta^{n-1}/\Delta^{n-1}_{0}\to\bar{R}_{n-1}
.
\bar{R}_{n-1}
has a single top-dimensional cell (of dimension
n-2
), that can be identified with the simplex defined by the inequalities
0\leq x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{n-2}\leq x_{n-1}=1
. The simplex
\partial\Delta^{n-1}
has
n
facets. The attaching map collapses the facet
x_{1}=0
to a point, and sends the interior of each of the remaining
n-1
facets of
\partial\Delta^{n-1}
homeomorphically onto the interior of the top dimensional cell of
\bar{R}_{n-1}
, with alternating signs. Since
n
is even,
n-1
is odd, so the total degree of the attaching map is
\pm 1
. It follows that
\bar{R}_{n}\simeq*
.
∎

From Lemmas
11.12
and
11.13
we have the following consequence

Corollary 11.14
.

There is an isomorphism of graded abelian groups

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{ab},\mathrm{Pa}_{n})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\Sigma^{n-1}\mathbb{Z}&n\ \mathrm{odd}\\
0&n\ \mathrm{even}\end{array}\right\}

12.
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},-)

In this section we will investigate
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
, where
G
is a polynomial functor. As usual, our method is to express the answer in terms of
\widehat{G}
. By Proposition
9.4
(
1
), the extension of
\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
is the functor

\widehat{\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)=\Sigma^{-m}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}}].

Which implies by Theorem
C
that

(34)

\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)\simeq\pi_{-*-m}\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}}],\widehat{G}(X)).

12.1.
Rational calculations

The formula above is especially easy to use over
\mathbb{Q}
, because in this case strict orbits are equivalent to homotopy orbits. We mention this chiefly to illustrate how our method can be used to recover some previous results from, e.g.,
[
36
]
. To see this, consider the functor
\mathbb{Q}\otimes({\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}})
. It is clear that its extension is given by

(35)

\mathbb{Q}\otimes\widehat{(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab})}(X)=\Sigma^{-m}\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}[X^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}}].

Suppose
X
is an object in some
\infty
-category
D
with an action of
\Sigma_{n}
. We may identify
X
with a functor
X\colon B\Sigma_{n}\to{\textbf{D}}
. Recall that
X^{h\Sigma_{n}}
and
X_{h\Sigma_{n}}
denote, respectively, the
\infty
-categorical limit and colimit of this functor.

Lemma 12.1
.

Suppose
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
is a polynomial functor that takes values in rational vector spaces. Let
\widehat{G}
be, as usual, the corresponding functor from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
to
Ch
. Then

\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)\cong\ext^{*}\left(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}\right),G)\cong\pi_{-*-m}\ce_{m}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})^{h\Sigma_{m}}.

Proof.

The first isomorphism is obvious, because
G
takes values in rational vector spaces, and the category of rational chain complexes is a localization of the category of integral chain complexes.

By rationalized version of (
34
) we have an isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),G)\cong\pi_{-*-m}\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}[X^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}}],\widehat{G}(X))

The canonical map
X^{\wedge m}_{h\Sigma_{m}}\to X^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}}
induces isomorphism in rational homology. Thus we have equivalences

\widetilde{Q}[X^{\wedge m}]_{h\Sigma_{m}}\simeq\widetilde{Q}[X^{\wedge m}_{h\Sigma_{m}}]\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}[X^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}}].

Therefore, we obtain an isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),G)\cong\pi_{-*-m}\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}[X^{\wedge m}]_{h\Sigma_{m}},\widehat{G}(X)).

The functor
\spectralNat(F,G)
converts
\infty
-categorical colimits in
F
to
\infty
-categorical limits. Therefore we have an equivalence

\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}[X^{\wedge m}]_{h\Sigma_{m}},\widehat{G}(X))\simeq\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}[X^{\wedge m}],\widehat{G}(X))^{h\Sigma_{m}}.

By a calculation similar to the one we did in the proof of Proposition
11.2
, the right hand side is equivalent to
\left(\ce_{m}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})^{h\Sigma_{m}}\right)
.
It follows that there is an isomorphism

\ext^{*}\left(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}\right),G)\cong\pi_{-*-m}\ce_{m}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})^{h\Sigma_{m}}.

∎

We can now recover some rational calculations done in
[
36
]
. We remind the reader that
\Part(n,m)={{}_{\Sigma_{n}}\backslash}\sur(n,m)/_{\Sigma_{m}}
is the set of partitions of the number
n
into
m
non-zero summands. Similarly let
\Stirling(n,m)=\sur(n,m)/_{\Sigma_{m}}
be the Stirling partition number, i.e., the number of ways to partition a set with
n
elements into
m
non-empty parts.

Proposition 12.2

(
[
36
]
, Theorem 3)
.

There are isomorphisms of graded
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces.

(1)

\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}))\cong\Sigma^{n-m}\mathbb{Q}[\Part(n,m)].

(2)

If
m=n\leq 1
then

\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}))\cong\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}))\cong\mathbb{Q}.

If
(m,n)
is not
(0,0)
or
(1,1)
then

\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}))\cong\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}))\cong 0.

(3)

\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}))\cong\Sigma^{n-m}Q[\Stirling(n,m)].

Proof.

(
1
) By a calculation virtually identical to the proof of Corollary
11.4
(
2
), the value of the
m
-th cross-effect of the functor
X\mapsto S^{-n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}[X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]
at
S^{0},\ldots,S^{0}
is
\Sigma^{-n}\mathbb{Q}[{{}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\backslash\sur(n,m)]
.
By equation (
35
) and Lemma
12.1
we have an isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}))\cong\pi_{-*}\left(S^{m-n}\otimes(\mathbb{Q}[{{}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\!\backslash\sur(n,m)])^{h\Sigma_{m}}.\right)

There are natural equivalences

\mathbb{Q}[{{}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\!\backslash\sur(n,m)/_{\Sigma_{m}}])\xleftarrow{\simeq}\mathbb{Q}[{{}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\!\backslash\sur(n,m)])_{h\Sigma_{m}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbb{Q}[{{}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\!\backslash\sur(n,m)])^{h\Sigma_{m}}.

Here the left homomorphism is the canonical map from homotopy orbits to strict orbits, which is rationally an equivalence. The right map is the “norm” map from homotopy orbits to homotopy fixed points, which also is a rational equivalence. Reminding ourselves that
\Part(n,m)={{}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\!\backslash\sur(n,m)/_{\Sigma_{m}}
We conclude that

\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}))\cong\pi_{-*}\left(\Sigma^{m-n}\mathbb{Q}[\Part(n,m)]\right)\cong\Sigma^{n-m}\mathbb{Q}[\Part(n,m)].

(
2
) First of all, note that there is a natural transformation from co-invariants to invariants

\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}\to\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}.

This transformation becomes an isomorphism after tensoring with
\mathbb{Q}
. It follows that there is an isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\mathrm{S}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}))\cong\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})).

So it is enough to calculate one of these groups. We will calculate Ext into
\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}
. Using Lemma
12.1
and Proposition
9.4
(
2
), the problem is reduced to calculating the
m
-th cross-effect of the functor
X\mapsto\Sigma^{-2n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}[(SX)^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]
.
By the usual calculation, we find that

\ext^{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}),\mathbb{Q}\otimes(\Gamma^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}))\cong\pi_{-*+2n-m}\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}[S^{n}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}\sur(n,m)_{+}]^{h\Sigma_{m}}\right).

If
m=n=0
or
1
, then clearly the right hand side is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Q}
. If
(m,n)=(1,0)
or
(0,1)
, then
\sur(n,m)=\emptyset
and we get zero. In general, if
n<m
the
\sur(n,m)=\emptyset
, and we get zero. So, suppose
n>m>0
. Then, as we saw already,
S^{n}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}\sur(n,m)_{+}
is contractible, and we get zero. Finally, suppose
n=m>1
. In this case the expression
\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}[S^{n}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}\sur(n,m)_{+}]^{h\Sigma_{m}}
simplifies to

\widetilde{Q}[S^{n}]^{h\Sigma_{n}}\simeq\widetilde{Q}[S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}].

Once again,
S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
is contractible, so the above chain complex is zero in
\pi_{*}
, so we get zero.

Part (
3
) is proved similarly, and is left to the reader.
∎

Integral calculations

Next we will do some integral calculations of
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
. This is where our methods really lead to some new results.

We will first give a presentation of
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
as the homotopy groups of an explicit homotopy limit. We will then use the general model to give a full calculation of
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
when
m=2,3
.

Remark 12.3
.

One can adapt our methods to do similar calculations involving the functors
\Gamma^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}
and, possibly,
\mathrm{S}^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab}
, though the latter is likely to be harder. We leave to the interested reader to work this out.

The starting point for calculating
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
is equation (
34
) on page
34
. Let us remark that
\spectralNat(F,G)
is an
\infty
-categorical mapping object. As such, it converts
\infty
-categorical (a.k.a homotopy) colimits in
F
to
\infty
-categorical limits. But it does not, in general, behave well on
1
-categorical limits. So it is not true that
\spectralNat(\widetilde{Z}[X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}],\widehat{G})
is equivalent to
\spectralNat(\widetilde{Z}[X^{\wedge n}],\widehat{G})^{\Sigma_{n}}
. To obtain a “formula” for
\spectralNat(\widetilde{Z}[X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}],\widehat{G})
we need to present
Z[X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]
as a homotopy colimit.

There is in fact a well-known way to present the strict orbit space of an action of a finite group as a homotopy colimit indexed by an orbit category. Let us introduce a few definitions

Definition 12.4
.

Suppose
G
is a finite group. Let
{\textbf{O}}_{G}
denote the orbit category of
G
. Its objects are transitive
G
-sets, and morphisms are
G
-equivariant maps.

More generally, suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a collection of subgroups of
G
, i.e., a set of subgroups closed under conjugation. Then let
{\textbf{O}}_{\mathcal{C}}
denote the full subcategory of
{\textbf{O}}_{G}
consisting of transitive sets whose stabilizers belong to
\mathcal{C}
.

We remind the reader that every transitive
G
-set
O
is (noncanonically) isomorphic to a set of the form
G/H
, where
H
is a subgroup of
G
. This is in fact a bijection between isomorphism classes of transitive
G
-sets and conjugacy classes of subgroups of
G
. For any
G
-space
X
there is a canonical homemorphism
\map(G/H,X)^{G}\cong X^{H}
.

Let
X
be a pointed
G
-space. Then
X
defines a contravariant functor from
{\textbf{O}}_{G}
to
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
,
O\mapsto\map(O,X)^{G}
. Note that there is a canonical map
\pointedmaps(O,X)^{G}\to X/_{G}
, given by evaluation at any point of
O
. This map induces a canonical map

\underset{O\in{\textbf{O}}_{G}}{\hocolim}\map(O,X)^{G}\to X/_{G}.

Proposition
12.5
below says that this map is an equivalence. This is a well-known consequence of Elmendorf’s theorem. As far as we know, it was first observed by Dror Farjoun. The statement for unpointed spaces is proved as Proposition 0.0(ii) in
[
35
]
. A similar argument works for pointed spaces.

Proposition 12.5
.

Let
X
be a pointed
G
-CW complex. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a collection of subgroups of
G
that contains all the non-basepoint isotropy groups of
X
. Then the following canonical map is an equivalence, where the homotopy colimit is taken in the pointed category:

(36)

\underset{O\in{\textbf{O}}_{\mathcal{C}}}{\hocolim}\map(O,X)^{G}\to X/_{G}.

Now let
X
be a pointed CW-complex, and consider the space
X^{\wedge n}
, with the action of
\Sigma_{n}
. It is easy to see that the isotropy groups of
X^{\wedge n}
are the Young subgroups of
\Sigma_{n}
. This means subgroups conjugate to
\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{i}}
, where
n_{1},\ldots,n_{i}>0
,
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{i}=n
, and
\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{i}}
consists of elements of
\Sigma_{n}
that leave invariant the blocks of some partition of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
with block sizes
n_{1},\ldots,n_{i}
. Let
{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}
be the collection of Young subgroups of
\Sigma_{n}
, and let
{\textbf{O}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}}
be the corresponding orbit category. Proposition
12.5
gives a presentation of
X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
as a homotopy colimit indexed on
{\textbf{O}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}}
.

We find it convenient to use a different description of
{\textbf{O}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}}
, as a category of multisets.

Definition 12.6
.

For us, a multiset is a standard finite set together with a positive integer (multiplicity) associated with each element. A multiset has the form
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})
, where
\{1,\ldots,i\}
is the underlying set, and
n_{1},\ldots,n_{i}
are the corresponding multiplicities. We refer to
i
as the number of elements, and to the sum
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{i}
as the total multiplicity of
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})
. A morphism between multisets
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{i})\to(n_{1},\ldots,n_{j})
is a function
\alpha\colon\{1,\ldots,i\}\to\{1,\ldots,j\}
such that for every
1\leq s\leq j
,

\sum_{t\in\alpha^{-1}(s)}m_{t}=n_{s}.

Clearly morphisms are, in particular, surjective functions of underlying sets, and there can only exist morphisms between sets of same total multiplicity. Let
{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}
denote the category of multisets of total multiplicity
n
.

There is a functor
{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}\to{\textbf{O}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}}
defined as follows. Suppose
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})
is an object of
{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}
. Define
\sur(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i};i)\subset\sur(n,i)
to be the set of all surjective functions
\alpha\colon\{1,\ldots,n\}\twoheadrightarrow\{1,\ldots,i\}
with the property that for each
1\leq j\leq i
,
\alpha^{-1}(j)
has exactly
n_{j}
elements.

The group
\Sigma_{n}
acts naturally on
\sur(n,i)
by pre-composition. It is clear that for any multi-set
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})
,
\sur(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i};i)
is a transitive invariant subset, with stabilizer
\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{i}}
. Thus
\sur(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i};i)
is a model for the
\Sigma_{n}
-orbit
\Sigma_{n}/\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{i}}
.

Suppose that we have a morphism in
{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}

\alpha\colon(m_{1},\ldots,m_{i})\to(n_{1},\ldots,n_{j})
. It is clear that
\alpha
induces a
\Sigma_{n}
-equivariant map
\sur(m_{1},\ldots,m_{i};n)\to\sur(n_{1},\ldots,n_{j};n)
. In this way we obtain a functor
{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}\to{\textbf{O}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}}
. The following lemma is elementary

Lemma 12.7
.

The assignment
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})\mapsto\sur(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i};n)
defines an equivalence of categories

{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\textbf{O}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}}.

The functor sends
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})
to a set isomorphic to
\Sigma_{n}/\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{i}}
.

Let us go back to the space
X^{\wedge n}
with the action of
\Sigma_{n}
. The contravariant functor
{\textbf{O}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}
that sends
O\mapsto\map(O,X^{\wedge n})^{\Sigma_{n}}
sends
\Sigma_{n}/\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{i}}
to
(X^{\wedge n})^{\Sigma_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{n_{i}}}\cong X^{\wedge i}
. Composing this functor with the equivalence of Lemma
12.7
, we obtain a contravariant functor
{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}\to{\textbf{S}}_{*}
that sends
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})
to
X^{\wedge i}
.
Combining Proposition
12.5
and Lemma
12.7
we obtain the following lemma

Lemma 12.8
.

Let
X
be a pointed CW complex. There is a natural equivalence

\underset{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})\in{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}}{\hocolim}X^{\wedge i}\xrightarrow{\simeq}X^{\wedge n}/_{\Sigma_{n}}.

The category
{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}
is an EI category, i.e., a category where every endomorphism is an isomorphism. Using Słomińska’s model for homotopy colimits over an EI category
[
35
]
, together with some elementary manipulations of colimits, it is not difficult to derive a presentation of the homotopy colimit that occurrs in Lemma
12.8
as a homotopy pushout of a cubical diagram as follows.

Suppose
1\leq i_{0}<\cdots<i_{p}\leq n
. Let us denote by
\operatorname{Iso}(i_{0},\ldots,i_{p})
the groupoid of chains of the form
\underline{i_{0}}\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow\underline{i_{p}}
in
{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}
, where each
\underline{i_{j}}
is a multiset with
i_{j}
elements and total multiplicity
n
. Then we have a contravariant cubical diagram, indexed by non-empty subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Suppose
i_{0},\ldots,i_{p}
are as above, with
0\leq p\leq n-1
. The cubical diagram is defined by the formula

(37)

\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{p}\}\mapsto\underset{\underline{i_{0}}\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow\underline{i_{p}}\in\operatorname{Iso}(i_{0},\ldots,i_{p})}{\hocolim}X^{\wedge i_{0}}.

Note that since
\operatorname{Iso}(i_{0},\ldots,i_{p})
is a groupoid, the homotopy colimit above is equivalent to a wedge sum indexed by the connected components of this groupoid.

(38)

\underset{\underline{i_{0}}\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow\underline{i_{p}}\in\operatorname{Iso}(i_{0},\ldots,i_{p})}{\hocolim}X^{\wedge i_{0}}\simeq\bigvee_{[\underline{i_{0}}\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow\underline{i_{p}}]}X^{\wedge i_{0}}_{h\operatorname{aut}(\underline{i_{0}}\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow\underline{i_{p}})}.

Here the wedge sum is indexed by a set of representatives of isomorphism classes of chains.
To obtain a commuting cubical diagram one may want to use the left hand side of (
38
), but for understanding the homotopy types of the terms, the right hand side may be preferable.

The point is that
X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
is equivalent to the (homotopy) pushout of this punctured cubical diagram. The following lemma is obtained by applying the machinery of
[
35
]
to the homotopy colimit in Lemma
12.8
.

Lemma 12.9
.

Let
X
be a pointed CW complex. The space
X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
is naturally equivalent to the homotopy colimit of the cubical diagram (
37
) (which up to homotopy is the same as the right hand side of (
38
)).

As a corolary, we now have the following general formula for
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
.

Theorem 12.10
.

Let
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
be a polynomial functor. Then
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
is isomorphic to
\pi_{-*-m}
of

\underset{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})\in{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}}{\operatorname{holim}}\ce_{i}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0}).

This is equivalent to the homotopy limit of a punctured cubical diagram of chain complexes, which on objects is defined by the formula

(i_{0},\ldots,i_{p})\mapsto\prod_{[\underline{i_{0}}\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow\underline{i_{p}}]}\ce_{i_{0}}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})^{h\operatorname{aut}(\underline{i_{0}}\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow\underline{i_{p}})}.

Here the product is indexed by a set of representatives of isomorphism classes of chains
\underline{i_{0}}\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow\underline{i_{p}}
in
{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}
, where each
\underline{i_{j}}
is a multiset with
i_{j}
elements.

Proof.

By (
34
),

\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
is isomorphic to
\pi_{-*-m}
of
\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}}],\widehat{G}(X))
. By Lemma
12.8

X^{\wedge n}/_{\Sigma_{n}}
is equivalent to
\underset{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})\in{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}}{\hocolim}X^{\wedge i}
. The functor
\widetilde{Z}
preserve homotopy colimit, so there is an equivalence

\widetilde{Z}[X^{\wedge n}/_{\Sigma_{n}}]\simeq\underset{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})\in{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}}{\hocolim}\widetilde{Z}[X^{\wedge i}].

Since
\spectralNat(-,\widehat{G})
takes homotopy colimits to homotopy limits, we obtain an equivalence

\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge m}_{\Sigma_{m}}],\widehat{G}(X))\simeq\underset{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})\in{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}}{\operatorname{holim}}\spectralNat_{X}(X^{\wedge i},\widehat{G}).

By the equivalence in (
30
), which was established in the proof of Proposition
11.2
, the right hand side is equivalent to

\underset{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{i})\in{\textbf{MSet}}_{n}}{\operatorname{holim}}\ce_{i}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
. This proves the first assertion of the theorem. The second assertion follows from the first because of Lemma
12.9
.
∎

Examples 12.11
.

We will now use the general results above to calculate
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{m}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
for
m=2,3
. We start with
m=2
. By equation (
34
),

(39)

\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)\cong\pi_{-*-2}\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X\wedge X)_{\Sigma_{2}}],\widehat{G}(X)).

When
n=2
, Lemma
12.9
amounts to saying that there is a homotopy pushout square, natural in
X

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\wedge\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X\wedge X)_{h\Sigma_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X\wedge X)_{\Sigma_{2}}}}

There the horizontal maps are induced by the diagonal, and the vertical maps are the natural maps from homotopy orbits to strict orbits. The functor
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[-]
from
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
to
Ch
preserves homotopy colimits. Applying
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[-]
to the diagram above, and then applying
\spectralNat(-,\widehat{G})
, we obtain the following homotopy pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\spectralNat_{X}\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X\wedge X)_{\Sigma_{2}}],\widehat{G}(X)\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X],\widehat{G}(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\spectralNat_{X}\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X\wedge X)_{h\Sigma_{2}}],\widehat{G}(X)\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X\wedge\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+}],\widehat{G}(X))}}

This is equivalent to the following pullback diagram, which is (the second statement of) Theorem
12.10
in the case
m=2
.

(40)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\spectralNat_{X}\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X\wedge X)_{\Sigma_{2}}],\widehat{G}(X)\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{G}(S^{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ce_{2}\widehat{G}(S^{0},S^{0})^{h\Sigma_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+},\widehat{G}(S^{0}))}}

So,
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
is isomorphic to
\pi_{-*-2}
of the pullback above.

We will now use this to calculate
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
for all
n
.

Lemma 12.12
.

There is an isomorphism of graded groups:

\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\Sigma^{n-2}\,\mathbb{Z}^{\Part(n,2)}&n\ \mathrm{even}\\
\Sigma^{n-2}\,\mathbb{Z}^{\Part(n,2)}\oplus\Sigma^{n-1}\,\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty})&n\ \mathrm{odd}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

By combining equation (
39
) with the pullback square (
40
), together with the formula for the cross-effects of
\widehat{\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}
(Corollary
11.4
(
2
)) we conclude that
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
is isomorphic to
\pi_{-*-2+n}
of the homotopy pullback of the following diagram

(41)

\left(\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,2)]\right)^{h\Sigma_{2}}\to\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+},\mathbb{Z})\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}.

Recall that
\Comp(n,2)
is the set of ordered pairs of positive integers
(n_{1},n_{2})
where
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
. The group
\Sigma_{2}
acts on
\Comp(n,2)
by switching
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
. The left side map in (
41
) is induced by taking
\mathbb{Z}[-]^{h\Sigma_{2}}
of the map
\Comp(n,2)\to\Comp(n,1)=*
.

Suppose first that
n=2k
is even. Then
\Comp(n,2)
has a fixed point
(k,k)
. The inclusion of this fixed point extends to a map from the following pullback square to (
41
)

\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+},\mathbb{Z})\to\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+},\mathbb{Z})\leftarrow*.

It is clear that the pullback of this diagram is
*
. It follows that the pullback of eq:pullback is equivalent to the pullback of the quotient of (
41
) by the pullback above. The quotient is the following pullback

\left(\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,2)\setminus*]\right)^{h\Sigma_{2}}\to*\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}.

Here
\Comp(n,2)\setminus*
denotes the set
\Comp(n,2)
with the fixed point removed. The group
\Sigma_{2}
acts freely on
\Comp(n,2)\setminus*
, and it follows that

\left(\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,2)\setminus*]\right)^{h\Sigma_{2}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[(\Comp(n,2)\setminus*)_{\Sigma_{2}}].

It follows that the last pullback is equivalent to

\mathbb{Z}[(\Comp(n,2)\setminus*)_{\Sigma_{2}}]\times\mathbb{Z}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[(\Comp(n,2))_{\Sigma_{2}}]=\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,2)].

We conclude that when
n
is even,
\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\pi_{-*-2+n}\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,2)]\cong\Sigma^{n-2}\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,2)].

Now suppose
n
is odd. Then
\Sigma_{2}
acts freely on
\Comp(n,2)
. In this case
\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,2)]^{h\Sigma_{2}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,2)_{\Sigma_{2}}]=\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,2)]
. Thus (
41
) takes the following form

\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,2)]\to\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+},\mathbb{Z})\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}.

Note that the right hand map in this pullback is equivalent to the inclusion
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}\times\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}P^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})
. It follows that the last pullback is equivalent to the pullback of the following diagram

\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,2)]\to\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})\leftarrow*.

The left map in this diagram is zero for dimensional reasons. It follows that the above pullback is equivalent to
\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,2)]\times\Omega\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})
. We conclude that when
n
is odd

\displaystyle\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{2}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\pi_{-*-2+n}\left(\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,2)]\times\Omega\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Sigma^{n-2}\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,2)]\times\Sigma^{n-1}\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}).

∎

Now let us consider the case
m=3
. Once again, equation (
34
) together with Corollary
11.4
(
2
) tell us that there is an isomorphisms

(42)

\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\pi_{-*-3+n}\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X^{\wedge 3})_{\Sigma_{3}}],\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X^{\wedge n})_{\Sigma_{n}}]).

Thus our next task is to understand
\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X^{\wedge 3})_{\Sigma_{3}}],\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X^{\wedge n})_{\Sigma_{n}}])
.

Proposition 12.13
.

\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X^{\wedge 3})_{\Sigma_{3}}],\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[(X^{\wedge n})_{\Sigma_{n}}])
is equivalent to

\begin{array}[]{lc}\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\Sigma^{-1}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}&\mbox{if }3\mid n\\[10.0pt]
\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\Sigma^{-1}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\times\Sigma^{-1}\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}/\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{2},\mathbb{Z})&\mbox{if }3\nmid n\end{array}

Proof.

We want to use the case
n=3
of Lemma
12.9
to resolve
(X^{\wedge 3})_{\Sigma_{3}}
as a homotopy colimit of a cubical diagram. The cube is indexed by isomorphism classes of chains in
{\textbf{MSet}}_{3}
. Recall that
{\textbf{MSet}}_{3}
is the category of multisets of total multiplicity
3
. The following diagram indicates the isomorphism classes of chains in
{\textbf{MSet}}_{3}
together with the corresponding groups of automorphisms (when the group is non-trivial).

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\Sigma_{2}
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 2
\displaystyle 3
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\displaystyle 2
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 3
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 1
\Sigma_{3}
3
\bullet
\bullet
\displaystyle 3
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\Sigma_{2}
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 2
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 1
\Sigma_{3}
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 2
\bullet
\bullet

Recall that the cubical diagram in Lemma
12.9
that resolves
(X^{\wedge n})_{\Sigma_{n}}
sends a chain
\underline{i_{k}}\to\cdots\to\underline{i_{0}}
to
X^{\wedge i_{0}}_{h\operatorname{aut}(\underline{i_{k}}\to\cdots\to\underline{i_{0}})}
. It follows that there is a homotopy pushout cubes of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\wedge\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\wedge{\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}}_{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\wedge X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\wedge 3}_{\Sigma_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\wedge X\wedge\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{\wedge 3}_{h\Sigma_{3}}}}

Let
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
be a polynomial functor. Applying
\spectralNat_{X}(-,\widehat{G})
to the cubical diagram, we find that there a homotopy pullback diagram of the following form (for typographical reasons we write
\ce_{i}\widehat{G}
to mean
\ce_{i}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ce_{3}\widehat{G})^{h\Sigma_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+},\ce_{2}\widehat{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\spectralNat_{X}(X^{\wedge 3}_{\Sigma_{3}},\widehat{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ce_{2}\widehat{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}}_{+},\widehat{G}(S^{0}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+},\widehat{G}(S^{0}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{G}(S^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{G}(S^{0})}}

Now we substitute
\widehat{G}(X)=\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]
. In this case
\widehat{G}(S^{0})=\mathbb{Z}
,
\ce_{2}\widehat{G}(S^{0},S^{0})=\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,2)]
and
\ce_{3}\widehat{G}(S^{0},S^{0},S^{0})=\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,3)]
. We obtain the following homotopy pullback cube.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,3)])^{h\Sigma_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+},\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,2)])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\spectralNat_{X}(X^{\wedge 3}_{\Sigma_{3}},X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,2)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}}_{+},\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty}_{+},\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

Consider the arrows marked
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
in the diagram above. The cofibers of these maps are, respectively
\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3},\mathbb{Z})
,
\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})
, and
\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,2)])
. It follows that
\spectralNat_{X}(X^{\wedge 3}_{\Sigma_{3}},X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}})
is equivalent to the total fiber of the following square diagram

(43)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,3)]^{h\Sigma_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}[\Comp(n,2)])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}},\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})}}

Let us analyze
\mathbb{Z}[C(n,3)]^{h\Sigma_{3}}
.
Recall that
\Comp(n,3)
is the set of ordered triples
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
of positive integers whose sum is
n
.
\Sigma_{3}
acts on this set by permuting the triples. The set of orbits of this action is
\Part(n,3)
. For each point
x\in\Comp(n,3)
let
[x]\in\Part(n,3)
be the orbit of
x
, and let
G_{x}\subset\Sigma_{3}
be the stabilizer of
x
. There is an equivalence

\mathbb{Z}[C(n,3)]^{h\Sigma_{3}}\simeq\prod_{[x]\in\Part(n,3)}\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}{G_{x}}_{+},\mathbb{Z}).

Furthermore, there is a natural equivalence
\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}{G_{x}}_{+},\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}\times\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}G_{x},\mathbb{Z}).
Therefore we obtain an equivalence

\mathbb{Z}[C(n,3)]^{h\Sigma_{3}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\prod_{[x]\in\Part(n,3)}\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}{G_{x}},\mathbb{Z}).

Suppose that
3\nmid n
. Then the action of
\Sigma_{3}
does not have a fixed point. In this case the action has two types of orbits: the free orbits, consisting of triples where
n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}
are distinct, and the orbits where two of the three are the same, but not all three. The stabilizer of such an orbit is conjugate to the group
\Sigma_{2}
permuting the first two coordinates. Each orbit of this type has a unique representative of the form
(m,m,m^{\prime})
where
m\neq m^{\prime}
and
2m+m^{\prime}=n
. It follows that when
3\nmid n
there is an equivalence

\mathbb{Z}[C(n,3)]^{h\Sigma_{3}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\prod_{\begin{array}[]{c}{\{(m,m,m^{\prime})\mid m,m^{\prime}>0,}\\
{2m+m^{\prime}=n\}}\end{array}}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}).

Consider the top map in diagram (
43
). With the splitting above, it takes the form

\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\prod_{(m,m,m^{\prime})}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})\to\prod_{(n_{1},n_{2})\in\Comp(n,2)}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}).

The map is determined by what it does on each factor. The map
\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\to\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\Comp(n,2)}
can only be null-homotopic, for dimensional reasons. It is not difficult to check that the map

\prod_{(m,m,m^{\prime})}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})\to\prod_{(n_{1},n_{2})\in\Comp(n,2)}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})

maps the factor indexed by
(m,m,m^{\prime})
isomorphically onto the factor indexed by the pair
(2m,m^{\prime})\in\Comp(n,2)
(it also maps the same factor by multiplication by
2
to the factor indexed by the pair
(m+m^{\prime},m)
, but this does not affect the homotopy type of the homotopy fiber). It follows that the fiber of the last map is a product of copies of
\Omega\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})
indexed by pairs
(n_{1},n_{2})\in\Comp(n,2)
where
n_{1}
is odd. The number of such pairs is
\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor
. We conclude that when
3\nmid n
, the homotopy fiber of the top horizontal map in (
43
) is equivalent to

\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\Omega\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}.

Now consdier the bottom map in (
43
). The fiber of this map is
\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}/\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{2},\mathbb{Z}
. So the total fiber of (
43
) is the fiber of a map

\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\Omega\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\to\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}/\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{2},\mathbb{Z}).

Once again, the restriction of the map to
\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]
can only be null, for dimensional reasons. Note that the targe of the map is
3
-local, while the factor
\Omega\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}
is acyclic at the prime
3
. It follows that the map can only be null, and therefore the total fiber of (
43
) is equivalent to

\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\Omega\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\times\Omega\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}/\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{2},\mathbb{Z}).

This proves the proposition when
3\nmid n
.

Now suppose that
3\mid n
, so
n=3l
. Then the action of
\Sigma_{3}
on
\Comp(n,3)
has a fixed point, namely
(l,l,l)
. In this case
\mathbb{Z}[C(n,3)]^{h\Sigma_{3}}
is equivalent to the following product

\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\prod_{\begin{array}[]{c}{\{(m,m,m^{\prime})\mid m,m^{\prime}>0,}\\
{m\neq m^{\prime},2m+m^{\prime}=n\}}\end{array}}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})\times\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3},\mathbb{Z}).

And in this case the square diagram (
43
)
takes the following form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\underset{(m,m,m^{\prime})}{\prod}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})\times\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3},\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underset{(n_{1},n_{2})}{\prod}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}},\mathbb{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})}}

Here the triples
(m,m,m^{\prime})
satisfy, as before
m,m^{\prime}>0
,
2m+m^{\prime}=n
and
m\neq m^{\prime}
. The pairs
(n_{1},n_{2})
range over all elements of
\Comp(n,2)
. Let us make the following observations about the maps in the last diagram:

(1)

The maps are zero on
\mathbb{Z}[\rho(n,3)]
for dimensional reasons.

(2)

The left vertical map restricts to an equivalence on
\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3},\mathbb{Z})
, and it restricts to a transfer map (which is a split injection) on each copy of
\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})
.

(3)

The right vertical map restricts to an equivalence on each copy of
\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})

(4)

The top horizontal map is equivalent to a product of three maps:

(a)

The trivial map on
\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]

(b)

The map

\prod_{(m,m,m^{\prime})}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})\to\prod_{(n_{1},n_{2})\neq(2l,l)}\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})

which maps the copy of
\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})
indexed by
(m,m,m^{\prime})
by an equivalence to the copy indexed by
(2m,m^{\prime})
and by multiplication by
2
to the copy indexed by
(m+m^{\prime},m)
.

(c)

The restriction map
\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3},\mathbb{Z})\to\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},Z)
where the latter copy of
\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},Z)
is indexed by
(2l,l)
.

(5)

The bottom map is the restriction map.

It follows that the induced map of horizontal fibers of the latest diagram has the following form

\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\Omega\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\times\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}/\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{2},\mathbb{Z})\to\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}/\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{2},\mathbb{Z}).

The map restricts to an equivalence of the factor
\pointedmaps(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}/\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{2},\mathbb{Z})
. It follows that the fiber of the latest map, which is the total fiber of (
43
) is

\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\times\Omega\pointedmaps(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}.

This proves the proposition in case
3\mid n
.
∎

Corollary 12.14
.

There is an isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\Lambda^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab},\Lambda^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\Sigma^{n-3}\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\oplus\Sigma^{n-2}\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}&\mbox{if }3\mid n\\[10.0pt]
\Sigma^{n-3}\mathbb{Z}[\Part(n,3)]\oplus\Sigma^{n-2}\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{*}(\mathbb{R}\mathrm{P}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z})^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\oplus&\mbox{if }3\nmid n\\
\oplus\Sigma^{n-2}\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}^{*}(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}/\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{2})&\end{array}\right.

Proof.

This follows easily from Equation (
42
) and Proposition
12.13
.
∎

13.
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},-)

Recall that
\mathrm{Pa}_{n}
denotes the
n
-th Passi functor. In this section we study
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},G)
. Recall from Proposition
10.1
that

\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}_{n}}=P_{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega-].

Recall from Remark
6.7
that
P_{n}
denotes Goodwillie’s
n
-th Taylor approximation, and it is the left adjoint of the inclusion functor

i_{n}\colon\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}})\to\fun_{\operatorname{ind}}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}}).

Let us recall that the functor
i_{n}
has a right adjoint as well. Furthermore, the right adjoint has a simple formula which we will now recall.

Recall that
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
denotes the category of pointed sets with at most
n
non-basepoint elements. Let
D
be a stable
\infty
-category. Let
\rho_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})
be the restriction functor.
Recall from Lemma
8.2
that
the restriction functor

\rho_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})

restricts to an equivalence, which we still denote by
\rho_{n}

\rho\colon\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}}),

and the inverse equivalence is given by left Kan extension.

It follows that the left Kan extension functor

\mathrm{L}_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})

factors as a composition, where each functor is a fully faithful embedding

\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\mathrm{L}_{n}^{0}}\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{i_{n}}\fun_{\operatorname{ind}}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{i}\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}}).

Since
\mathrm{L}_{n}=i\circ i_{n}\circ\mathrm{L}_{n}^{0}
is left adjoint
to the restriction functor
\rho_{n}\colon\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})
, and
i\colon\fun_{\operatorname{ind}}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
is a fully faithful embedding, it follows that
i_{n}\circ\mathrm{L}_{n}^{0}
is left adjoint to the composition
\rho_{n}\circ i
, which we will denote simply as
\rho_{n}\colon\fun_{\operatorname{ind}}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})
.

Since
\mathrm{L}_{n}^{0}
is an equivalence, it follows that the composition of functors
\mathrm{L}_{n}^{0}\circ\rho_{n}

\fun_{\operatorname{ind}}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow{\rho_{n}}\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n},{\textbf{D}})\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\mathrm{L}_{n}^{0}}\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D)}}

is a right adjoint to
i_{n}
. By slight abuse of notation, we denote the right adjoint of
i_{n}
simply by
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}.
Given a functor
F\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{D}}
,
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}F
is obtained by restricting
F
to
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
and then left Kan extending it back to
{\textbf{S}}_{*}
.

The utility of the right adjoint is that it allows us to calculate maps from the left adjoint, as in the following proposition. To set up the notation, let
D
be a stable
\infty
-category, let
d
be an object of
D
and let
K
be a finite pointed CW complex. Consider the representable functor
d\otimes\pointedmaps(K,-)\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{D}}
that sends
X
to
d\otimes\pointedmaps(K,X)

Proposition 13.1
.

With notation above, let
H\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{D}}
be a functor. There is an equivalence

\spectralNat(P_{n}(d\otimes\pointedmaps(K,-)),H)\simeq\spectralmaps_{{\textbf{D}}}(d,\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}H(K)).

Proof.

Note that when we write
P_{n}
it really is a shorthand for
i_{n}P_{n}
, where
i_{n}
is the inclusion of
\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
into
\fun_{\operatorname{ind}}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
and
P_{n}
is the left adjoint of
i_{n}
. Since
i_{n}
also has a right adjoint
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}
, we have equivalences

\spectralNat(i_{n}P_{n}(d\otimes\pointedmaps(K,-)),H)\simeq\map_{\exc_{n}({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})}(P_{n}(d\otimes\pointedmaps(K,-)),\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}H)\simeq\\
\simeq\spectralNat(d\otimes\pointedmaps(K,-),i_{n}\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}H)\simeq\spectralmaps(d,\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}H(K)).

∎

As a corollary we have the following description of
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},G)
.

Proposition 13.2
.

Let
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
be a polynomial functor. There is an isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},G)\cong\pi_{-*}\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}\widehat{G}(S^{1}).

Proof.

We know that

\widehat{\mathrm{Pa}}_{n}=P_{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[\Omega-]\simeq\mathbb{Z}\otimes\pointedmaps(S^{1},-).

It follows that

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},G)\cong\pi_{-*}\spectralNat(P_{n}\left(\mathbb{Z}\otimes\pointedmaps(S^{1},-)\right),\widehat{G}(-)).

Apply Proposition
13.1
to the right hand side, with
{\textbf{D}}={\textbf{Ch}}
. Since
\mathbb{Z}
is the unit of
Ch
, it follows that the right hand side is equivalent to
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}\widehat{G}(S^{1}).

∎

The last proposition motivates us to find a formula for
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}
. Let
H\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{D}}
be a functor. By definition
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}H
is obtained by restricting
H
to
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
and then homotopy left Kan extending it back. There is a standard description of it as an
\infty
-categorical coend

(44)

\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}H(X)\simeq\Sigma^{\infty}X^{i}\otimes_{i_{+}\in{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}}H(i_{+}).

There is also a (well-known) smaller model for
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}
that uses coend over the category
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
rather than
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
. We continue letting
D
denote a stable (or more generally additive and idempotent complete)
\infty
-category.
Recall from Proposition
8.3
that there is a “Morita” equivalence between categories

-\otimes_{i}X^{\wedge i}:\hbox to212.54pt{\vbox to18.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 106.27077pt\lower-9.12332pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {

{{{{{}}{
