{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{{}}}{{{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.39 -5.11 L -26.71 -5.11}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-19.50623pt}{-3.69275pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 -26.99 -5.11)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}:\nat_{X}(X^{\wedge i},-)

Notice that the hypothesis on
D
is self-dual. Thus the proposition applies both to covariant and contravariant functors. If
F
is a contravariant functor from
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}
to
D
then the corresponding functor from
Epi
to
D
is
i\mapsto X^{\wedge i}\otimes_{X}F(X)
.

The following lemma says that these equivalences of functor categories induce equivalence of coends.

Lemma 13.3
.

With same hypotheses on
D
, suppose we have a pair of functors
F,G\colon{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}\to{\textbf{D}}
. There is an equivalence

G\otimes_{{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}}F\simeq(G(X)\otimes_{X\in{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}}X^{\wedge i})\otimes_{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}}\nat_{Y}(Y^{\wedge i},F(Y)).

The same holds when
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}
and
Epi
are replaced with
{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}
and
{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}
.

Proof.

We give a sketch of proof, where we blithely manipulate
\infty
-categorical coends as if they were
1
-categorical coends without stopping to justify every step.

By Proposition
8.3
there is an equivalence, natural in
X

F(X)\simeq X^{\wedge i}\otimes_{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}}\nat_{Y}(Y^{\wedge i},F(Y)).

It follows that there is an equivalence

G\otimes_{{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}}F\simeq G(X)\otimes_{X\in{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}}\left(X^{\wedge i}\otimes_{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}}\nat_{Y}(Y^{\wedge i},F(Y))\right).

This gives the desired result by associativity of coend (a.k.a the categorical Fubini theorem).
∎

Remark 13.4
.

Let
F\in\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})
. We saw in Lemma
11.3
that there is an equivalence

\nat_{X}(X^{\wedge i},F(X))\simeq\ce_{i}F(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0}).

In other words, the functor

\nat_{X}(X^{\wedge-},F(X))\colon\fun({\textbf{FinSet}}_{*},{\textbf{D}})\to\fun({\textbf{Epi}},{\textbf{D}})

is the functor that associates to
F
the cross-effects of
F
which themselves assemble into a functor
{\textbf{Epi}}\to{\textbf{D}}
. The same statement in dualized form holds for contravariant functors.

We now can describe
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}
in terms of coend over
Epi
.

Lemma 13.5
.

Let
D
be a stable
\infty
-category, and let
H\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{D}}
be a functor. There is a natural equivalence

\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}H(X)\simeq X^{\wedge j}\otimes_{j\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}}\ce_{j}H(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0}).

Proof.

We saw in (
44
) that

\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}H(X)\simeq X^{i}\otimes_{i_{+}\in{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}}H(i_{+}).

By Lemma
13.3
it follows that

\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}H(X)\simeq(X^{i}\otimes_{i_{+}\in{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}}(i_{+})^{\wedge j})\otimes_{j\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}}\left(\nat_{Y\in{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}}(Y^{\wedge j},H(Y))\right).

(Note that the role of
X
in Lemma
13.3
is played by
i_{+}
here, the role of
i
there is played by
j
here, and
X
here does not have a counterpart in Lemma
13.3
. We apologize to the reader for this change of variables and hope it does not cause too much confusion).
We just saw that
\nat_{Y\in{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}}(Y^{\wedge j},H(Y))\simeq\ce_{j}H(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
. We claim that for
j\leq n
there is an equivalence

X^{i}\otimes_{i_{+}\in{\textbf{FinSet}}_{*}^{\leq n}}(i_{+})^{\wedge j}\simeq X^{\wedge j}

by an easy direct calculation. The lemma follows.
∎

Remark 13.6
.

We have a model for
\mathrm{L}_{n}\rho_{n}H
as an
\infty
-categorical coend. It is worth remarking that in many cases it can be calculated as a
1
-categorical coend. We claim that that for a fixed pointed simplicial set
X
, the functor
X^{\wedge-}\colon{\textbf{Epi}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}\to{\textbf{sSet}}_{*}
is cofibrant in the projective model structure. Indeed, it is easy to check that it is cofibrant in the generalized Reedy model structure of
[
6
]
, and that the Reedy model structure coincides with the projective model structure in this case. Suppose that
D
is an
\infty
-category arising from a combinatorial model category. Then
\fun({\textbf{Epi}},{\textbf{D}})
has an injective, as well as projective model structure. A functor is cofibrant in the injective model structure if it is objectwise cofibrant. Suppose
\ce_{j}H(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
is cofibrant for every
j
. Then it follows from
[
27
, Remark A.2.9.27]
that the coend in Lemma
13.5
can be computed 1-categorically.

This gives us a combinatorial model for
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},G)
.

Lemma 13.7
.

Let
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
be a polynomial functor. Assume that we have a model for
\widehat{G}\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
such that for every pointed set
n_{+}
,
\widehat{G}(n_{+})
is a cofibrant chain complex. Then there is an isomorphism

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{n},G)\cong\pi_{-*}\left(S^{i}\otimes_{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq n}}\ce_{i}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\right).

Here the coend can be calculated either
1
or
\infty
-categorically.

Proof.

The statement for
\infty
-categorical coend follows from Proposition
13.2
and Lemma
13.5
. Note that
\ce_{j}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
is a direct summand of
\widehat{G}(i_{+})
, and thus the cross-effects of
\widehat{G}
take place in free abelian groups. The statement about
1
-categorical coend follows now from Remark
13.6
.
∎

Now we can calculate some examples. Let us consider
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{m},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
. We describe these groups in terms of homology of a certain space that we will now define.

Definition 13.8
.

Let
X
be a pointed space. Suppose we are given positive integers
m,n
. Define

\Delta^{n}_{m}X=\{x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge x_{n}\in X^{\wedge n}\mid\mbox{at most }m\mbox{ of the }x_{i}\mbox{ are distinct}\}

We also define
\Delta^{n}_{0}X=*
when
0<n
and
\Delta^{0}_{0}X=S^{0}
.

Remark 13.9
.

Note that
\Delta^{n}_{n-1}X=\Delta^{n}X
is the fat diagonal in
X^{\wedge n}
. For
m<n
,
\Delta^{n}_{m}X
is a subspace of
\Delta^{n}X
. For
m\geq n
,
\Delta^{n}_{m}X=X^{\wedge n}
.

Let
n/{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq m}
be the slice category of arrows
n\twoheadrightarrow i
where
n
is fixed and
i\leq m
. Then it is easy to see that there is a homeomorphism

\Delta^{n}_{m}X\cong\underset{n\twoheadrightarrow i\in n/{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq m}}{\colim}X^{\wedge i}.

Here colimit is taken
1
-categorically. The
1
-categorical colimit is homotopy equivalent to the
\infty
-categorical one because the functor
i\mapsto X^{\wedge i}
is cofibrant.

Proposition 13.10
.

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{m},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{n-*}(\Delta^{n}_{m}S^{1}).

Proof.

By Lemma
9.1

\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)=\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}]
. By a straightforward calculation,
\ce_{i}\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\simeq\Sigma^{-n}\mathbb{Z}[\sur(n,i)]
. Note that this is a cofibrant chain complex. Indeed, it is a chain complex concentrated in a single degree, in which it is free abelian.
It follows from Lemma
13.7
that

\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{m},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\pi_{-*}\left(S^{i}\otimes_{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq m}}\Sigma^{-n}\mathbb{Z}[\sur(n,i)]\right)

and the right hand side is isomorphic to
\pi_{n-*}\left(S^{i}\otimes_{i\in{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq m}}\mathbb{Z}[\sur(n,i)]\right)
. The coend can be calculated
1
-categorically. It is easy to see that the coend is equivalent to

\widetilde{Z}[\underset{n\twoheadrightarrow i\in n/{\textbf{Epi}}^{\leq m}}{\colim}S^{i}]\simeq\widetilde{Z}[\Delta^{n}_{m}S^{n}]

which proves the proposition.
∎

In
[
36
]
, Proposition 5 it is proved that if
0<m\leq n
then
\ext^{n-m}(\mathrm{Pa}_{m},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
is non-trivial. The following corollary refines this statement.

Corollary 13.11
.

If
m<n
,
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{m},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
is concentrated in degree
*=n-m
. Furthermore,
\ext^{n-m}(\mathrm{Pa}_{m},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
is a free abelian group, which is non-trivial for
m>0
. For
m\geq n

\ext^{0}(\mathrm{Pa}_{m},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{m},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong 0
for
*>0
.

Proof.

By Proposition
13.10
, the graded group
\ext^{*}(\mathrm{Pa}_{m},\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab})
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{\operatorname{H}}_{n-*}(\Delta^{n}_{m}S^{1})
. For
m\geq n
we have
\Delta^{n}_{m}S^{1}\cong S^{n}
, and we are done. For
0<m<n
,
\Delta^{n}_{m}S^{1}
is the one-point compactification of an arrangement of subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The maximal elements of this arrangement have dimension
m
, and for any subspace
H\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
that belongs to the arrangement, the rank of
H
in the intersection poset is
m-\dim(H)
.

It follows readily from the Goresky-Macpherson formula, or better still from Ziegler-Živaliević’s version of the formula
[
38
, Theorem 2.2]
, that
\Delta^{n}_{m}
is equivalent to a wedge copies of
S^{m}
. Furthermore, the number of copies is non-zero whenever the arrangment is non-empty, i.e. whenever
0<m
. The case
m=0
is trivial.
∎

14.
Stable cohomology of
\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)

One of the motivations for studying
\ext
in the category of functors
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
comes from its application to the cohomology of groups of automorphisms of free groups with coefficients in a polynomial functor. The application is made possible thanks to remarkable work of Djament
[
12
]
, which itself builds on a famous theorem of Galatius
[
16
]
. In this section we show how our methods can be combined with Djament’s results. But first let us review the setup that we will work in.

Let
\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle
be the free group on
n
generators. Suppose
G
is a functor
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
. Then for each
n
,
G(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)
is a representation of
\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)
. Moreover, there are natural homomorphisms

\cdots\to\operatorname{H}^{*}(\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle);G(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle))\to\operatorname{H}^{*}(\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n-1\rangle),G(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n-1\rangle))\to\cdots

Definition 14.1
.

Define the stable cohomology of automorphisms of free groups with coefficients in
G
to be the inverse limit of the sequence above

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};G):=\lim_{\infty\leftarrow n}\operatorname{H}^{*}(\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle);G(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)).

The following stability result shows that the stable cohomology agrees with actual cohomology in a range.

Theorem 14.2

(Randal-Williams – Wahl
[
32
]
)
.

Suppose
G
is a polynomial functor of degree
d
. The homomorphism

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};G)\to\operatorname{H}^{*}(\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle);G(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle))

is
\frac{n-3}{2}-d
-connected.

The following theorem of Djament shows that one can calculate
\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};G)
in terms of mapping spaces in the
\infty
-category of polynomial functors
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
.

Theorem 14.3

(
[
12
]
, Theorem 3.3)
.

Let
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
be a polynomial functor. There is an isomorphism

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut},G)\cong\mathbb{H}^{*}\left(\Sigma_{\infty};\bigoplus_{d\geq 0}\Sigma^{d}\mathbb{H}^{*}\left(\Sigma_{d};\operatorname{R}\hom(\mathrm{T}^{d}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)\otimes\mathbb{Z}[-1]\right)\right).

Here

(1)

\mathbb{H}^{*}
denotes hypercohomology.

(2)

\operatorname{R}\hom(\mathrm{T}^{d}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
is the derived
\hom
in the category of functors, equipped with the action
\Sigma_{d}
induced by the action on
\mathrm{T}^{d}
. To be really explicit,
\operatorname{R}\hom(\mathrm{T}^{d}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)
can be constructed as the cochain complex obtained by taking
\hom
from a projective resolution of
\mathrm{T}^{d}\circ\mathrm{ab}
to
G
.

(3)

\mathbb{Z}[-1]
is the sign representation of
\Sigma_{d}
.

(4)

\Sigma_{\infty}
acts trivially on the cochain complex
\bigoplus_{d\geq 0}\Sigma^{d}\mathbb{H}^{*}\cdots
.

Remark 14.4
.

The case when
G
is the constant functor is Galatius’s theorem
[
16
]
, which says that

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut},\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*}(\Sigma_{\infty};\mathbb{Z})
. The proof of Theorem
14.3
in
[
12
]
uses Galatius’s result.

We now can reinterpret Djament’s result in terms of our construction.

Theorem 14.5
.

Let
G\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to{\textbf{Ab}}
be a polynomial functor of degree
n
. Let
\widehat{G}\colon{\textbf{S}}_{*}\to{\textbf{Ch}}
be, as usual, the extension of
G
given by Theorem
C
. There is an isomorphism

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};G)\cong\pi_{-*}\pointedmaps\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}_{+},\prod_{d=0}^{n}\ce_{d}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})^{h\Sigma_{d}}\right).

Proof.

Switching from cohomological to homological grading, Theorem
14.3
says that
\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};G)

is isomorphic to

\pi_{-*}\pointedmaps\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}_{+},\bigoplus_{d\geq 0}\Sigma^{-d}\left(\spectralNat_{\poly({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}(\mathrm{T}^{d}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)\otimes\mathbb{Z}[-1]\right)^{h\Sigma_{d}}\right)

where
\spectralNat_{\poly({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}(-,-)
denotes the mapping object (that may be viewed as a chain complex, or a spectrum) in the
\infty
-catgory of polynomial functors from
{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}
to
Ch
.

By the equivalence of categories
\poly({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})\simeq\exc({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}})
, we have an equivalence

\spectralNat_{\poly({\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}},{\textbf{Ch}})}(\mathrm{T}^{d}\circ\mathrm{ab},G)\simeq\spectralNat_{\exc({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{d}\circ\mathrm{ab}},\widehat{G}).

By Lemma
9.1

\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{d}\circ\mathrm{ab}}
is the functor
X\mapsto\Omega^{d}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge d}]
, where
\Sigma_{d}
acts by permuting both the
X
coordinates and the loop coordinates (see Remark
9.2
). This is equivalent to saying that the action of
\Sigma_{d}
on
\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge d}]
is desuspended by
d
and twisted by the sign. It follows that there is an equivalence

\Sigma^{-d}\left(\spectralNat_{\exc({\textbf{S}}_{*},{\textbf{Ch}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{d}\circ\mathrm{ab}},\widehat{G})\otimes\mathbb{Z}[-1]\right)^{h\Sigma_{d}}\simeq\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge d}],\widehat{G}(X))

where
\Sigma_{d}
acts trivially on the suspension coordinate
\Sigma^{d}
on the right hand side. It follows that
\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};G)
is isomorphic to

\pi_{-*}\pointedmaps\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}_{+},\bigoplus_{d\geq 0}\left(\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge d]}],\widehat{G})\right)^{h\Sigma_{d}}\right)

By Lemma
11.3

\spectralNat_{X}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge d]}],\widehat{G})\simeq\ce_{d}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})
. Since we assume that
G
is polynomial of degree
n
, it follows that
\widehat{G}
is
n
-excisive, and thus
\ce_{d}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\simeq*
for
d>n
. We finally conclude that

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};G)\cong\pi_{-*}\pointedmaps\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}_{+},\prod_{d=0}^{n}\ce_{d}\widehat{G}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})^{h\Sigma_{d}}\right).

∎

Let
R
be a commutative ring with
1
. Define
\mathrm{T}^{n}_{R}\colon{\textbf{Fr}}^{\mathrm{fg}}\to R-{\textbf{mod}}
to be the functor

\mathrm{T}^{n}_{R}(G)=R\otimes\mathrm{T}^{n}(\mathrm{ab}(G)).

Similarly define

\Lambda^{n}_{R}(G)=R\otimes\Lambda^{n}(\mathrm{ab}(G))

and also similarly define functors
\Gamma^{n}_{R}
,
\mathrm{S}^{n}_{R}
, etc. The following proposition describes the stable cohomology of
\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)
with coefficients in some of these functors in terms of cohomology of symmetric groups with coefficients in
R
. Let us note that while integral cohomology of symmetric groups may be known “in principle”, cohomology with mod
p
coefficients is known explicitly, and can be read for example from
[
10
]
.

Proposition 14.6
.

Let
n>0
. There are isomorphisms

(45)

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\mathrm{T}^{n}_{R})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*-n}\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}};R\right)^{\Bell(n)}.

Here
\Bell(n)
is the total number of partitions of a set with
n
-elements.

(46)

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\Lambda^{n}_{R})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{H}^{*-n}\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}\times\left(\coprod_{d=1}^{n}\Comp(n,d)\times_{\Sigma_{d}}E\Sigma_{d}\right);R\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{d=1}^{n}\bigoplus_{\Lambda\in\Part(n,d)}\operatorname{H}^{*-n}(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}\times\operatorname{B}\operatorname{aut}(\Lambda);R).

Here
\Lambda
ranges over a set of representatives of orbits of the action of
\Sigma_{d}
on
\Comp(n,d)
and
\operatorname{aut}(\Lambda)
denotes the stabilizer group of
\Lambda
.

(47)

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\Gamma^{n}_{R})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*-n}\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}\times\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{n};R[-1]\right).

Here
R[-1]
denotes
R
on which
\Sigma_{\infty}
acts trivially, and
\Sigma_{n}
acts by sign.

Proof.

(
45
) By Lemma
9.1
we have
\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}\circ\mathrm{ab}}(X)=\Omega^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}[X^{\wedge n}]
. It is clear that by the same argument one can prove that
\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}_{R}}(X)=\Omega^{n}\widetilde{R}[X^{\wedge n}]
. It follows that
\ce_{d}\widehat{\mathrm{T}^{n}_{R}}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\cong\Omega^{n}R[\sur(n,d)]
. Note that the action of
\Sigma_{d}
on
\sur(n,d)
is free. It follows that
\Omega^{n}R[\sur(n,d)]^{h\Sigma_{d}}\simeq\Omega^{n}R[\sur(n,d)_{\Sigma_{d}}]
, and thus

\prod_{d=0}^{n}\Omega^{n}R[\sur(n,d)]^{h\Sigma_{d}}\simeq\Omega^{n}R^{\Bell(n)}.

By Theorem
14.5
it follows that

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut},\mathrm{T}^{n}_{R})\cong\pi_{-*}\pointedmaps\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}_{+},\Omega^{n}R^{\Bell(n)}\right)\cong\operatorname{H}^{*-n}(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty};R)^{\Bell(n)}.

(
46
) By tensoring with
R
the result of Proposition
9.4
(
1
) we have
\widehat{\Lambda^{n}_{R}}(X)\simeq\Sigma^{-n}\widetilde{R}[X^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]
. It follows that
\ce_{d}\widehat{\Lambda^{n}_{R}}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})\simeq\Sigma^{-n}R^{\Comp(n,d)}
. Note that
\Comp(n,0)=\emptyset
. And now by Theorem
14.5
it follows that

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut},\Lambda^{n}_{R})\cong\pi_{-*}\pointedmaps\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}_{+},\Sigma^{-n}\prod_{d=1}^{n}(R^{C(n,d)})^{h\Sigma_{d}}\right)

By some elementary manipulations, the right hand side is isomorphic to

\pi_{-*+n}\pointedmaps\left({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}_{+}\wedge\bigvee_{d=1}^{n}(\Comp(n,d)_{+})_{h\Sigma_{d}},R\right).

This in turn is isomorphic to

\operatorname{H}^{*-n}\left(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}\times\left(\coprod_{d}\Comp(n,d)\times_{\Sigma_{d}}E\Sigma_{d}\right);R\right).

This proves the first statement of (
46
). The second statement is just a reformulation of the first.

(
47
) Once again, by tensoring with
R
the result of Proposition
9.4
(
2
) we find that
\widehat{\Gamma^{n}_{R}}(X)=\Sigma^{-2n}\widetilde{R}[(SX)^{\wedge n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]
. Therefore
\ce_{d}\widehat{\Gamma^{n}_{R}}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})=\Sigma^{-2n}R[S^{n}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}\sur(n,d)_{+}]
. We saw already in the proof of Corollary
11.4
(
3
) that
S^{n}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}\sur(n,d)_{+}\simeq*
unless
n=d
. when
n=d
we have
S^{n}\wedge_{\Sigma_{n}}\sur(n,d)_{+}\simeq S^{d}
, on which
\Sigma_{d}
acts by permutation. It follows that

\prod_{d=0}^{n}\ce_{d}\widehat{\Gamma^{n}_{R}}(S^{0},\ldots,S^{0})^{h\Sigma_{d}}\simeq\Sigma^{-2n}\left(\widetilde{R}[S^{n}]\right)^{h\Sigma_{n}}\simeq\Sigma^{-n}R[-1]^{h\Sigma_{n}}.

Applying Theorem
14.5
one more time, we obtain the isomorphism

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\Gamma^{n}_{R})\cong\pi_{-*}\pointedmaps({\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}}_{+},\Sigma^{-n}R[-1]^{h\Sigma_{n}}).

And it is easy to see that the right hand side is isomorphic to
\operatorname{H}^{*-n}(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}\times\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{n};R[-1]).

∎

When
R=\mathbb{Q}
the formulas of Proposition
14.6
simplify, unsurprisingly. The following corollary is originally due to Oscar Randal-Williams
[
33
, Theorem A (ii) and Corollary D]
. See also
[
36
, Theorem 4]
, with the difference that we state the result in terms of stable cohomology rather than homology.

Corollary 14.7
.

We have the following isomorphisms of graded groups

(1)

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\mathrm{T}^{n}_{\mathbb{Q}})\cong\Sigma^{n}\mathbb{Q}^{\Bell(n)}.

(2)

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\Lambda^{n}_{\mathbb{Q}})\cong\Sigma^{n}\mathbb{Q}^{\Part(n)}

where
\Part(n)
denotes the number of all partitions of the number
n
.

(3)

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb{Q}})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\mathrm{S}^{n}_{\mathbb{Q}})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\Sigma\mathbb{Q}&n=1\\
0&n>1\end{array}\right..

Proof.

The first two statements of the corollary follow from the corresponding statements of Proposition
14.6
because rational cohomology of
\Sigma_{n}
, and also of
\Sigma_{\infty}
is the same as of a point.

For the third statement of the corollary, first of all recall that there is a rational isomorphism of functors
\mathrm{S}^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}}\Gamma^{n}
. So it is enough to prove the claim for the functor
\Gamma^{n}_{Q}
. The third statement of the corollary follows from the third statement of the proposition because for
n>1
the rational cohomology of
\Sigma_{n}
with coeffitients in the sign representation is trivial in all dimensions.
∎

Examples 14.8
.

Let us use Proposition
14.6
to calculate the stable cohomology of
\operatorname{aut}(\operatorname{F}\,\langle n\rangle)
with coefficients in
\mathrm{T}^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab}
,
\Lambda^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab}
, and
\Gamma^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab}
. It is easy to see that

(1)

\Bell(3)=5
,

(2)

\Comp(3,1)
consists of a single element
(3)
, whose group of automorphisms is trivial.

(3)

\Comp(3,2)
consists of two elements
(1,2)
and
(2,1)
, which lie in the same orbit of the action of
\Sigma_{2}
on
\Comp(3,2)
and have trivial stabilizers.

(4)

\Comp(3,3)
consists of a single element
(1,1,1)
, whose stabilizer is
\Sigma_{3}

We conclude that there are isomorphisms

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\mathrm{T}^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*-3}(B\Sigma_{\infty})^{5}

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\Lambda^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*-3}(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}\sqcup\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}\sqcup\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}\times\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3}).

\operatorname{H}^{*}_{s}(\operatorname{aut};\Gamma^{3}\circ\mathrm{ab})\cong\operatorname{H}^{*-3}(\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{\infty}\times\operatorname{B}\Sigma_{3};\mathbb{Z}[-1]).
